Zbigniew Chodyła

# Outline of the Earliest History of the Olęder Settlements in the Pyzdry Forest, 1746 – 1793

Pyzdry 2015

[p. 5]

# The Near Unknown

The Pyzdry Forest (Puszcza Pyzdrska) is a large forest complex that owes its name to the fact that nearly one sixth of its area belonged to the town of Pyzdry from 1387 to 1793. The natural boundaries of this geographical region are formed on the west, north and east by the rivers Prosna, Warta and Powa. The southern boundary of the Pyzdry Forest is set by the extent of the continuous woods stretching north of Brudzew, Stawiszyn and Ceków Kolonia. Centuries of agricultural colonisation, which consisted in cutting down the forest, reduced its area considerably. A continuous forest complex of 16,500 ha has nevertheless survived to our day, together with three other complexes close by of 1,100 to 1,400 ha. In their neighbourhood there are, besides, more than 300 smaller woods.

We owe the breaking up of the forest chiefly to the Olęder settlers, who in the eighteenth, but also in the nineteenth century cleared about 50% of its area, creating in the years 1746-1793 alone, that is in less than half a century, 58 settlements with their own churches, schools and cemeteries. These were not colonists settled in the time of the Prussian partition by the Prussian occupying authorities. The Olęder settlers came at the invitation of Polish landowners at the end of the First Commonwealth, migrating to Poland for their bread. Settlers of the Protestant confessions (chiefly the Lutheran), of German and sometimes of Polish nationality, made up the greater part of this movement, but Catholics and Poles took part in it too, in more than a third. Besides settlements whose people were of one confession and one nation there were also settlements mixed in both respects.

After the Second World War, when the descendants of the Protestant German Olęders were expelled, many of these villages were emptied. On land that until recently had been worked intensively, cleared and drained for farming, natural succession set in, and the abandoned meadows turned into marsh. Locally the forest began to be called "the Bieszczady of Greater Poland", a name that sums up the character of the region.

To the north the Pyzdry Forest borders on an area farmed just as extensively, the valley of the Middle Warta, protected within the Nadwarciański Landscape Park, which includes two areas of the NATURA 2000 network. Together the two regions form an area with a clear

[p. 6]

geographical, natural and cultural unity that sets it apart from the more urbanised areas around it. Its geographical and cultural unity is made by clear boundaries shaped by nature and by the Olęder landscape, a mosaic of woods, fields and meadows with lone farmsteads scattered among them and set into the landscape. Its natural unity comes from the large share of woodland and the great quantity of extensively used fields and meadows. There are many marshy wilds and high dunes within the woods, but also corners of villages full of colour, warmth and quiet. In the frequent floods the Warta valley turns into a vast expanse of water, a refuge of wetland birds of European importance. The cultural unity that sets the whole area apart from its neighbours is made above all by the rich heritage of Olęder architecture. Many inhabited traditional farmsteads, found elsewhere only in open-air museums, have survived to our day. A particular feature are the "houses of iron", that is, houses built of bog iron ore. The forest has the largest number in Poland of buildings built of this interesting material. Architectural surveys made in 2009 made it possible to pick out the common features of the building here. This is the basis for gradually defining a style of architecture of its own, which will lastingly and favourably distinguish this region in Poland and in the world. The isolation of the forest from the surrounding areas favoured the survival of old trades and ways of farming. The old crafts are represented by the potter's workshop in Czarny Bród, the only one in Greater Poland. Old ways of animal husbandry and the customs that go with them are also kept up to this day, such as the free grazing of the geese of Zagórów and the meadow communities in the Warta valley, unchanged since 1864.

The unity of the area, and at the same time its difference from the neighbouring areas, was noticed by local volunteers, gathered chiefly in the Cultural Society "Echo Pyzdr". In 2009 the long-forgotten name "Puszcza Pyzdrska" was restored. Thanks to grants won in competitions for non-governmental organisations, the first research in history on the cultural heritage of the area (architecture, design), which had until then seemed uninteresting, was begun. Its results were very fruitful and were partly presented in public, among other places in the Ethnographic Museum in Poznań, at the exhibition "Puszcza Pyzdrska. Olędrzy i ich domy z żelaza" (The Pyzdry Forest. The Olęders and their houses of iron). Workshops in old building techniques were held, teaching materials were prepared for teachers and teachers were trained in using them in lessons, and the first tourist map of the forest was made and published, with the publication "Puszcza Pyzdrska – olęderskie dziedzictwo" (The Pyzdry Forest – the Olęder heritage). In 2013 the first tourist guide to the Pyzdry Forest was prepared and published, and together with the map it received a distinction at the Tour Salon tourism fair in Poznań. A demonstration outcrop of bog iron ore at Wierzchy was opened, and the building of stops – museums was begun. In 2014 the first documentary film about the people of the Pyzdry Forest, "Ludzie z żelaznych domów" (People of the houses of iron), was made. Those who revived the name "Puszcza Pyzdrska" realised the size of what they had set in motion. The forest turned out to cover, as a reasonably compact area, 15 and in a wider

[p. 7]

sense even 17 communes in 6 districts, which until then had practically not worked together. To meet this challenge the Local Tourist Organisation "Puszcza Pyzdrska" was founded as early as 2012, a coalition of all the local forces and authorities with an interest in building the identity and the tourist potential of this large area. The Board of the Landscape Parks of the Greater Poland Voivodeship also took an active part in the revival of the Pyzdry Forest. Since 2014 the two institutions have together been carrying out the plan of an Ecomuseum of the Warta Valley and the Pyzdry Forest. Its purpose is to protect the natural, cultural and landscape values of the Nadwarciański Landscape Park and the Pyzdry Forest, treated as one region, and to make them accessible to residents and visitors. The Ecomuseum is a museum without staff, walls or display cases. It is a network of sites scattered across the land, which together form a collection showing the attractions of the region. It takes in monuments, valuable natural sites, traditional farmsteads, craft workshops and pieces of tourist infrastructure. By an Ecomuseum is meant an active, not a passive, way of saving the whole natural, cultural and landscape heritage, as three inseparable parts.

The publication "Zarys najstarszych dziejów osad olęderskich w Puszczy Pyzdrskiej (1746-1793)" by Dr Zbigniew Chodyła fits perfectly into the educational work carried on among the inhabitants of the Ecomuseum. It is the first publication on the history of the founding and the workings of the 58 Olęder villages of the Pyzdry Forest, based chiefly on archival material not known until now. This and other educational work will aim to strengthen among the people of the forest a sense of pride in living in a remarkable region, and this will make them more responsible for the state of the local heritage. The goal is an attractive tourist product centred on outstanding natural attractions (the unregulated river Warta, rare species of birds, the natural landscape, traditional farming) and cultural ones (old settlements, archaeological sites, "ghost" places, a style of architecture of its own, folklore, battlefields, cemeteries, regional shops and cooking) found within one geographically coherent area. All these attractions must be well described and marked on the ground and attractively promoted. But it is not worth waiting until the Ecomuseum is fully realised. I invite you now to immerse yourselves in the Pyzdry Forest. Following the trail of the Olęders by bicycle, we will discover a world full of secrets, until recently condemned to oblivion. We will come across abandoned fields and farmsteads, or only traces of them, such as old pear trees, chestnuts or lilac bushes. In secluded places we will come upon mysterious Protestant cemeteries. Paddling a canoe down the Warta or the Prosna we can admire the beauty of these rivers and the richness of their plants and animals.

President of the Local Tourist Organisation "Puszcza Pyzdrska"

[p. 8]

# Ladies and Gentlemen!

It is with great pleasure that we hand you a publication on the earliest history of the Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest. The forest, running through the communes of the District of Kalisz – Blizanów, Stawiszyn, Mycielin and Ceków Kolonia – is threaded with field tracks and canals, with dunes overgrown by woods, and with clearings cut long ago by the first settlers. We are sure that the beauty of this land and the hospitality of its people will stay in the memory of everyone who decides to visit us.

Caring for the heritage of our ancestors, we are also undertaking a number of measures for the development and the future of the district. The Kalisz land is a region open to new economic ventures and to cooperation with potential investors. All this, together with its many assets in culture and tourism, means that our district is ready for further development in many areas of life.

We warmly invite everyone to read this book and to visit the District of Kalisz. We hope that after a first visit you will want to come back to us many times.

Krzysztof Nosal, Starost of the District of Kalisz

[p. 9]

# Introduction

One of the most important types of rural rent-paying settlement in the Polish lands in the years 1547-1864 was Olęder settlement, which took its name from its pioneers, the Dutch, called in Old Polish *olędrzy*, *olendrzy* or *holędrzy*. What decided its nature, however, was not the settlers' ethnic origin, the kind of land (wet or dry) or the way it was taken in (by draining or by clearing), but the Dutch or Olęder law, brought by the Dutch and applied by those who came after them, chiefly Germans and Poles, together with the economic function carried out under it: for the most part, taking in land that was hard to bring into use, farming the land in one piece, and a large place for stock-raising. Under this law the Olęders, *holędrzy*, so called even when they were no longer of Dutch nationality, were personally free and held their land by emphyteusis: at first a lease for many years, a long term, and later (especially in Greater Poland) a perpetual lease, a perpetual rent tenure for what was called entry money (*okupne*), with the right to pass the farm on to their heirs or to sell it after informing the owner of the land. When the free years allowed for setting up the farm had run out, they owed the feudal lord only rents (in money and in kind) and, to a very limited extent (though not always), labour. They had a limited freedom of the land, answered jointly, as a whole commune, for meeting their obligations to the lord on time, had an elected commune self-government, which among other things saw to it that these obligations were met, and kept a limited judicial freedom, being subject to their own village court and only partly (in criminal cases and on appeal) to the lord's jurisdiction. They paid, besides, dues to the Catholic Church and taxes to the state, as a rule had freedom of trade, and enjoyed freedom of religion and the right to found their own schools, which were most often also houses of prayer. Of these principles it was above all the collective solidarity of the Olęder commune towards the lord, which guaranteed him a sure and steady income (especially from the money rents), the equality of rights and the mutual help of neighbours within the commune that followed from it, and the particular organisation and functions of the village (commune) self-government that

[p. 10]

made up the legal distinctness and the greater attraction of Olęder settlement compared with other forms of colonisation under German law, such as settlement under a sołtys (village mayor), in which the settlers received their holdings (farms) for perpetual use, but not, like the Olęders, on the strength of foundation contracts made with the whole commune: by individual charters[^1].

According to research so far, at least about 1,700 Olęder settlements in all were founded in the years 1547-1864 in the lands of the First Commonwealth and, after 1795, in the lands of its three partitions, at least 350 of them with an exclusive or partial share of ethnic Dutch of the Mennonite confession and their descendants, some of them by then turned Lutheran. At least about 925 of these settlements arose in Greater Poland proper, which in the Old Polish period consisted of two voivodeships, of Poznań and of Kalisz, and from 1768, after a third voivodeship, that of Gniezno, was formed from part of the second, of three such administrative units[^2]. In the years 1597-1793 alone, that is from the year the oldest settlement was founded[^3] until the whole of Greater Poland proper had been brought within the borders of Prussia in the Second Partition of Poland, 758 Olęder settlements in the wider sense were created in 8 of its 10 districts (Poznań, Kościan, Kalisz, Konin, Nakło, Pyzdry, Gniezno and Kcynia), at least 711 of them (with 8 ephemeral ones) certain, 22 of a transitional type and 25 whose legal and constitutional character is unclearly documented in the sources[^4]. These settlements, also called for short (as were their inhabitants, the farmers and their families) *olędry*, were founded

[^3]: Z. Chodyła, *Ługi Ujskie – najstarsza osada olęderska w Wielkopolsce (1597–1772)* [Ługi Ujskie – the oldest Olęder settlement in Greater Poland], "Rocznik Nadnotecki". Vol. XXX, 1999, pp. 25–58.

[^4]: The certain settlements had a sure legal and constitutional character, confirmed in the sources, and above all in their foundation charters, issued to whole communes. The transitional settlements, whose legal constitution is not quite certain, could develop towards settlements that were certainly Olęder, or towards sołtys or emphyteutic ones. The settlements whose legal status is unclearly stated in the sources had an elected self-government or a sołtys appointed by the lord, but did not always keep complete equality and solidarity towards the lord, and, besides, because of the sometimes small number of hearths – farms (2-3) – and of inhabitants, they raise the doubt whether an Olęder self-government could have been organised and could have worked in them. Since, however, 1) before and in the years 1789-1793, and later, they were very consistently described as *olędry* and showed other secondary features of Olęder settlements, 2) they were probably for the most part tied administratively and judicially to larger Olęder settlements or formed communes of two or more villages, and 3) by the end of the eighteenth century and the first quarter of the nineteenth they had grown, and their legal status had on the whole probably settled, and most often they had gained a separate administrative and judicial organisation, we count them as such.

[p. 11]

under various types of feudal landownership: royal, town, church (diocesan and monastic), and above all under the noble, which far outweighed the others. In the Greater Poland region they were inhabited by people who had come in: Dutch of the Mennonite confession and their descendants (in about 0.8%); in the great majority (about 54%) by Protestants who had come in, chiefly Lutheran, with relatively few Reformed, chiefly ethnic Germans, though in part also Poles); and in a large percentage (about 38%) by Catholic, Polish people, and in part also by people mixed in confession and ethnicity, that is Protestant-Catholic and German-Polish (in about 7.2%)[^5].

An area of Greater Poland in which Olęder settlement also developed fairly vigorously in the eighteenth century was the Pyzdry (or Pyzderska) Forest, from the early Middle Ages to the present day one of the still fairly large forest complexes, which has kept the features of a natural landscape to a considerable degree. It lay and lies, broadly, on the west, east and north between stretches of the river Warta and its tributaries, the Prosna and the Powa, and on the south it reaches a line running roughly through the places Brudzewek, Brudzew, Lipe, Jarantów, Stawiszyn, Ceków Kolonia, Zbiersk, Mycielin, Stropieszyn, Kosmów, Ceków Kolonia, Przespolew Kościelny, Poroże, Smaszew, Gadów, Nowy Świat, Lisiec Nowy and Kowalewek, which partly follows the course of small streams flowing into the Prosna, the Czarna Struga and the Powa, tributaries of the Warta.

This forest area, a part of which has now been set apart as the Nadwarciański Landscape Park (which takes in a part of the Pyzdry Forest in its southern part, but also land north of the Warta), measured, as it stood in the middle of the eighteenth century, about 44,000 ha, that is 440 km². Of this, more than 7,000 ha belonged to the town of Pyzdry. In the Pyzdry Forest the watersheds, the sandy soils and the dunes were the most heavily wooded. Pine predominated among the trees, with some birch, and on wet ground oak and alder. Along the western edge of the forest, parallel to the Prosna, ran the road from Kalisz through Pyzdry to Poznań and Gniezno, and its eastern edge was served by the route joining Kalisz and Kruszwica through Konin. The middle of the forest was crossed by the Stawiszyn-Lądek road along the Czarna Struga, from which above all the economic penetration was made, by settlement going deep into the Pyzdry Forest and gradually reducing its area. This settlement went on in several phases: in the Middle Ages (twelfth/thirteenth to fifteenth century), in early modern times (1501-1793), in modern times (1794/1796-1918), in recent times (to 1945) and in the present, up to today, and within them in several periods. Its growth or its halting were tied to the booms and slumps of social and economic life, deepened by political events, by wars (above all the Swedish "Deluge" (1655-1659/60), the Great Northern War (1700-1721), and the First and Second World Wars) and by natural disasters, among them above all epidemics. The shrinking of the network of settlements was also furthered by the extensive system of farming then in general use, the communal three-field system, which exhausted the soil and produced what are called settlement deserts, that is, the disappearance of settlements as they

[^5]: Cf. Z. Chodyła, the works cited in n. 1.

[p. 12]

were depopulated and overgrown by forest. The decline in the number of settlements was overcome gradually, on a wider scale, only from the 1720s, by the founding of rent-paying settlements, which from the second half of the 1740s were founded above all under Olęder law. As a result the number of places grew, in absolute and in relative terms, through the founding of entirely new villages and hamlets on what was called raw root (*surowy korzeń*, untouched land) and through the rebuilding of old ones, but on other legal terms and mostly with new inhabitants.

In following the development of the earliest settlement, Olęder settlement included, in the Pyzdry Forest on the basis of Old Polish sources, one has to remember that, in the old administrative divisions, its area lay within a single voivodeship, that of Kalisz, but within three of its districts, namely those of Pyzdry, Konin and Kalisz. Today the area of the Pyzdry Forest lies within a single voivodeship, that of Greater Poland, six districts (Kalisz, Konin, Pleszew, Słupca, Turek and Września) and fifteen communes: Blizanów, Ceków-Kolonia, Chocz, Gizałki, Grodziec, Lądek, Malanów, Mycielin, Pyzdry, Rychwał, Rzgów, Stare Miasto, Stawiszyn, Tuliszków and Zagórów[^6].

Among the rural settlements that arose in this forest complex in early modern times, and especially in the second half of the eighteenth century, the Olęder settlements, 58 in number, decidedly predominated, and this study is devoted to their earliest history. These settlements arose in the years 1746-1793, and today 67 villages or parts of villages correspond to them, mostly under changed names. They are listed below in Tab. 1 in alphabetical order, by their position in the three Old Polish districts and in particular parishes (which lay within various archdeaconries and deaneries of the archdioceses of Gniezno and Poznań[^7]), with their present names and the districts and communes they belong to.

As Tab. 1 shows, of the total of 58 Olęder settlements that arose in 3 Old Polish districts of the voivodeship of Kalisz, in 21 parishes (4 of which lay on the border of two districts), 5 were founded in the district of Pyzdry in 4 parishes, 28 in the district of Konin in 8 parishes, and 25 in the district of Kalisz in 10 parishes. Today these villages, as 67 rural settlements or parts of them, lie in the voivodeship of Greater Poland, in 6 districts and 15 communes, namely in the districts of Kalisz (Blizanów – 3, Ceków Kolonia – 9, Mycielin – 5, Stawiszyn – 1), Konin (Grodziec – 8, Rychwał – 4, Rzgów – 1, Stare Miasto – 1), Pleszew (Chocz – 4, Gizałki – 7), Słupca (Lądek – 1, Zagórów – 8), Turek (Malanów – 2, Tuliszków – 2) and Września (Pyzdry – 13)[^8].

Below we present in outline only the beginnings of the history of these Olęder villages, from the founding of the oldest in 1746 and of the later ones up to 1793, when, as a result of the Second Partition of Poland, they came, like the whole of Greater Poland, under Prussian rule, in the province called South Prussia, in which Olęder settlement went on, in the Pyzdry Forest too, but in other political and partly also other economic conditions[^9]. Because of the limited size of this study we deal in turn, as briefly as possible, with the following questions: 1) the distribution, chronology, causes, conditions and organisation of the founding, and the naming, of the Olęder settlements that arose in the years 1746-1793 in the Pyzdry

[^8]: See Z. Chodyła, *Osadnictwo olęderskie w Wielkopolsce (1597–1793)* [Olęder settlement in Greater Poland], Annexes I/3, 4 and 5.

[^9]: The Olęder settlements that arose after 1793 and before 1864 are recorded by the maps discussed below: the Prussian maps of South Prussia of 1796-1795 and 1801-1803 (the so-called large and small Gilly) and the Polish maps of the Kingdom of Poland, made by the Quartermaster General's staff of its army before 1830, and of 1859, by General W. Chrzanowski.

[p. 16]

Forest, 2) their area, layout, size and types by the number of hearths (farms) and by their social and functional-economic organisation, 3) the population of these settlements, its numbers and its demographic, social and occupational, confessional and ethnic structure, and the origin of the inhabitants, 4) the legal status and the administrative and judicial organisation of the settlements, 5) their economic and social life, and 6) the material and spiritual culture and the daily life of the inhabitants of these settlements. We present them, however, with unequal precision, according to how far the sources survive and what they contain.

The literature on the Olęder settlements that concern us is modest. Most of the Olęder settlements founded in the districts of Pyzdry and Kalisz are recorded by K. J. Hładyłowicz in his study of the changes of the landscape and the development of settlement in Greater Poland from the fourteenth to the nineteenth century, who, however, leaves out the district of Konin on principle[^10]. Some of the settlements of this kind founded in the district of Konin in the eighteenth century on noble estates are described by B. Szczepański in his article[^11]. The results of Szczepański's research on Olęder settlement on noble estates, and of J. Wąsicki's on state settlement in the period of South Prussia (1793-1806)[^12], were put on a map, with a commentary, by the geographer Stanisława Zajchowska[^13]. The Olęder settlements in the southern part of the Pyzdry Forest, within the district of Kalisz, were taken into account by W. Rusiński in his article on the settlement structure and the population of that district[^14]. The development of settlement in the Pyzdry Forest, Olęder settlement included, was set in the wider context of its natural environment by I. Tłoczek in his contribution (though concentrating chiefly on the Pyzdrskie Ol., which belonged to the town of Pyzdry)[^15]. The history of settlement in the Pyzdry Forest from the Middle Ages to 1945 (Olęder settlement included), though above all in the eighteenth century, was shown very briefly by Z. Chodyła in one of the sketches in a popular, brochure-style publication entitled "Puszcza Pyzdrska. Olęderskie dziedzictwo" (The Pyzdry Forest. The Olęder heritage).

[^10]: K. J. Hładyłowicz, *Zmiany krajobrazu i rozwój osadnictwa w Wielkopolsce od XIV do XIX wieku* [Changes of the landscape and the development of settlement in Greater Poland from the fourteenth to the nineteenth century], Lwów 1932, pp. 151-153 and 227-228.

[^11]: B. Szczepański, *Osadnictwo olęderskie w dobrach szlacheckich powiatu konińskiego w XVIII wieku* [Olęder settlement on the noble estates of the district of Konin in the eighteenth century], "Rocznik Wielkopolski Wschodniej", vol. 1, Poznań 1973, pp. 43–61. Some attention is given to the group of settlements on the royal estates of the district of Konin, Olęder ones included (but only in the starosty of Koło, leaving out the Lisieckie Ol. in the starosty of Konin), by J. Borowiak, *Stan posiadania gospodarstw chłopskich w krółewszczyznach powiatu konińskiego na schyłku XVIII w.* [The holdings of peasant farms on the crown estates of the district of Konin at the end of the eighteenth century], "Roczniki Dziejów Społecznych i Gospodarczych", vol. XVIII, 1956, Poznań 1957, pp. 239–285 (Olęders pp. 242-243, 259-261).

[^12]: J. Wąsicki, *Kolonizacja niemiecka w okresie Prus Południowych 1793 – 1806* [German colonisation in the period of South Prussia], "Przegląd Zachodni", vol. IX, 1953, no. 9/10, pp. 137-197.

[^13]: S. Zajchowska S., *Osadnictwo* "*olęderskie" i fryderycjańskie w Wielkopolsce. Mapa pomocnicza 14* [Olęder and Frederician settlement in Greater Poland. Auxiliary map 14], [in:] *Atlas języka i kultury ludowej Wielkopolski* [Atlas of the language and folk culture of Greater Poland]. Prepared by a team ed. Z. Sobierajski and J. Burszta, vol. V, pt. 2, Wrocław 1989, pp. 17–22.

[^14]: W. Rusiński, *Struktura osadnictwa i zaludnienie powiatu kaliskiego w 1789 r*. [The settlement structure and the population of the district of Kalisz in 1789], "Rocznik Kaliski", vol. 3, Poznań 1970, pp. 53–116.

[^15]: I. F. Tłoczek, *Osadnictwo rolnicze w dolinach rzek nizinnych* [Agricultural settlement in the valleys of lowland rivers], "Zeszyty Naukowe Szkoły Głównej Gospodarstwa Wiejskiego – Akademii Rolniczej w Warszawie". Seria historyczna, no. 15, Warsaw 1978, pp. 9-18 (where the author, judging from Fig. 1 (a small map): Dutch settlement of the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries in the woods of the town of Pyzdry, extends the area of this forest complex, and correspondingly of the settlement mentioned, southwards and to the west of Konin).

[p. 17]

This publication, very richly illustrated, presents in other sketches by W. Kowalska the present state of the natural environment in the Pyzdry Forest, the kinds of building and other elements of the cultural landscape of the (former) Olęder villages of the area and the state in which they survive (Protestant churches and cemeteries), archaeological monuments, farming, orchards and gardens, craftsmen and folk artists, cooking traditions, markets, fairs and church feasts, place names, local activists and leaders, and hints useful for spreading knowledge of local history and keeping its memory alive among the present inhabitants of the region[^16]. Some information on certain of the Olęder settlements of the Pyzdry Forest at the end of the eighteenth century, and on their later fortunes in the nineteenth, was given by K. P. Woźniak in his book on the development of German settlement between the Prosna and the Pilica and the Vistula from the 1770s to 1866[^17]. He did so chiefly from the German literature, which deals with details and rests on manuscript sources to a limited degree; the most important of it are the studies of W. Maas, and above all of A. Breyer, which we have also used here[^18].

For the questions of the material and spiritual culture and the daily life of the inhabitants of the Olęder settlements, the ethnographic studies and the synthesis of the history of material culture cited below proved helpful.

The information on the oldest settlements founded in the Pyzdry Forest could be enriched to some extent from source editions: the lists of town and rural settlements, with their location by the church's administrative divisions and their owners, in the years 1783-1784, prepared by the Rev. Franciszek Czaykowski, canon of the chapter of Łowicz[^19]; the inventories and inspections of noble estates in the district of Kalisz[^20] and of Konin among others[^21] from the second half of the

[^16]: Z. Chodyła, W. Kowalska, *Puszcza Pyzdrska. Olęderskie dziedzictwo* [The Pyzdry Forest. The Olęder heritage], Pyzdry 2010 (1st ed.), pp. 7-11 (the text *Osadnictwo w Puszczy Pyzderskiej od średniowiecza do 1945 r*. [Settlement in the Pyzdry Forest from the Middle Ages to 1945], by the author of this study).

[^17]: K. P. Woźniak, *Niemieckie osadnictwo wiejskie między Prosną a Pilicą i Wisłą od lat 70. XVIII wieku do 1866 roku. Proces i jego interpretacje* [German rural settlement between the Prosna, the Pilica and the Vistula from the 1770s to 1866. The process and its interpretations], Łódź 2013 (pages by the index of places).

[^18]: W. Maas, *Mittelpolnische Hauländereien*, "Deutsche Wissenschaftliche Zeitschrift für Polen" (...), Posen (cited below as DWZP) 36 (1939), pp. 39–62; A. Breyer, *Deutsche Bauernkultur in Mittelpolen vor 150 Jahren*, "Deutsche Monatshefte in Polen", Poznań (below: DMhP), Jg. 5 (15) Mai/Juni 1939, H. 11/12, pp. 475–486 (cited below as Breyer 1); the same, *Deutsche Gaue in Mittelpolen*, DMhP, Jg. 1 (11), April 1935, H. 10, pp. 393–434 (cited below as Breyer 2); the same, *Die deutschen ländlichen Siedlungen im mittelpolnischen Warthebruches*, DWZP, 1938, H. 34, pp. 22–70 (cited below as Breyer 3); the same, *Ostdeutschland als Mutterland der deutschen Siedlungen in Mittelpolen*, DMhP, Jg. 2 (12), Juli/August 1935, H. 1/2, pp. 48–57 (cited below as Breyer 4).

[^19]: *"Regestr diecezjów" Franciszka Czaykowskiego, czyli właściciele ziemscy w Koronie w 1783–1784* [Franciszek Czaykowski's "Register of the dioceses", or the landowners in the Crown in 1783-1784]. Prepared for print by S. Górzyński. Introduction and notes by K. Chłapowski, S. Górzyński, Warsaw 2009 [2nd ed.].

[^20]: *Inwentarze dóbr szlacheckich powiatu kaliskiego* [Inventories of the noble estates of the district of Kalisz], Vol. II: 1751–1775, ed. W. Rusiński, Wrocław 1959; *Inwentarze dóbr szlacheckich dawnego powiatu kaliskiego* [Inventories of the noble estates of the former district of Kalisz] 1776–1792. Part I: Inventories of 1776–1779; Pt. II: Inventories of 1780–1784; Pt. III: Inventories of 1785–1792. Collected and edited by W. Rusiński. "Rocznik Kaliski". Vol. XIV, pp. 99–264; vol. XV, pp. 129–274 and vol. XVI, pp. 85–241; cited below as Rus. Inw. 1 and Rus. Inw. 2 Pt. I, II, III).

[^21]: *Materiały do dziejów chłopa wielkopolskiego w drugiej połowie XVIII wieku* [Materials for the history of the Greater Poland peasant in the second half of the eighteenth century]. Selected from the municipal court books and edited by J. Deresiewicz, Vol. III: Województwo kaliskie [The voivodeship of Kalisz], Wrocław 1957. (PAN IH. Materiały do dziejów wsi polskiej. Ed. S. Arnold. Series III. Inwentarze dóbr ziemskich); cited below as Der., *Materiały do dziejów chłopa*. Vol. III.

[p. 18]

eighteenth century; and, besides, the books of the Crown Referendary Court (the central court of appeal for the peasants of villages on royal estates) of the years 1768-1794[^22].

Given this state of knowledge about the earliest history of Olęder settlement in the Pyzdry Forest, the sources of this article are, in the main, archival (most of them written in Polish, but a large part also, or only, in German, and in Latin), in the form of: 1) settlement charters (in fact contracts), which tell above all of the time, causes and conditions of the founding, the legal constitution, the social and economic constitution, the internal organisation of the settlements and the dues their inhabitants owed to the manor, the Church and the state; 2) inventories and inspections; 3) commune (sołtys) books and the village ordinance (*wilkierz*); 4) other, various records (secular as well as church ones, and among the latter the registers of the parish of Szymanowice of the second half of the eighteenth century and the visitation of the deanery of Konin of 1790-1791), cited below in the notes, which for lack of space we do not discuss further here. The most important of them survive in the series of the municipal court books (*księgi grodzkie*), especially those of Pyzdry, Konin and Kalisz[^23], but also in a valuable collection of originals and copies of such documents and records called the *Papers of Albert Breyer* (*Spuścizna Alberta Breyera*)[^24], kept in the State Archive in Poznań (cited below as APP). For questions of the same kind, but also others, concerning above all the number and structure of the farms, the population and the social and economic life of the Olęder settlements, of fundamental importance are: the surveys of the crown estates of 1789[^25], the tables of hearths, of hearth tax and of population and the tax of the offering of 10 and 20 groszy of the years 1789-1790, and the so-called grain levy (*zsypka zbożowa*) of 1791[^26]. Because of the gaps in these sources, however, full findings on particular questions are possible only for a part of these settlements.

Use was also made of the *Geograficzno–statystyczne opisanie parafiów Królestwa Polskiego w roku 17*9 [sic!] *przez K. Pertheesa geografa króla Stanisława Augusta* [Geographical and statistical description of the parishes of the Kingdom of Poland in the year 179 by K. Perthees, geographer of King Stanisław August],

[^22]: *Księgi Referendarii Koronnej z drugiej połowy XVIII wieku* [The books of the Crown Referendary Court of the second half of the eighteenth century], Vol. I (1768-1780) and II (1781-1794), ed. A. Keckowa and W. Pałucki. (Materiały do dziejów wsi polskiej, series II), Warsaw 1955 and 1957; cited below as KsRK. Vol. 1 and 2.

[^23]: These books are described by J. Bielecka, *Inwentarze archiwów ksiąg grodzkich i ziemskich* *Wielkopolski XIV-XVIII wieku. Województwo poznańskie, kaliskie, gnieźnieńskie, inowrocławskie* [Inventories of the archives of the municipal court and land court books of Greater Poland, fourteenth to eighteenth century. The voivodeships of Poznań, Kalisz, Gniezno, Inowrocław], Poznań 1965.

[^24]: The papers of A. Breyer (1889-1939), a teacher in the German schools of pre-war Poland, a researcher of the history of German settlement (rural in the sixteenth to twentieth centuries, and urban and industrial in the nineteenth and twentieth) and of the life of the German minority in central Poland (Greater Poland proper, eastern Greater Poland, Kuyavia, Masovia), the author of many scholarly and popular publications in this field, mostly on particular points, based on written sources and fairly reliable, number 515 archival units. We cite them below for short as SAB.

[^25]: The surveys of the crown estates of 1765 and 1789 are kept in the original in the Treasury Archive branch of the Central Archives of Historical Records (below AGAD) in Warsaw, and in part as copies entered (*oblata*) in the municipal court books of Kalisz, Konin and Pyzdry, which are in the APP.

[^26]: Most of the tax tables and surveys, kept as a separate collection in the APP, are listed by J. Bielecka, *Inwentarze archiwów ksiąg grodzkich i ziemskich* *Wielkopolski XIV-XVIII wieku...*, pp. 502-506. For the district of Konin they survive only in very small fragments, both in this collection and in the municipal court books and elsewhere.

[p. 19]

vols. V–VI: Archidiecezja Gnieźnieńska [The archdiocese of Gniezno][^27], and the official Prussian printed and manuscript descriptive and statistical works for the years 1775-1776, 1793 and 1795, and 1796: those of A. F. Büsching (printed) and of August Sadebeck, under the pseudonym Sirissa, kept as a draft manuscript in the APP and afterwards printed, and of the General Directory of South Prussia, written, kept in the Central Archives of Historical Records in Warsaw[^28].

For the questions of the distribution of the Olęder settlements, and partly of their layout, cartographic sources proved useful. Among the Polish ones: the "szczegulna" [detailed] map of the voivodeship of Kalisz by Karol de Perthées, court cartographer of King Stanisław August Poniatowski, of 1791 and about 1801, at a scale of 1:225,000[^29], the *Karta topograficzna Królestwa Polskiego* [Topographical map of the Kingdom of Poland] of 1822-1843 at a scale of 1:126,000, prepared by the Quartermaster General's staff of the Polish Army and published in 1834 [*recte* 1843], and the *Karta dawnej Polski...* [Map of old Poland] of 1859 at a scale of 1:300,000 by General W. Chrzanowski[^30]. Among the Prussian ones: two maps of South Prussia, made in the years 1796-1805 and 1802-1803 under the direction of Brodowski, Gilly and Langner, one at a scale of 1:57,600, called the "large" Gilly, and the other reduced to 1:150,000, called the "small" or "printed" Gilly[^31].

In describing some questions of the material culture and the daily life of the people of the Olęder settlements founded in the Pyzdry Forest in the Old Polish period, use was also made of descriptions of the few cottages and farm buildings surviving in a few villages, of surviving pieces of the furnishing of dwelling houses, of tools and of other objects, which, though they date

Sadebeck], *Materiallen zur historisch, statistik und topographisch Beschreibung von Südpreussen (...) 1793-1798*, call nos.: 2, 3; Sirisa (A. Sadebeck), *Historisch–statistisch–topographische–Beschreibung von Südpreussen und Neu–Ostpreussen oder Königlich–Preussischen Besitz–nehmungen von Polen in den Jahren 1793 und 1795 entworfen*, Leipzig 1798; AGAD, General Directorium Südpreussen, call no. 1745: register of hearths and population of 1796.

Photocopies from the collections of the workshop of the Historical-Geographical Dictionary of Greater Poland in the Middle Ages, Poznań branch of the Institute of History of the Polish Academy of Sciences.

Ostrowski, Warsaw, 21-22 November 1997. National Library Warsaw, PAN, 2000; B. Olszewicz, *Polska kartografia wojskowa. (Zarys historyczny)* [Polish military cartography. (A historical outline)], Warsaw 1921, pp. 177-188.

[^31]: [*Königliche Grosse Topographische Vermessungs Karte*] prepared by David Gilly, Cron, Langner, 1:5000, Staatsbibliothek Preussicher Kulturbesitz, West Berlin, call no. Kart N 14431. [In Rus.: MGd – Map of South Prussia of 1796-1805 at a scale of 1:57,600 (large Gilly)]; *Special Karte von Südpreussen (...) aus der Königlichen Grossen Topographischen Vermessungs Karte unter Mitwürking des Directors Langer reducirt und herausgegeben von Geheimen Bau-Rath Gilly (...)*.

[p. 20]

from the nineteenth and twentieth centuries, were undoubtedly based on older, eighteenth-century patterns, and of the museum photographs of them. This was done, however, at second hand, from the typescript of the master's thesis of Mirosława Jasińska on the Olęder settlements on the royal estates of the district of Pyzdry in the second half of the eighteenth century and from the article of S. Błaszczyk presenting the folk culture of the district of Kalisz, and, in the light of field research, from the studies of the architects I. F. Tłoczek, Piotr Szwiec and Radosław Barek[^32].

Written sources (in originals and copies) concerning above all the founding of the Olęder settlements and their internal administrative and judicial organisation, together with parts of cartographic sources showing where the Olęders were and portraits of their founders, are reproduced in the text of this study, with an explanation of each there and in the list of illustrations.

At the end of this study, besides the conclusions that follow from the account of these questions, we show very briefly, in outline, chiefly from the literature (and above all from the studies of A. Breyer mentioned above, from the cartographic sources and from the communes' development plans), some of the most important facts of the later history of rural settlement (Olęder settlement included) in the Pyzdry Forest, in the period from 1794 to recent years. The range of subjects and of time, the large holdings and different character of the sources and their scattering over many different archives mean that the history of this settlement in the years 1794-1864, and the later fortunes of the Olęder settlements, require a separate study.

In its construction the study consists of an introduction; six separate chapters, which present the questions named above in an essentially scholarly manner and are furnished with notes, bibliographical and sometimes on points of substance, numbered throughout; a conclusion; a *Glossary of Old Polish terms* that appear often in the text and are not understood today and need explaining; and a list of illustrations.

Master's thesis written in the Department of Early Modern History to the end of the eighteenth century, Adam Mickiewicz University, under the supervision of Prof. J. Topolski, Poznań 1992); St. Błaszczyk, *Kultura ludowa ziemi kaliskiej* [The folk culture of the Kalisz land]. [In:] *Osiemnaście wieków Kalisza* [Eighteen centuries of Kalisz], Vol. 2, Poznań 1961, pp. 386-459; I. F. Tłoczek, *Chałupy polskie* [Polish cottages], Warsaw 1958; P. Szwiec, a collective work, *Katalog architektury regionalnej dla obszaru Puszczy Pyzdrskiej* [Catalogue of the regional architecture of the Pyzdry Forest area], Pyzdry 2009; R. Barek, a collective work, *Puszcza Pyzdrska – wzornik zabudowy* [The Pyzdry Forest – a pattern book of buildings], Pyzdry 2011; the last two studies on the website www.puszczapyzdrska.com

[p. 21]

# 1. Distribution, chronology, causes, conditions and organisation of the founding of the Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest, and their naming

As has already been said above, 58 Olęder settlements were founded in the years 1746-1793, that is within almost half a century, in the area of the Pyzdry Forest, within 21 parishes and 3 former districts – those of Pyzdry, Konin and Kalisz – in the voivodeship of Kalisz, under several types of feudal landownership. The whole network of these settlements is shown on a map published in our day, Puszcza Pyzdrska (1:55,000), available in tourist bookshops. For times nearer to their founding it is shown, with varying accuracy, together with the Olęder settlements founded after 1793 and against the whole picture of settlement, by the Polish and Prussian maps described above. Parts of three of them, the *Mapa szczególna województwa kaliskiego* of Karol de Perthées, the map of South Prussia of David Gilly, and the *Karta topograficzna Królestwa Polskiego* prepared by the Quartermaster General's staff of the Polish Army, with the network of Olęder settlements in various parts of the Pyzdry Forest, are reproduced below (Ill. 1-3)[^33].

Below, for these 58 Old Polish settlements, we discuss in detail or more generally (according to how much the sources tell): their distribution (within the Old Polish territorial and administrative divisions of state and church, and the types of feudal landownership), chronology, causes, conditions (the natural and economic character of the land) and organisation (the legal, social and technical organisation of their founding, with the first names, surnames and

Kart N 14431. [In Rus.: MGd – Map of South Prussia of 1796-1805 at a scale of 1:57,600 (large Gilly)]; *Special Karte von Südpreussen (...) aus der Königlichen Grossen Topographischen Vermessungs Karte unter Mitwürking des Directors Langer reducirt und herausgegeben von Geheimen Bau-Rath Gily (...), 1802 i 1803*, Simon Schrop und Comp. Berlin [1:150,000] (small Gilly). Gilly's map (the smaller-scale, printed one) shows settlements unknown from written sources, or wrongly described as "holendry", rent-paying forest settlements that did not have an Olęder legal and economic character, both of which arose only after the Second Partition; see also: K. Buczek, Prace kartografów pruskich w Polsce za czasów Stanisława Augusta na tle współczesnej kartografii [The work of Prussian cartographers in Poland in the time of Stanisław August against the background of contemporary cartography], PKAHP PAU, III, 1935, pp. 176-177; the same, *Dzieje kartografii polskiej od XV do XVIII w.* [The history of Polish cartography from the fifteenth to the eighteenth century], Ossolineum, Wrocław-Warsaw-Kraków 1963; B. Medyńska-Gulij, D. Lorek, *Pruskie mapy topograficzne Wielkopolski do 1803 roku* [Prussian topographical maps of Greater Poland to 1803], Badania Fizjograficzne nad Polską Zachodnią. and property status of the founders, that is the establishers, sometimes of the locators and of the settlers, with their social position, confession and nationality, the kinds of founding documents, the ways and costs of setting up the settlements, and the naming of the settlements).

The oldest Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest, founded in 1746 in the district of Konin, in the parish and on the estate of Trąbczyn, were the Trąbczyńskie Stare Ol. and the Luśnie Ol. They were founded, on half of the wood Ostrowiec, half of the wood or rather marsh Stawisko, and on the woods Miąsykąt, Dziadulin and Luśnie, stretching from Stawisko towards Czarny Bród, by the married couple Paweł and Katarzyna née Rozdrażewska Prusimski, the owners of the estate at the time, by a charter of theirs that has not survived. This is known from the decree of the boundary commission court of 1776 on fixing the boundaries between the estates of Trąbczyn and Łukom. According to it, a part of the Trąbczyn estate together with the Olęder villages, which in 1771 had been wrongly adjudged by the boundary commission court and *tradowana*, that is officially handed over, to Stanisław Chełmski, the owner of the estate of Łukomia or Łomów, was to be restored (together with the income unlawfully drawn from it for 4 years) to the nephew and heir of the founder, Antoni Prusimski, owner of Trąbczyn Mały or Nowa Wieś, starost of Niszczewice [so called from the village of Niszczewice, the seat of a non-castle starosty in the district and voivodeship of Inowrocław]. On the strength of what this source says about the names, the Lutheran school and the cemetery, it can be said that the settlers placed in these Olęder villages were German peasants of the Lutheran, Evangelical-Augsburg, confession[^34] (Ill. 4). Since they were settled on wooded and marshy land, which needed clearing and draining, they probably received 7-8 free years to make the land fit for tillage and to put up their buildings. The inspection [description] of the Trąbczyn estate of 1754, eight years later, says that 15 Olęders lived in these settlements. Of these, 12 Olęders and an inn were in the wood Lusina, that is in the Luśnie Ol.[^35], which was taken to be the greater part of the Trąbczyńskie Stare Ol. in the wider sense, but was later also called the Łukomskie Ol. According to the act of tradition (judicial handing over) of a part of the Trąbczyn estate of 1771, mentioned above, to Stanisław Chełmski, owner of the estates of Łukomia and Łomów, by Antoni Prusimski,

Gr. 153 (1782), fols. 1404-1404v (decree of the commission court in the case between Antoni Prusimski, starost of Niszczewice, and Stanisław Chełmski, treasurer of Wschowa, held on 17 X 1782 in the house of the Olęder Marcin Hunt in the Trąmpczyńskie Ol., ordering that this Olęder's farm be joined to Prusimski's Trąbczyn estate). The foundation charter of 1746 is also mentioned in the summons to Karolina Fridenberg of the Kolonia Starołazińska before the Civil Tribunal in Kalisz of 7 (19) X 1860 to give evidence in the dispute between the owner of the Trąbczyn estate, Count Władysław Miączyński, and the colonists of the Kolonia Staro- and Nowołazińska, APP, SAB 783.

Olędrów 15, szesnasta huba próżna, jedni się pobudowali dobrze, drugi (!) tylko budy w ziemi mają" [Nowa Wieś – – . 15 Olęders, the sixteenth huba empty; some have built well, others (!) have only huts in the ground]; [in the Trąbczyn woods] "Zaś mimo Łazów i Osin między Hutą, gdzie najpiękniejsze drzewo było, i tam powycinane same też wybirki stoją. Także i dęby w lasach stojące dotychczas same też wybirki stoją tylko, gdyż olędry pozabirali hubami, gdzie piękniejsze drzewo było, wydzielonymi z dawna każdemu z nich" [And past Łazy and Osiny, by Huta, where the finest timber was, it has been cut there too, and only the culls stand. Of the oaks standing in the woods, too, only the culls stand so far, for the Olęders took them in hubas, where the finer timber was, allotted long ago to each of them].), APP, Konin. Gr. 136 (1754), fol. 122v, *oblata* (below: obl.) 12 VII 1754; the same in Der., *Materiały do dziejów chłopa*, vol. III, pp. 12-13.

[p. 31]

starost of Niszczewice, owner and possessor of the estates of Trąbczyn and others, there were 12 Olęders with a shepherd (10 with German and 2 with Polish names) in the settlement founded *not very long ago* in the forest or wood called Luśnie[^36].

The Trąbczyńskie Nowe Ol. probably arose after 1754 and before 1767. This seems to follow, indirectly, from two inspections, of the fields and of the boundaries of the Trąbczyn estate, both of 1767. According to the inspection of the fields, water flowing from the Stawisko had flooded the farms *of the Olęders settled in the Trąbczyn woods with ploughland measured out for tillage, and meadows*..., through the fault of people from the village of Łukom, acting on their owner's orders; and according to the inspection of the boundaries, the meadows of 4 Trąbczyn Olęders had been flooded, so far that they could not be mown, because the flowing water had been dammed in the pond by the throwing up of a dike[^37]. In 1791 Catholics and Lutherans lived in the Trąbczyńskie St. and Nw. Ol., and they then had 31 "self-tilling" (*samorolne*) hearths (farms)[^38], that is, farming ones.

Before 1750 and in 1772 the Przespolewskie Ol. arose in the district of Kalisz, in the parish of Przespolew Kościelny, on the noble estate of Przespolew Pański; they took in a part of Przespolew Pański and Poroże Wielkie. The approximate date of the founding of their oldest part can be fixed from the mention of the rents of the "olendrzy" in the act of tradition of 1750 to Leon Bielecki, *posesor*, that is the holder, of a part of the Przespolew estate[^39]. The founder, or rather the co-founder, of the second part of the Przespolewskie Ol., the settlement of Poroże Wielkie Ol., founded in 1772, was Marcin Stanisław Zaremba, Adjutant General of His Royal Majesty, owner of the property of Przespolew. On the strength of the foundation charter he issued that year to the "industrious Olęders" Marcin Schmarschel and Fryderyk Jakobcek, Marcin Lehmann and others, at least several German settlers, Lutherans, settled in the place. They were settled on wooded land, that is most probably in the forest, since they received 7 free years for setting up their farms and buildings[^40]. In the decree of the commission court of 1779 on the tradition of a part of the Przespolew estate in payment of a debt of its owner, Tadeusz Zaremba, to the creditor Tomasz Moraczewski, 3 Olęders living at Poroże are named (two of them probably the same as those named above in the charter of 1772), who paid, among other things, rent, and of the others it is said that they give no rent *since they still enjoy the freedom under their contracts for a time, except the innkeeper, handed over* [officially transferred] *to His Honour Pan Wolski* [probably yet another creditor]. *All these Olęders, in Przespolew as on the said Poroże, are handed over with their cottages, buildings and ploughland, rents and hubas*[^41]. The inventory of a part of the Przespolewskie

Gr. 414 (1771), fols. 402-402v and 403. See also Konin. Gr. 115 (1768-1774), fols. 185v-186v, 226 (inspection of the damage to the inn at Luśnie of 1772), 245.

Gr. 115 (1791), fol. 543 ("Hollendry Luźne Stare i Nowe z Hutą" [the Loose Old and New Hollendry with Huta]).

[p. 32]

Ol. of 1785, drawn up when they were handed over into the possession of Józef Wielowiejski by Ignacy Zaremba as guardian of the under-age Zarembas, names 22 Olęders with German names, 7 of whom had 3-6 free years left[^42]. In 1789, 25 hearths were recorded in Poroże Wielkie Ol., and in 1790, 37 hearths, that is farms, in the Przespolewskie Ol. in all, 12 more than in 1789; their number grew because further settlers were brought in[^43].

After 1754, and certainly before 1789 or by 1793, Łazy (Olędry) arose in the district of Konin, in the parish and on the noble estate of Trąbczyn, probably founded by those already mentioned above: Katarzyna née Rozdrażewska Prusimska, widow, or Antoni Prusimski, starost of Niszczewice[^44]. In the sources of the Old Polish period (as, for instance, in the inspection of the Trąbczyn estate of 1754) they are recorded without the addition *Olędry*, with which they appear only in the Prussian register of houses and population of 1796, which, however, gives them the same number of houses/hearths as the table of "self-tilling", that is farming, hearths of 1791. In 1791 they were inhabited by Catholics, 48 persons in all[^45]. It seems that, as an Olęder settlement, they arose through the transfer of their existing inhabitants, who had owed labour service, to Olęder law.

After 1765, and before 1793, the settlement of the Lisewskie Ol. (Olsz) was founded in the starosty and district of Pyzdry, in the parish of Szymanowice (whose centre lay in the district of Kalisz), by Michał Drogosław Skórzewski, then starost of Pyzdry in the years 1751-1789 (Ill. 5). Its founding can be linked, indirectly, with the conversion to rent of the village of Lisewo, until then a labour-service village, carried out after 1751 by the same starost. Having moved the labour-service village of Lisewo to rent for an entry fine, he decided to found,

Gr. 468 (1787), fol. 118v (lease of the Przespolew estate between F. Mokrzycki, the holder, and T. Kobylański, the lessee, of 24 VI 1786, with information on the Olęders' harvest labour)). See also Kalisz. Gr. 455 (1784), fols. 641v, 642-642v.

Kalisz (detailed table of the hearths and population of the district of Kalisz), fol. 130v: "...Holendry Nowa Kolunia w nazwisku Poroże Wielkie – 25 dm." [the Hollendry, the New Colony by the name of Poroże Wielkie – 25 hearths]; among them "chałup gront wyrudowany mający[ch] i z niego czynsz opłacają" [cottages having their ground cleared and paying rent from it] 13, "Budów z wolności nie wyszłych i czynszów nie opłacających oprócz jednego jeszcze bez zabudowania" [huts not yet out of their free years and paying no rent, besides one still without buildings] 12 (of these, 7 huts settled in 1789, which began clearing in 1789 and are to pay rent of 36 złp. each in 1796, 2 huts settled, which began clearing in 1787 and are to pay rent of 36 złp. each in 1794, and 3 huts settled, which began clearing in 1788 and are to pay rent of 36 złp. each in 1793), and in 1 hearth, in which lives "the schoolmaster [teacher], out of his free years...", who pays no rent at all; Tab. 25. Gr. 115 (Table of the grain levy from the self-tilling hearths of the districts of the voivodeship of Kalisz of 1791), fol. 543: "Łazy" – 5 hearths (fol. 543); AGAD. [^45]: ADWł., Wiz. 85, fols. 23 ff.

[p. 33]

north-west of Lisewo, the new settlement of the Lisewskie Ol., also called Olsz or Olszowiec. The information of two surveys of the starosty of Pyzdry, that of 1765 on the entry-fine village of Lisewo paying rents, and that of 1789 on the farmers and the rent-paying cottagers in Lisewo and on four *contract settlers on isolated holdings* (*pustkowianie kontraktowi*) by the village of Lisewo[^46], does not undermine the view that this second settlement was Olęder in its legal character, even though its inhabitants are not expressly called Olęders. Some doubt on this point can be raised only by the fact that its inhabitants were on individual contracts (agreements). This, however, seems to have been temporary, and they seem to have obtained a collective (though to us unknown) foundation charter, since in the later sources too the addition *Olędry* was consistently used in the name of this village. In the registers of the parish of Szymanowice of the years 1775-1780 there are entries on inhabitants of the "Lisewskie Olędry *alias* [otherwise] Olszowiec", probably mixed in nation and confession[^47]. This settlement also appears as *Holendry Lisewskie* on the map of K. Perthées. The village now called Kolonia Lisewo, on the other hand, was not an Olęder settlement (at least not an Old Polish one). It was founded with German and Lutheran settlers in the so-called Frederician colonisation in the period of so-called South Prussia, in its Poznań department, on the state domain of Pyzdry, as a colony by the demesne farm of Lisewo, which in 1804 numbered 20 families, 99 persons, with an area of 23 łany and 13 morgen[^48].

Before 1755-1756 the Ciemierowa/Tomickie/Szymanowickie Stare Olędry were founded, situated in the district of Kalisz and the parish of Szymanowice, and recorded on Gilly's map. Their founder was probably Ignacy Samek Glis[z]czyński, starost of Pasierby, so called from the village of Pasierby, the seat of a non-castle *starosty*, in the district of Gostynin, in the voivodeship of Rawa, owner of the estate of Tomice. From the form of the names of the Olęders written in the baptismal registers of the parish of Szymanowice in those years[^49] it can be inferred that Catholics, Germans and Poles, lived in this settlement, though it is not out of the question that some of them were Protestant Lutherans, who were also often forced to receive this sacrament at the hands of Catholic priests.

In 1761 the Zbierskie Olędry were founded in the district of Kalisz, in the parish of Zbiersk, on the noble estate of that name. Their founder was Stanisław Dzik Kożuchowski, land judge of Wieluń. According to the emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter he issued at Zbiersk on 16 December 1761, the colonists were settled in the forest, and received 8 free years for clearing and setting up their farms

[^47]: ADWł, Registers of the parish of Szymanowice 1762-1797 (I cite after M. Jasińska, *op. cit.*).

[^48]: J. Wąsicki, *Kolonizacja niemiecka w okresie Prus Południowych 1793 – 1806*, p. 165.

See also the receipts for rents received from the Olęders in the village of Tomice of the years 1769-1783, 1789), APP, Pyzdry. Gr. 107 (1784) fols. 1215-1216; in Czaykowski, p. 117: Hollendry and Wierzchy in the parish of Szymanowice in the deanery of Pleszew; MGdruk: Szymanowicer Hol. On the Topograficzna Karta Królestwa Polskiego, col. I, sec. 2: Holendry Stare Ciemierowa. Although Ciemierów earlier belonged to the district of Pyzdry, in the sources of the end of the eighteenth century (APP, Collection of tax tables and surveys. Tab. 21 and 25 (Tof. z Todl). Kalisz) it is recorded in the district of Kalisz, as are the Stare Ol. lying above it.

[p. 34]

(counted from 11 XI 1761). 14 settlers were settled (7 with German and 7 with Polish names), Protestants (as is shown by the permission to keep a *bakałarz*, that is a teacher who read the Gospel to them, and by the setting aside of a plot for a burial ground). Under the *oblata* (official copy) of the text of the foundation charter the Olęders are named, in the number given above, settled in the years 1761-1778, partly the same as the first ones of 1761 but partly others, probably among other things because farms were sold and bought. This charter laid down *that from the beginning and the choosing of his huba every inhabitant is to take a card to remember his free years and to go to his lord, so that their first names and surnames be written down*[^50] (Ill. 6). From these two premises it can be inferred that the founding of the whole village took as long as 17 years. The inventory of the Zbiersk estate, which was then held by Leon Gąsiorowski, royal chamberlain (*szambelan*), drawn up in 1771 by the lessees, the married couple Jakub and Franciszka Przespolewski, names 6 Olęders in this settlement (the sołtys among them) and their dues, and 3 Olęders not yet paying them[^51]. The inventory of the Zbiersk estate drawn up in 1775 found, among other things, that there were still 14 farmers in the Zbierskie Ol.[^52].

Before or in 1767 Wielołęka was founded as an Olęder settlement in the district of Konin, in the parish of Grodziec, on the noble estate of Królików. In a protest lodged in 1767 with the Crown Tribunal at Bydgoszcz by Piotr Kurcewski, owner of the estate of Kuchary Borowe, against Józef Wężyk, sword-bearer and judge of the land of Wieluń, owner of the estates of Grójec (Grodziec) and Królikowo, there is already mention of the *deserta* [empty land] *Łęka, now called Wielołęka with Olędry*[^53]. This village was founded first by a collective foundation charter issued by Józef Wężyk, castellan of Konary and of Sieradz, given at the castle of Grodziec on 6 September 1770. A copy of this charter in German shows that the settlers had earlier received separate, individual foundation charters, from the issuing of which their seven free years, meant for clearing and setting up their farms on the deserted village overgrown with forest, were to be counted[^54]. According to the second collective emphyteutic-perpetual charter, issued at the castle of Grodziec on 6 November 1770 by the same Józef Wężyk, which was in fact an agreement between the founder of the settlement

[^50]: APP, Kalisz. Gr. 448, fols. 254-255v (obl. 21 VII 1783). The Reformed confession of the settlers may, but need not, be indicated by the letter of Adam Oppeln Bronikowski, owner of the estate of Żychlin, to Jakub Przespolewski, holder of the Zbiersk estate, of 10 VII 1776, on the latter's hindering the Zbiersk Olęders from attending services in the Reformed church at Żychlin, in spite of the laws of 1768 and 1775 guaranteeing dissenters freedom of public worship, APP, Kalisz. Gr. 424 (1776), fols. 165-165v.

III, p. 54; see also: "Regestr pretensji z posiedzicielstwa dóbr Zbierska od roku 1771 aż do r. 1784 wynikających" [Register of claims arising from the holding of the Zbiersk estate from 1771 to 1784], APP, Kalisz. Gr. 469 (1787), fols. 158-159, 180, 190, 199-200, 208, 211.

[p. 35]

6\. (1-2) Parts of the emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the Zbierskie Ol. of 11 XI 1761, issued by Stanisław Dzik Kożuchowski, land judge of Wieluń, owner of the Zbiersk estate. APP Kal. Gr. 448, fols. 254-255v (obl. 25 VII 1783).

[p. 36]

and the Olęders, personally free peasants, 9 in number (8 of them with German names and 1 with a Polish name), Lutherans, settled in Wielołęka *by the right of free purchase* (as is shown by the allotment of half a huba to the *szkolny* [teacher] and by the mention of an agreement with the parish priest of Grodziec about the fees for weddings and funerals). The settlement arose *on the lands of the empty village of Wielołęka... now overgrown with woods and forests*. The foundation agreement was made with the founder on behalf of the Olęders by Jan Czarneski and Jan Kimel[^55] (Ill. 7). The inventory of the properties of Grodziec, Królików and others of 1779 shows that 15 farmers lived in the Wielołęckie Ol. (14 of them named with first names and surnames of German sound, 1 innkeeper and 1 not named

APP, SAB. 789.

[p. 38]

7\. (2) Parts of the foundation charter for Wielołęka as an Olęder settlement, issued on 6 XI 1770 by Jan Wężyk, castellan of Konary, of Sieradz, in Polish, an authenticated extract from the records of the Civil Tribunal of the Government of Kalisz of 19 IX/1 X 1841, a typescript copy. APP, SAB. 789.

[p. 39]

by name, the *szkolny*, that is the teacher), partly still *rudujący* [clearing] their holdings, which differed in area[^56]. This village was in a way re-founded and enlarged by his son-in-law Count Franciszek Stadnicki, starost of Ostrzeszów, owner of the estates of Grójec (Grodziec), Królików and Biskupice, by his emphyteutic-perpetual charter, issued at Biskupice on 3 February 1791. He did so... *to increase the population and multiply the intrats* [income] *from useless woods, moved by the requests* [!] *of free incomers, partly already settled and partly still to settle..., who have already paid the gruntgielt* [entry money]. The free years were probably 7, or at least 5. In this charter the founder also assigned the village half a huba free of charge for a little school, and laid on the settlers, who were of mixed confession, Catholics and dissenters (Protestants), the obligation to pay fees for baptisms, weddings and funerals to the parish priest of the parish church at Grodziec; the dissenters too were to hold their baptisms and weddings in that church[^57].

In 1768 the Cekowskie Stare Ol. arose in the district of Kalisz, on the royal estates, in the lease (*dzierżawa*) of Ceków, in the parish of Kosmów. Their founder was Jan Bezan, captain of the Front Guard of the Crown of His Royal Majesty and the Commonwealth, and the foundation charter he issued at Ceków on 18 April 1768 was confirmed on 26 January ("tycznia") 1774 by their next lessee, Franciszek Byszewski. 9 *honest men of the Olęder estate* (2 with Polish and 7 with German names), Lutherans and Catholics, were settled in the settlement. They settled "in the thickets of the Ceków estate", that is in woodland that needed *rudunek* (clearing), for which, and for setting up their farms, they received 7 free years. They were also bound to pay tithe to the parish priest of the parish church at Kosmów. The settlers declared that they were ready to pay for the "contract", that is the collective charter, a minted thaler each, having already paid for it *one red złoty*, that is a ducat[^58]. According to the inventory of the Ceków estate of 1774 there were then 5 Olęder farmers here, newly brought in, settled under a contract, that is under the foundation charter described above. *"They have put up huts for themselves, by which they want to bind themselves further according to the measuring out of the hubas* [łany, włóki], *only that His Honour Pan* [Antoni] *Kawecki, owner* [of neighbouring] *Kuszyn, forbids them, maintaining that it is his own ground; where the lands were allotted them by the late His Honour Pan Jan Bezan, holder of the estate of the village of Ceków, and for that reason they do not want to bind themselves by these"*[^59].

In the years 1770-1772 six more Olęder settlements were founded in the district of Konin, in the parishes of Królików and partly Grodziec and Królików, on the noble estates of Grodziec and Królików.

[^56]: Inventory drawn up at the request of Paweł Drogosław Skórzewski, *wojski* of Kalisz, 15 V 1779, APP, Kalisz. Gr. 433 (1779), fols. 462-462v (the same in: Der., *Materiały do dziejów chłopa*, vol. III, p. 27).

[^57]: APP, Kalisz. Gr 482 (1791), fols. 81-81v and 87-87v; the enlargement of this village is shown by the growth of the number of its hearths from 13 in all in 1789 to 24 "self-tilling" hearths in 1791, APP, Pyzdry. Gr. 115 (1791), fol. 540.

[^58]: APP, Kalisz. Gr. 435 (1780), fols. 153v-154 (obl. 19 VI 1780).

[^59]: Inventory of the Ceków estate, drawn up at Ceków on 20 I 1774 when it was handed over on lease to Arnold Byszewski, colonel of the Front Guard of the Crown army, signed by Franciszek Byszewski, [in:] *Królewszczyzny kaliskie w drugiej połowie XVIII w*. [The crown estates of Kalisz in the second half of the eighteenth century]. Edited with an introduction by W. Rusiński, "Rocznik Kaliski". Vol. XVII, Poznań 1984, p. 171.

[p. 40]

They included, first, Konary or the Konarskie Olędry, which their owner, Józef Wężyk, already mentioned, founded on the Grodziec estate by a charter that was in effect shared with, and in any case identical in content to, that of the Wielołęka Ol., issued on 6 September 1770. Lutheran Olęders settled there, as is shown by the mention in this charter of an agreement with the parish priest of Grodziec about the fees for weddings and funerals[^60] (Ill. 8). The founding of this settlement was formally confirmed and extended by Count Franciszek Stadnicki by his charter, shared by them and the Borowieckie Ol., issued in Polish at the castle of Grójec (Grodziec) on 25 August 1785. The settlement was founded in the Grójec forests, which were a part of the Pyzdry Forest. The settlers were granted 7 free years *...to those now already settled, and these* [the Grójec forests] *...granting to each from his entering and paying the gruntgielt..."*. According to this charter the settlers were Protestants and Catholics[^61]. This last piece of information can, however, apply only to the Borowieckie Ol., since in Konary, as information from another contemporary source cited below shows, only Lutherans lived.

Probably in 1770 the Grójeckie or Grodzieckie Olędry (today a part of Grodziec, or perhaps Janowo?) were founded on the Grodziec and Królików estates, by an unknown charter, also issued by Józef Wężyk. The settlers were placed in the forest. This follows from the inspection of the estates of Grodziec and Królików with their appurtenances of 1771, made at the request of Helena née Jordan, widow of Józef Wężyk, *pani oprawna* (that is, holding a life settlement on this property), in which they are described as: *the Hollendry of Grudziec, newly settled on ten hubas, who are only now clearing [them] for building themselves*[^62].

Probably in the same year 1770 the Białobłockie (Białe, Białogłowy, otherwise Królikowskie) Olędry arose on the Królików estate, founded by an unknown charter issued by the owner of this estate, Józef Wężyk, named above[^63]. Their existence in 1775 is confirmed by a deed of purchase and sale of land, dated 10 March 1775 and signed by the sołtys and the assessors of this commune[^64]. They were re-founded and enlarged by a charter of 17 October 1787, issued by his son-in-law and heir, the Count Franciszek Stadnicki already mentioned. He did so *to multiply the intrats* [income] *from an increased population, moved by the request of free incomers wishing to settle in the Królików forests..*. [and at the] *request of the Olęders now already settled and of free incomers wishing to settle in the Królików forests*. He granted them *a right that is to serve in perpetuity*

8\. Part of the foundation charter in German of Józef Wężyk, castellan of Konary, of Sieradz, for Konary/the Konarskie Ol. of 6 IX 1770. APP, SAB 696.

[p. 42]

*to the whole community of the Białobłockie Hollędry now already settled, granting to each from his entering and paying the gruntgield...* The settlers then took up the Królików forests, which they were to clear within 7 free years. In this charter the founder also laid on the Olęders, who were of mixed confession, Catholics and dissenters (Protestants), the obligation to pay fees for baptisms, weddings and funerals to the parish priest of the parish church at Królików[^65] (Ill. 9). The number of settlers,

APP. Kalisz Gr. 476 (1789), fols. 421-422v.

[p. 44]

10\. (2) Parts of the emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the community of the Białobłockie Olędry "now already settled", of 17 X 1787 and 1792, of Count Franciszek Stadnicki, SAB 626 (no. 8, extract of the *oblata* of 29 VI 1788 from the municipal court book of Kalisz). APP, SAB 626.

[p. 45]

including those brought in to settle in 1787, is unknown. One of the phrases of this charter quoted above suggests that they had come in and settled in the area of this village earlier, partly on the strength of individual agreements, from the making of which their free years were counted.

Probably as early as about 1770 the Borowieckie Stare Ol. also arose on the same estates, founded by an unknown charter issued by Józef Wężyk[^66]. This is confirmed by a deed of purchase of land of 1770, signed by the sołtys and the assessors of this commune[^67]. According to the table of hearths of 1775 this settlement, described as "Olędry Borowiec i Huta", had 29 farms[^68]. The inventory of the properties of Grodziec and Królików of 1779 found 27 farmers living in the Borowieckie Ol., named with their first names and surnames (4 of them Polish), on holdings that differed in area[^69]. The existence of this settlement was confirmed by Wężyk's son-in-law and heir, Count Franciszek Stadnicki, also already mentioned, in the joint foundation charter for the Konarskie Ol. and for them of 25 August 1785, founding at the same time, it seems, the Borowieckie Nowe Ol. This charter contains the statement: *All these points and descriptions set out above, in keeping with the customary Dutch laws, the whole settlement of the Borowieckie Holendry most solemnly promises, and is bound, to observe*. The settlers, who were then settled in an unknown number, were Lutherans and Catholics by confession[^70] (Ill. 9).

Before and in 1771, and in 1791, the Grądy Stare and Nowe Ol. were also founded on the same property. The former were probably founded by Józef Wężyk himself[^71]. Their founding was confirmed, and extended to the Grądy Nw. Ol. as well, by Count Franciszek Stadnicki in an emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter of 1791, meant for *the Olęders on the Grądy, who have already paid the grontgielt*; he declared that he did so *...to increase the population and multiply the intrats from useless woods, moved by the request of free incomers, partly already settled and partly still to settle...*, that is with a motive like the one he gave in the charters for the Konarskie Ol. and the Białobłockie Ol. (Ill. 10). The settlers, Catholics and Protestant Lutherans (judging from the mention of a little school and of the obligations to the parish priest of the Catholic parish church of Grodziec), were settled in the forest, and received probably 7 free years. This charter stated that: *The farmers newly settled not long ago on poorer lands* [probably in the Grądy Nw. Ol. – Z.Ch.] *after their rudowanie* [clearing], *when they are recognised as such by the commune office and the manor's jurisdiction* [authority], would have relief in their dues to the manor[^72] (Ill. 11).
