11\. Part of the joint second emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the Grądy St. and Nw., founded before and in 1772 and in 1791, in the district of Konin, issued on 6 II 1791 by Count Franciszek Stadnicki, starost of Ostrzeszów, owner of the estates of Grójec (Grodziec), Królików and Biskupice. APP, Kalisz. Gr. 482, fols. 77-77v and 89-89v, obl. 4 III 1791.

[p. 47]

About 1771 the Gliny Olędry were founded in the district of Konin, in the parish of Dzierzbin (whose centre lay in the district of Kalisz), on the estate of Biała (Panieńska) of the convent of the Order of St Clare (the Franciscan nuns) of Kalisz[^73]. The inhabitants of this village were probably Catholics and Poles.

Not much later, in 1774 and 1780, the Cekowskie Nowe [Ol.] or Prażuchy were set up in the district of Kalisz, on the royal estates, in the lease of Ceków. The founders of this settlement were first Arnold Byszewski, colonel of the Front Guard, later major general of the Crown army, and Krzysztof Celiński, holder [from or after 1775] *by the right he acquired, both of life tenure and of emphyteusis for fifty years, which... serves him by inheritance*. At least 8 settlers (6 with German and 2 with Polish names), Lutherans, settled there. They took up *the ground in Ceków beyond the pond called Prażuchy*, which was undoubtedly wooded, since in the contract (foundation charter) of 1780 they received 7 free years *for clearing the ground and the meadows*. This contract was made with four *honest free men of the Dutch estate*, named by their surnames, who (...) *had settled earlier under the contract of the former holder, the Most Illustrious and Magnificent His Honour Pan Arnold Byszewski, major general in the Crown army, and also with other men of the Dutch estate, whoever signs this contract*. The list of Olęders included in the charter shows that the founding of the village was still going on in the years 1780-1782, and that some farms changed owners later, in 1785 and 1787[^74] (Ill. 12).

12\. (1) Parts of the emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the Cekowskie Nw. Ol., issued on 28 X 1780 by Krzysztof Celiński, APP, Kal. Gr. 450, fols. 27-27v and 29-29v; obl. 27 III 1783 by the honest Bogusław Szwander, Olęder of the Ceków estate.

[^73]: See APP, Kalisz. Gr. 414 (1771), fols. 124v and 141-142.

[^74]: APP, Kal. Gr. 450 (1783), fols. 27-27v and 29-29v (emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter issued at Stropieszyn on 28 X 1780 by Krzysztof Celiński; obl. 27 III 1783 by the honest Bogusław Szwander, Olęder of the Ceków estate); this foundation charter is mentioned in: APP, Tab. 20, Kalisz (Survey of the starosties and crown estates of the voivodeship of Kalisz of 1789), p. 23; cf. also the Cekowskie St. Ol.

[p. 48]

In the years 1775 and 1784, in the same district, on the noble estate of Kosmów, in the parish of that name, the Kosmowskie or Szadokierskie Olędry were founded. Their founder was Antoni Kawiecki, owner of the estates of Kosmów [Kuszyn and Gostynie]. The settlement agreement (foundation charter) of 1775 was made between him and two colonists settled in the Kosmów forest called Szadykierz, with Polish names, Marcin Kapliński and Jan Droga/Droże, *...who make the contract in their own name and that of others..*., acting as the locators, that is the establishers, of this village. In this agreement ...*all who, together with those making the contract, wish to come and build were given* 7 free years. The colonists were Protestant Lutherans, as is confirmed by the mention in the charter of an agreement with the parish priest of Kosmów about their obligations to him[^75]. Two further settlers, "honest", that is personally free, also with Polish names, were settled under Olęder law by a foundation charter of 1784, receiving the same free years[^76].

Next, in the years 1776-1781, in the same district, on the same estates and in the same parish, and by the same founder, Antoni Kawiecki, owner of the estates of Kuszyn, Kosmów and Plewnie, the settlement of the Kuszyńskie or Jaszczurowo Olędry was founded. 7 colonists (4 with German and 3 with Polish names), Lutherans, settled there. They took up *the forest or deserta* (empty land) Jaszczurowo, belonging to Kosmów, and received 7 free years. They first made the settlement agreement in 1776 with the founder

12\. (2) Parts of the emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the Cekowskie Nw. Ol., issued on 28 X 1780 by Krzysztof Celiński, APP, Kal. Gr. 450, fols. 27-27v and 29-29v; obl. 27 III 1783 by the honest Bogusław Szwander, Olęder of the Ceków estate.

[^75]: APP, Kalisz. Gr. 433 (1779), fols. 509-510.

[^76]: APP, Kalisz Gr. 482 (1791), fol. 682 (foundation charter for 2 Olęders to build on half-hubas in Kosmów under Olęder law, "jako i in. Ol. Kosmowskie" [like the other Kosmowskie Ol.], issued at Kuszyn on 2 VI 1784; *oblata* in 1791, with a receipt for the hearth tax of 1787-1789); Tab. 24. Kalisz (Tof. z Todl. of 1789), fols. 90v-91 and 115, with the information that 4 Olęders will begin to pay rent in 1790,

[p. 49]

two representatives of the settlers, Michał Gesse and Krzysztof Malus, and then in the years 1776-1781 a further 5 Olęders were added to it. According to this charter the settlement, because the place was so small, had a little school and a cemetery in common with the neighbouring Kosmowskie Ol.[^77].

In 1776 the Lubieńskie (Lubień) or Biała Olędry were set up in the district of Konin, in the parish of Dzierzbin, on the estate of Biała (Panieńska) belonging to the convent of the Order of St Clare (the Franciscan nuns) of Kalisz. Their foundress was Anna Chlebowska, prioress of that convent, who together with other members of its governing body issued two emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charters on 10 November 1776. According to them the village was founded at the urgent *requests of the Holędrzy*. The settlers, personally free, Catholics and Poles, settled *by the right of free purchase* on the *deserta* [empty land] Lubień, and received 7 free years (counted from 10 XI 1776), but their years of freedom were to run out according to their separate contracts, that is agreements[^78] (Ill. 13). This last piece of information seems to show that they settled in woodland that needed *rudunek* (clearing), at first under individual agreements.

Probably as early as about 1770, and in 1781, two settlements were founded in the district of Pyzdry, in the parish of Zagórów, in the starosty of Pietrzyków: Wrąbczynek and the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol. Their earlier origin is confirmed by two *konotacje* (memoranda) issued to the Olęders by I. Gałęski, the plenipotentiary of Szczepan Turno, starost of Pietrzyków. In the first, of 23 July 1776, the manorial dues from 12 hubas (włóki) of farms were set *for the industrious people of Rąmpczyn*, that is for the inhabitants of Wrąbczynek, who still owed labour service (after the old *konotacja* had been lost). In the second, of 30 September 1777, a confirmation was issued of the measuring of the lands by the sworn surveyor Bogusław, which had found only 11 hubas, and accordingly the inhabitants of Wrąbczynek were let off the rent of 1 huba. It also contained the statements that on that day the community was to choose a sołtys and 2 assessors with the manor's consent, and that a royal law would be issued for them, that is a confirmation of the foundation charter by the monarch. This *konotacja* also contained the information: *Since the Rąbczynek Holendry, their free years for the rumunek being out, have today received the law under which they are to remain in their dues, so that these too* [that is the Olęders in Wrąbczynek], *settled together with them, are not raised to higher dues than the Holędrzy, they are ascribed an equalisation with those, so that they* [belong] *to the dues set out here*[^79]. It follows that the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol. were founded on overgrown, probably wooded, land, and that the two settlements were founded at the same time. In the act of tradition (judicial handing over) of the starosty of Pietrzyków

[^77]: APP, Kalisz. Gr. 448, fols. 85v and 98 (emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter, issued at the manor of Kuszyn on 1 V 776 [sic], with additions on the settling, besides the 2 settlers who made the agreement in 1776, of further colonists: 2 (from 1 V 1776) + 1 (from 1 V 1777) + 1 (from 1 V 1779) and 1 (from 1 V 1781), which was also confirmed in its *oblata* of 1781, dated Kuszyn 1 V 1776 and 1781).

[^79]: APP, Pyzdry. Gr. 110, fols. 200-202.

[p. 50]

13\. (1) Parts of the emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the Lubieńskie Ol., issued on 10 XI 1777 by Anna Chlebowska, superior of the convent of the Order of St Clare (the Franciscan nuns) in Kalisz. APP, Kalisz. Gr. 429 (1778), fols. 153v-154v.

[p. 51]

13\. (2, 3) Parts of the emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the Lubieńskie Ol., issued on 10 XI 1777 by Anna Chlebowska, superior of the convent of the Order of St Clare (the Franciscan nuns) in Kalisz. APP, Kalisz. Gr. 429 (1778), fols. 153v-154v.

[p. 52]

of 19 June 1781, issued by Krystyna née Szembek Turnowa, wife of the *stolnik* of Poznań, the mother, and Stefan/Szczepan Turno, starost of Pietrzyków, the son, to Ignacy Dobrski, the description of the village of Wrąbczynek contains the information that there are handed over to him *the Olędry both on the Budy and the Nowe Olędry, whose freedom runs out this very year, with all their dues and rents...*[^80]. The inspection of 1776 of the place (land) *Benefickie* beyond the river Warta, belonging to the estates of the town of Pyzdry, tells of its being taken over, together with a part of the old road from Pyzdry to Konin, by an Olęder recently settled by Stefan (Szczepan) Turno, and that *in the wood Spławie the Olęders of the said Pan Turno are clearing, cutting the wood down to the stump and destroying the building timber*[^81]. According to the joint, collective and final emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for both settlements, issued at Pietrzyków on 20 October 1781 by the said Szczepan Turno, they were founded to bring the ruined royal estates of the starosty of Pietrzyków, and precisely *the villages of Rąpczyno*, into good condition. The founder therefore granted to Jakub Krysa [Kriese], sołtys, and to Wojciech Kurtz and Krzysztof Szultz, assessors, *...the demesne farm with all its lands and the village thickets* in the existing village of Wrąbczynek, with a total area of 11 hubas measured by the surveyor, *besides the gardens and village plots, and the pastures; and to the Rąpczyn Hollendry* [that is the inhabitants of the Wrąbczynkowskie Olędry], 9 ½ hubas cleared by them from the *deserta* [empty land] in the forest, having earlier set up in them a sołtys and 2 assessors, named with their first names and surnames. They received their free years, that is the time for building and setting up their farms, according to the individual contracts they had obtained earlier. They received, besides, 16 morgen of meadows they had cleared. The provisions of the charter applied to both settlements, as the phrase in it confirmed: *By this present law* [the collective one], *long promised them, I secure both the village commune and the Dutch commune...*". Settlers with German and Polish names settled in both settlements. Most of them were probably Lutherans, as is shown by the grant to both settlements of land for a teacher and for a burial ground[^82], although the visitation of the deanery of Konin of 1792 shows only Catholics in Wrąbczynek and only Protestants in the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol.[^83]. In an *asekuracja* (written assurance) of 20 XI 1781, issued by S. Dobrski, plenipotentiary of Turno, starost of Pietrzyków, besides confirming that the labour services had been done, the Olęders were promised that they would obtain the royal law (that is, the king's confirmation of the foundation charter issued by the starost-tenant) once they had paid a contribution for the purpose, and it is stated *that the Germans, who have been measured out on the lord's demesne farm, will be bound to pay the remaining half of the grungielt*"[^84]. The confirmation of this charter by King Stanisław August Poniatowski, however, came only on 2 February 1787[^85].

[p. 53]

In 1777, and then before and in 1784, the Gadowskie Stare and Nowe [Ol.] (today Gadowskie Holendry) were set up in the district of Konin, in the parish of Dzierzbin. Their founders were Piotr Sokół Gałczyński and Agnieszka née Sieroszewska Gałczyńska, his widow, life holder of the estates of Gadów, Smuszew and others with their appurtenances. She justified the founding of the settlement on economic grounds in her foundation charter issued at Gadów on 1 November 1784: *Since the extent and abundance of the woods on the estates of Gadów with their appurtenances... bring no use, neither now nor later, taking the example practised in this country until now in setting up Holędrzy, on hereditary estates and on estates held for life, seeing the need to strengthen the revenues* [income] *of Gadów, weakened by various accidents to the land, and wishing to keep their condition at its best for my most beloved children in my lifetime* [that is, to keep these estates in the best possible condition for the children], *and also to hand* [pass] *on to them the intrats of these estates increased later* [the profits of these estates increased later], *I have resolved ... to give emphyteutic law to the Gadowskie Holendry, to free people subject to no subjection, brought in while my husband was still alive... and coming in during my possession ... according to separate contracts* [agreements]. This charter names 3 settlers with German names, the sołtys and the assessors, but there were also others with German and Polish names, Protestant Lutherans and Catholics. They were settled in the forest called "Zielony Las" (the Green Wood). Its text also shows that they settled gradually and received free years fixed individually, set earlier by separate contracts[^86]. According to a protest of 23 VIII 1787 against A. Gałczyńska, lodged by Bogumił Leszczyc Dobrzycki, royal chamberlain, owner of the estate of Rychwał and of the *deserta* of Sokołowo with their appurtenances, which bordered on the Gadów estates, *A. Gałczyńska settled some dozen Olęders on the Rychwał forests and the deserta* [empty land] *of Sokołowo, which she had seized by force and unlawfully, having cut down the trees and sold them, and besides she is carrying off by force the hay from meadows she has appropriated*[^87]. In the inspection of the Gadów estates of 10 June 1780, A. Gałczyńska argued that the Olęders had been settled on land whose belonging to Gadów had not been questioned for centuries, and in the answers to the court's interrogatories (questions) of 3 XII 1787, which were also put to the inhabitants of the Gadowskie Ol., there was, among other information, also this, that they had been settled 10 years before[^88].

After 1777 and before 1784 the Smuszewskie Ol. arose, in the same district and parish. This seems to be indicated, indirectly, by the following passage of the foundation charter for the Gadowskie Ol. of 1784: *I reserve for myself for later, that the sołtys and the assessors keep every discipline and obedience of the said Dutch communes...*[^89], which may also concern the Smuszewskie Ol. and may indirectly

[^87]: APP, Konin. Gr. 121 (1787-1788), fols. 201-201v.

[^88]: APP. Konin Gr. 148 (1780), fol. 341 (obl. 12 VI 1780); according to this inspection, made at the request of Agnieszka Gałczyńska, people from Sarbicko, Dobrzycki's village, pulled down 2 buildings of an Olęder who had settled under a charter once given by P. Gałczyński, and grazed off and trampled the grain he had sown and the meadows he had cleared, committing all these abuses in an area whose belonging to Gadów had not been questioned for centuries; Kalisz. Gr. 469 (1787), fol. 419.

[^89]: Cf. n. 86.

[p. 54]

show that they too arose at the latest before or in 1784, and probably formed a common commune with the Gadowskie Nw. and St. Ol., which also seems to be suggested by the fact that in 1791 they had only 2 "self-tilling" hearths[^90].

In 1778 the Pyzdrskie Olędry were founded. They were founded by the council of the town of Pyzdry (the president Karol Ogrodowicz and 5 councillors) in order to protect the forests belonging to the town. The settlers were Poles and Catholics, partly burghers of Pyzdry and partly peasants of the surrounding area. They settled in the Pyzdry forests and received 7 free years for clearing their holdings[^91] (Ill. 14). The issuing of their foundation charter on ordinary paper and not on stamped paper, as the law required, became the cause of long lawsuits between the Olęders and the town authorities[^92]. The Pyzdrskie Olędry were the only Olęder settlement in Greater Poland that arose on the estates of a royal town. Today, as was said above, 9 settlements or parts of them correspond to it[^93] (cf. Tab. 1).

In the same year 1778 the Jarantowskie (Jaruntowskie) or Jarantów Olędry were founded in the district of Kalisz, in the parish of Lipe, on a noble estate[^94]. From the remains of a Protestant cemetery it can be said that only, or also, Protestants lived there.

In 1781 Wierzchy, or the Tomickie Nowe Olędry, were created in the district of Kalisz, in the parish of Szymanowice, on the noble estate of Tomice. Their founders, by an emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter issued at Tomice on 23 September of that year, were Jan Samek Glis[z]czyński, chamberlain (*podkomorzy*) of His Royal Majesty, owner of the estates of Tomice and Wierzchy, and his wife K. Glis[z]czyńska. 12 settlers with Polish (8) and German (4) names settled in the settlement, most probably Lutherans, since remains of a Protestant cemetery have survived in the village, or mixed in confession and nation, given the German and Polish names of its inhabitants recorded for this village in the

Gr. 118 (1780), fols. 158-159v; here it was entered (*oblata*) on 16 X 1780 by the honest Jakub Bończyk, sołtys, and Józef Baranowski, assessor.

[p. 58]

baptismal registers of the parish of Szymanowice (which is discussed in detail below). They received 7 free years for building and setting up their farms, which ran out for all the Olęders in 1788, except the first, whose freedom ran out in 1787[^95]; so they settled on overgrown, most probably wooded, land.

In the years 1782 the Kazimierskie or Niniewskie Stare Ol. were founded, and in 1790 the Kazimierskie Nowe Olędry, situated in the district of Kalisz, in the parish and on the estate of Chocz. For the first of them the foundation charter of 1782 is unknown[^96]. The second received a charter issued on 9 November 1790 by the Rev. Kazimierz Lipski, mitred provost of Chocz, abbot of Lubin. It shows that the locator of this settlement was Georg [Jerzy] Müller. The settlers, who were Germans and Lutherans, received 7 free years (counted from 9 XI 1790), running out in 1797[^97]. This last piece of information shows that the village arose on wooded land, which needed so long a time for clearing and setting up the farms. It seems that the Kazimierskie St. Ol. were founded earlier on land of a similar character, and that their founder too was the said Kazimierz Lipski (Ill. 15), from whose first name both settlements took their names.

Before and in 1783 two Dzierzbin settlements were founded in the district of Kalisz, in the parish of Dzierzbin: the Zdanowiec and the Zamęty Olędry. Their founder was

APP, Pyzdry Gr. 82 (1780), fols. 36-39, the same in: Konin Gr. 118 (1780), fols. 158-159v (obl. 16 X 1780 by the honest Jakub Bończyk, sołtys, and Józef Baranowski, assessor).

[^95]: APP, Pyzdry Gr. 109 (1786), fols. 1077-1077v (the charter named above, with additions of 1786 and undated, entered (*oblata*) in 1784 by Andrzej Markwat, sołtys).

[^96]: The foundation charter for the Kazimierskie St. Ol. of 1782 is mentioned [in:] Breyer 2, p. 412.

[^97]: APP, SAB 650.

[p. 59]

Kazimierz Poraj Biernacki, *wojski* of the district of Ostrzeszów, owner of the estate of Siąszyce, which was set down in one joint collective emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter, issued at Kalisz on 25 June 1783, Dzierzbin and the property of Sul[i]szew. The founder justified their creation by saying that he did so *having so many woods beyond his need*, and at the request of the Olęders. The recipients of this charter were Marcin Szynwald, Jan Fitz and other settlers not named, mostly, if not exclusively, Lutherans.

They were given the woods called Zdanowiec and Zamęty to settle. The first settlers received holdings of one włóka each. The two villages formed separate communes, with separate sołtyses and assessors. The charter stated that: *They are allowed freedom for as many years as they have agreed in their separate contracts, which will be entered in the wilkierz or inventory, making them free of all dues and labour during these free years*[^98] (Ill. 16). From this last piece of information it can be inferred that settlers had settled in this settlement under individual agreements earlier, that is before 1783. Later, further settlers were brought into both villages and farms were divided through inheritance, since rent in the years 1791-1792 and taxes in the years 1789-1795 were paid, or were to be paid, from different numbers of hubas, as is shown by the entries in a village book common to both settlements[^99].

Also before 1783 the Plewińskie (Stare) Olędry were founded in the district of Kalisz, in the parish of Kosmów, and in 1793 the Plewińskie Nowe or Kuźnickie Olędry. Their founder was Antoni Kawiecki, owner of the estates of Kuszyn, Kosmów and Plewnie. The first, also called Stara Huta, whose foundation charter is unknown to us, are recorded in the years 1783-1784, and in 1789 they had 7-8 hearths[^100]. From the foundation charter issued by A. Kawiecki for the Plewińskie Nowe Ol. at the manor of Kuszyn on 1 September 1793 it is known that their locators were the "honest", that is personally free, Bogusław Werner and Paweł Pus, who made the foundation contract in their own name and that of others who *...wish to come and build* with them. 19 settlers settled in this village, 14 of them *newly settled on the Kuźnica by Plewnie* and 5 "*By the Old Hollendry, the New Hollendry*", 14 with German and 5 with Polish names, Protestant Lutherans. The place of the founding was *the Plewnie forest called the Kuźnica, belonging to Plewnie*. The settlers received 4 free years for clearing it and setting up their farms[^101] (Ill. 17).

[p. 63]

In 1783 two settlements were founded by one charter in the district of Kalisz, in the parish of Brudzew and on the estate of Lipe: the Piskory Ol., founded in the Lipe wood on the land of Bi[e]lawy, and Dębniałki, or the Bolimowskie or Brudzewskie Olędry, founded "*on the Brudzew land called Rosocha and Dębianki* [!]", with separate sołtyses and assessors. In the charter, issued at Tymianka on 17 VII 1783, only 3 are named (2 with German names and 1 with a Polish name), Catholics and Lutherans *...who ...have settled and will settle*; they took up the Lipe wood and forest and meadows, *ground long useless*, which needed *rudunek*, and accordingly received 7 free years counted from the time each settler settled; there were, however, more settlers (6 Olęders received free years to 1791 and 2 to 1792). The dissenters were also to receive plots for a burial ground and for a school, common to both settlements. The issuers of the charter and founders of both settlements were Wawrzyniec Antoni Łodzia Czarnecki, *stolnik* of the district of Inowłódz, deputy castle starost of the voivodeship of Łęczyca, owner of the little town of Stryków with the villages of Smolice Górne and Dolne, Lipe, Cesarka, Dobra and Kiełmina in the voivodeship of Łęczyca, district of Brzeziny, and in the voivodeship and district of Kalisz of the villages of Brudzew and Bolimów and of the former little town of Korab, and his wife Ewa née Nowowiejska Czarnecka, owners of the village of Lipe in the latter district and voivodeship[^102] (Ill. 18 and 19). In the second charter, issued by Ewa Czarnecka as a widow on 10 September 1790 at Lipe, the Piskory Ol. received free pasture in the forests and pastures, and the use of the pond at Piskory, in return for each of the 16 farmers ploughing within the year

Wawrzyniec Antoni Łodzia Czarnecki, *stolnik* of Inowłódz, deputy castle starost of Łęczyca (d. 1783), founder, together with his wife Ewa née Nowowiejska, of the villages of Dębniałki Ol. and Piskory Ol. (1783), in the district of Kalisz. Katalog Zabytków Sztuki w Polsce, Vol. V, no. 6, p. 6, fig. 106.

APP, Kalisz. Gr. 482, fols. 1-2v; obl. 7 I 1791.

[p. 65]

10, and in all 150 *laski* (rods) of the manor's ploughland on the Bilawy, *for fallow, spring crop and strip*. This document, which was in fact an agreement between the parties, also states that it concerns the Olęders settled on the Bilawy by the late Wawrzyniec Czarnecki[^103]. This suggests that at least one of these two settlements was founded before 1783, or else that further settlers were brought into this settlement by the same co-founder after 1783. According to the Table of the tax of the offering of 10 and 20 gr: *The Olędry called Piskory, from separate Olęders, whose years of freedom end from 1791 and who will begin to pay in that year;* (...) *in the year 1792 again 11 szczegulni* [individual, separate] *Olęders will end their freedom and begin to pay ...; one Olęder lived in Lipe*[^104].

In 1783 and before 1788 the Oborskie Olędry arose in the district of Kalisz, in the parish of Żegocin, on the noble estate of Obory, in the Obory wilds: the Stare Oborskie Ol., that is Wronów, and the Nowe Oborskie Olędry. Their founders were the owners of the village and demesne farm of Obory, the married couple Jan and Marianna née Rożnowska Szarzyński, treasurer of Latyczów and his wife[^105]. The settlers, whose number and confession the charter does not give, were, as the remains of a separate Protestant cemetery show, only or also Lutherans.

Before and in 1784 the settlements of the Orlińskie (Orlina) Duże and Małe Olędry were created in the district of Konin, on the estate and in the parish of Królików(o). Their founder, and the issuer of the preliminary charter, issued at Królików on 5 May 1784, was the owner of this estate, Count Franciszek Stadnicki, starost of Ostrzeszów, and directly his plenipotentiary Józef Koźmiński, and the locators were Bogusław Krol, sołtys, and Michał Arent. This charter contains the phrase ... *in future all the present and the earlier ones* [settlers] *will have one law*, which justifies the supposition that one of the two settlements, that is the Orlińskie Duże Ol., arose earlier, and the second, the Orlińskie Małe Ol., was founded later, in 1784. The founder also stated in it: *I allow this sołtys free clearing and rudowanie with his co-samsiads* [!] [neighbours], *whose first names and surnames will be written on this contract*. In 1784, 20 settlers settled (most of them with Polish names and some with German), Catholics and Lutherans[^106].

After 1783-1784 and before 1789 the Lisieckie [Ol.], or Lisiec, were founded on the river Powa, in the district of Konin, in the parish of Lisiec Wielki, in the Lisiec key (group of estates) of the starosty of Konin. In 1791 they had only 2 self-tilling hearths, inhabited by dissenters (perhaps Reformed Protestants, attached to the church at Żychlin, as another name of this settlement, the Żychlińskie Ol., would seem to show), and in 1796, 4 houses in all[^107]. Their founder was probably Józef Mycielski, starost of Konin, lieutenant general of the Lithuanian army, voivode of Inowrocław, owner of the estate of Szamotuły.

[^103]: APP, Kalisz. Gr. 482, fols. 3v-4v (obl. 7 I 1791)

[^104]: APP, Tab. 24. Kalisz (Tof. z Todl.), fols. 106-106v.

[^105]: APP, Pyzdry. Gr. 106 (1783), fols. 275-275v; obl. 19 III 1783 (a charter modelled on the charters for other settlements: rent "according to the Łukomskie) or Trąbczyńskie Olędry", in the district of Konin).

[^106]: APP, SAB 732 (an unauthenticated copy).

[^107]: APP. Pyzdry. Gr. 115, fol. 540v; Sirisa, Materiallen 2, p. 401 (4 houses).

[p. 66]

Perhaps as early as about 1777, and certainly in the years 1785-1789, the settlement of Stara Huta-Grójeckie Olędry (today Stara Huta) arose in the same district, in the parish and on the noble estate of Grodziec ("Grojec"). Its founder was probably Count Franciszek Stadnicki, judicial starost of Ostrzeszów, then owner of the estates of Grodziec, Królików and Biskupice. The settlement was founded on empty land overgrown with forest. The inhabitants of this village were Catholics and Poles[^108].

About 1786, and certainly before 1790, the Siąszyckie or Złotkowskie Olędry were founded in the district of Konin, in the parish of Grochowy, on the noble estate of Siąszyce. Their founder was most probably the then owner of the Siąszyce estate, Kazimierz Biernacki, mentioned above, by then styled *stolnik* of Piotrków. According to the *manifestacja* (protest) of his plenipotentiary of 1790, Onufry Gałczyński, a *towarzysz* of the National Cavalry, with a few of his men and 2 privates, attacked in December 1789 the Olęders settled *on the manor land of the village of Siąszyce*, took the cattle of two of them, Łukasz and Bernard, and carried off one Olęder, Łukasz Łyszkowski, to Konin, forcing him to enter military service in the squadron in which he served, although he was a farmer and an immigrant and the new law of the Diet forbade it[^109] (Ill. 20). In 1791 this settlement had 9 "self-tilling" hearths[^110].

In 1787 the Mycielińskie Stare and Nowe Olędry (today Kazala St. and Nw.) were founded in the district of Kalisz, in the parish of Kościelec, on the noble estate of Mycielin, by two charters, in fact settlement agreements, made at Mycielin on the same day, 30 March 1787. Their founder was Maciej Łuczycki, owner of the estate of Mycielin. He made one preliminary agreement with 2 "honest", that is personally free, peasants with German names, who, being unable to write, put crosses under it. The settlers were settled on land partly *wyrudowany* [cleared], partly overgrown with various trees, called Kazalnia, towards the boundary of the village of Korzeniew, and received 6 free years for setting up their farms[^111] (Ill. 21). The second agreement, also a preliminary one, was made between Maciej Łuczycki and 6 Olęders, also "honest" (3 of them with Polish names, 1 with a German one, 1 named only by his first name and 1 whose surname is not given). *They were settled on a part of the ground, that is of the thickets dense with various trees, called Krzewina*, on land called Rusiny, by the boundary of the lands of the village of Słuszków, which needed *rudunek*. They were guaranteed 7 free years. Should there not be enough land for the sixth of these settlers, he was to be settled on another part of the ground called Kazale [Kazalnia] and to clear his holding there. The agreement was signed by the founder and the Olęders, in the case of the latter, who could not write, with crosses[^112].

[^108]: APP. Pyzdry. Gr. 115, fol. 538v: 5 "Stara Huta" + 1 "Kuźnia"; AGAD. General Directorium Südpreussen 745: "Stara Huta Grojec" – 5 houses (p. 406). In 1762: "Stara Huta deserta" (Der., *Materiały do dziejów chłopa*, vol. III, p. 27; ADWł. Wiz. 85: Records of the visitation of the deanery of Konin 1790-1791, call no. 85, fols. 54 ff.

[^109]: APP, Konin. Gr. 122 (1790), fols. 275v-276 (*manifestacja*); see also Kal. Gr. 483 (1791), fols. 108-108v (no. 251) – Siąszyce with the Ol.

[^110]: APP, Pyzdry. Gr. 115, fol. 538v.

[^111]: APP, Kalisz. Gr. 472 (1788), fols. 50-50v, 53 (obl. 22 I 1788).

[^112]: APP, Kalisz. Gr. 472 (1788), fols. 51-51v and 52 (obl. 22 I 1788).

[p. 67]

20\. Information on the Siąszyckie Ol., founded about 1786, in the *manifestacja* (protest) of Leon Bi(e)lski, plenipotentiary of Kazimierz Biernacki, *stolnik* of Piotrków, owner of the estate of Siąszyce, against Onufry Gałczyński, a *towarzysz* of the National Cavalry, of 1790. APP, Konin. Gr. 122 (1790), fols. 275v-276.

[p. 68]

21\. (1) Parts of the first emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the Mycielińskie St. and Nw. – Kazala St. and Nw. Ol., issued by Maciej Łuczycki, owner of the estate of Mycielin, on 30 III 1787. APP, Kalisz. Gr. 472 (1788), fols. 50-50v, 53; obl. 22 I 1788.

[p. 69]

21\. (2) Parts of the first emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the Mycielińskie St. and Nw. – Kazala St. and Nw. Ol., issued by Maciej Łuczycki, owner of the estate of Mycielin, of 30 III 1787. APP, Kalisz. Gr. 472 (1788), fols. 50-50v, 53; obl. 22 I 1788.

[p. 70]

In the years 1787 and 1791 the Stropieszyńskie, or Radzany and Gostynie, Olędry were founded in the district of Kalisz, in the parish of Kościelec. Their founder, and the issuer of the emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charters, was Ignacy Skarbek Kozietulski, treasurer and later *wojski* of the land of Czersk, owner of the village and demesne farm of Stropieszyn. According to the first charter, which he issued on 6 December 1787 at Stropieszyn, the settlers took up "Stropieszyn" land (of the village of Stropieszyn) that was ready, meadows, and overgrown land "under Bugaj and in Radzanów by the ponds", receiving 3 free years for setting up their farms (counted from 11 XI 1787). The village also received land for a school and for a burial ground[^113] (Ill. 22). From this last piece of information it may be inferred that the Olęders were Lutherans and Germans. The second charter, issued on 22 November 1791 at Stropieszyn, mentions an earlier agreement of 27 November 1787, and by it the issuer at the same time confirmed and supplemented the first charter for this village, adding to it also the married couple Józef and Elżbieta Gierecki, servants, settled under a separate charter on ½ huba of ploughland[^114].

Before 1789 the following arose in the district of Konin, in the parish of Zagórów, on the noble estates of Łukom and Łomowo, on the initiative of their owners of the time: the settlements of Bukowo/Bukowe or the Łomowskie Olędry[^115], Imielno Olędry[^116], and a village mixed in its legal constitution, "Myszaków with the Olędry", or more exactly Myszakówko[^117], and Stara Huta with the Olędry, or the Zagórowskie Ol. (today Huta Łukomska)[^118]. The people who settled in them were of various confessions and nations[^119].

In the same district, in the parishes and on the estates of Sławsk and Grodziec, there were founded: before 1789, the Kowalewskie Olędry[^120], and before 1792 the Jaroszewickie Olędry (today Jaroszewice Grodzieckie)[^121]. Their founders were the owners of these estates at the time, Stefan Zielonacki and Count Franciszek Stadnicki, who were also the largest landowners in the district of Konin in the years 1789-1791[^122].

[^113]: APP, Kalisz. Gr. 466, fols. 335-336v (obl. 7 XII 1787 by the honest Michał Gultz, Olęder).

The Olędry alone had, by estimate, 8 hearths in the years 1789-1793; AGAD. General Directorium Südpreussen 745: "Bukowe H." – 9 houses (p. 397).

APP, Księgi ziemiańskie [Landowners' court books], Konin. Zm. 1, fols. 321-322; AGAD. General Directorium Südpreussen 745: "Jaroszewice H." – 11 houses (p. 399).

[p. 71]

22\. (1) One of the two first emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charters for the Stropieszyńskie-Radzany and Gostynie Ol., issued on 6 1787 by Ignacy Skarbek Kozietulski, treasurer of the land of Czersk, owner of the village and demesne farm of Stropieszyn. APP, Kalisz Gr. 466 (1787), fols. 335-336v; obl. 7 XII 1787 by the honest Michał Gultz, Olęder.

[p. 72]

22\. (2) One of the two first emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charters for the Stropieszyńskie-Radzany and Gostynie Ol., issued on 6 1787 by Ignacy Skarbek Kozietulski, treasurer of the land of Czersk, owner of the village and demesne farm of Stropieszyn. APP, Kalisz Gr. 466 (1787), fols. 335-336v; obl. 7 XII 1787 by the honest Michał Gultz, Olęder.

[p. 73]

In 1789 the Ciążeńskie or Zarzeczne Olędry were created in the district of Pyzdry, in the parish of Ciążeń, in the Ciążeń key of the mensal estates of the bishopric of Poznań. According to the revision of this key of 1793, 4 Olęders lived there: 3 with Polish names and 1 with a German name, Catholics; they were founded by a charter of 25 June 1789, not yet approved by the chapter, of Antoni Onufry Okęcki, ordinary bishop of Poznań[^123] (Ill. 23-24).

Before 1789 the Olęder settlement of Poroże Małe arose in the district of Kalisz, in the parish and on the estate of Przespolew Kościelny. It was a small settlement, with only 2 hearths in the years 1789-1790[^124], probably tied administratively to the Przespolewskie Ol., later, it seems, enlarged, and inhabited by Lutheran Olęders, as is shown, indirectly, by the survival of a Protestant cemetery in it.

23\. Part of the revision of the Ciążeń key of the mensal estates of the bishops of Poznań of 1793, with information on Bishop Antoni O. Okęcki's foundation charter for the Ciążeńskie or Zarzeczne Ol. of 1789 and with a list of their settlers by name. AAP. CP 152 fols. 23-24v.

24\. Antoni Onufry Okęcki (1729-1793), bishop of Poznań (1780-1793), founder of the Ciążeńskie Ol. in 1789.

[^123]: Archdiocesan Archive in Poznań. Records of the Chapter. CP 152 fols. 23v-24. See also: APP. Księgi ziemiańskie. Pyzdry. Zm. 1, fols. 802-811v and 830-836 (Inventory of the estates of the Ciążeń key of the bishopric of Poznań of 1791); W. Sobisiak, *Rozwój latyfundium biskupstwa poznańskiego od XVI do XVIII wieku* [The development of the latifundium of the bishopric of Poznań from the sixteenth to the eighteenth century], Poznań 1960, p. 39 (where the foundation charter is wrongly dated 25 IV 1789).

[^124]: Tab. 22. Kalisz (detailed table of the hearths and population of the district of Kalisz of 1789): 2 hearths (fol. 130), Tab. 25. Kalisz (Tof. z Todl. of 1789): "Nowe Kolonie. Poroże Małe" – 2 hearths (fol. 62); Table of hearths and of the *delata* (return) of the hearth tax of 1790 – Kalisz. Gr. 481: "Poroże Małe kolonia..." – 2 hearths (fol. 228), Table of the *delata* from the voivodeship and districts of Kalisz, Konin... of the hearth tax, half hearth tax... for the March instalment of the year 1791 – Kal. Gr. 483: 2 hearths (fol. 107).

[p. 74]

In 1790 the settlement of Świdle Nowa Kolonia, or the Świdle Olędry, was founded in the district of Kalisz, in the parish and on the noble estate of Przespolew(o). Its founder was Ignacy Zaremba, son of the starost of Prośniatów, civil and military commissary for order of Kalisz, holder of this estate. According to the table of the grain levy from the self-tilling hearths of the district of Kalisz of 1791, the Świdle Ol. were "*newly set up in the year 1790*", and they then had 3 "self-tilling" hearths[^125].

Summing up and supplementing the detailed information given above on the distribution, time, causes, conditions and organisation of the founding of the 58 Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest in the years 1746-1793, the following more general conclusions can be drawn, which, to make them easier to read, we set out in several points. 1) Of the 58 Olęder settlements founded in the years 1746-1793 in the Pyzdry Forest in 3 districts of the voivodeship of Kalisz, in 21 parishes (4 of which lay on the border of two districts), 5 were founded in the district of Pyzdry, in 4 parishes: Ciążeń (1), Pyzdry (1), Szymanowice (2) and Zagórów (2); 28 in the district of Konin, in 8 parishes: Dzierzbin, whose centre lay in the district of Kalisz (6), Grochowy (1), Grodziec (5), Królików (2), partly lying in both of the last two parishes (4), Lisiec Wielki (1), Trąbczyn (3), Sławsk (1), Zagórów (5); and 25 in the district of Kalisz, in 10 parishes: Brudzew (1), Chocz (2), Dzierzbin (2), Kościelec (5), Kosmów (5), Lipe (2), Przespolew Kościelny (3), Rzegocin (2), Szymanowice (2), Zbiersk (1). Of these 58 Olęder settlements, 7 were founded on royal estates (1 in the starosty of Pyzdry and 2 in the starosty of Pietrzyków in the district of Pyzdry, 1 in the starosty and district of Konin, 2 in the lease of Ceków in the district of Kalisz, 1 on the estates of the royal town of Pyzdry), 3 on church estates (1 on secular church estates, in the Ciążeń key of the latifundium of the bishopric of Poznań, in the district of Pyzdry, and 2 on the monastic estates of the convent of the Order of St Clare, the Franciscan nuns, in Kalisz, in the district of Konin), and the majority, 48 settlements, on noble estates (25 of them in the district of Konin and 23 in the district of Kalisz), mostly on such estates as Gadów, Grodziec-Królików and Trąbczyn, Łukom and Łomowo in the district of Konin, and Dzierzbin, Chocz, Kosmów and Lipe in the district of Kalisz. Of these, two villages (the Kazimierskie Stare and Nowe Olędry) in the parish and on the estate of Chocz, which formed the endowment of a noble foundation of the chapter of the collegiate church of Chocz (created in 1620 on the condition that it be presided over by a clergyman of the Lipski family, with the dignity of a mitred prelate), are sometimes classed in the sources as villages that were church property[^126]2) The date of origin of more than half (31) of the 58 Olęder settlements that arose in the Pyzdry Forest in the eighteenth century is fixed exactly by their foundation charters; for the rest it can be established exactly or approximately from the first mentions in other kinds of sources, such as inspections, inventories, court decrees and tax tables. The chronology of their founding, certain and hypothetical, falls within the periodisation we have adopted for the history of Olęder settlement

[^125]: APP. Pyzdry. Gr. 115, fol. 533v.

[^126]: Ibid.

[p. 75]

in Poland and Greater Poland[^127], within their second phase (1660-1793/1795) and third period (1721-1793/95). Of these 58 settlements, 3 arose in the first sub-period (1721-1750), 11 in the second (1751-1772) and 44 in the third (1773-1793) of this period. This order in time of the founding of the Olęder settlements is not wholly clear-cut, however, since some of them were re-founded or had further settlers brought in during different sub-periods.

3) The cause of the founding of the settlements for the feudal founders was the wish to increase their income by settling and bringing into use the reserves of land they held, especially woodland not settled until then, or settled but long deserted, depopulated and overgrown again with forest, and, exceptionally, land that was ready, already tilled. The settling of Olęders, who answered jointly, in accordance with one of the basic principles of Olęder law, for paying money rents to the lords, assured these of a steady and regular income. This is also shown, indirectly, by the founding of some Olęder settlements on usurped land, that is land that was the subject of lawsuits between the owners of neighbouring estates. For the Olęders the motive for settling were favourable legal and economic conditions of life, far better than in the country and the place of their origin or their earlier residence in Poland, and the assurance of religious toleration and freedom of worship, guaranteed to the settlers in the foundation charters in accordance with the principles of Olęder law, which will be discussed in detail below. The foundation charters for the settlements in the Pyzdry Forest (apart from five, for Wielołęka, the Białobłockie, Konarskie, Grądy St. and Nw. and Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol.) do not speak directly of these mutual economic advantages, since they were, so to speak, obvious.

4\. The character of the land on which Olęder settlements were founded in the Pyzdry Forest in the years 1746-1793 varied. Most of them were founded on wooded land or on land that had become overgrown again. In the light of the source information given above, at least 37 of the 58 settlements were founded on woodland. Of these, at least 27 Olęder villages were founded on raw root, 5 were founded on the site of settlements or parts of settlements that had existed earlier under the same names, even since the Middle Ages, and had been deserted and overgrown with forest at the end of the sixteenth and the beginning of the seventeenth century, and 5 on land partly tilled and ready, 1 of them (the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol.) also on the lands of a demesne farm. In the light of the source information on the character of the land where the settlements were founded, and of the data of modern soil maps, it can be said that of the 58 settlements, 27 were founded on podzolic soils (Cekowskie Nw. Ol., Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol., Grądy St. and Nw., Gliny Ol., Grodziec(-kie) Ol., Imielno, Jaroszewickie Ol., Kosmowskie Ol., Kowalewskie Ol., Kuszyńskie Ol., Lisieckie Ol., Myszakówko Ol., Oborskie St. and Nw. Ol., Poroże M. Ol., Przespolewskie Ol., Siąszyckie Ol., Smuszewskie Ol., Świdle Ol., Trąbczyńskie St. and Nw. Ol., Wielołęka, Wierzchy Ol., Wrąbczynkowskie Ol., Dzierzbińskie – Zdanowiec Ol.), 4 on black

[^127]: Z. Chodyła, *Zarys dziejów osadnictwa olęderskiego w Polsce 1547-1864*, [in:] *Olędry-przestrzenie obok nas*..., pp. 35, 53-54; the same, *Osadnictwo olęderskie w Wielkopolsce (1597–1793).*

[p. 76]

earths (Jarantowskie Ol., Plewińskie St. and Nw. Ol. and Zbierskie Ol.), 6 on marsh soils (Dzierzbińskie-Zamęty Ol., Mycielińskie St. and Nw. Ol., Stropieszyńskie – Gostynie and Radzany Ol., Stara Huta-Grojeckie Ol.), 13 on muck soils (Bukowa Ol., Borowieckie St. and Nw. Ol., Cekowskie St. Ol., Dębniałki Ol., Konary, Lubieńskie, Luśnie, Łazy, Orlińskie M. and D. Ol., Piskory Ol., Stara Huta-Zagórowskie Ol.), 4 on river alluvia (Ciążeńskie Ol., Lisewskie Ol., Białobłockie Ol. and Ciemierowa/Szymanowickie St. Ol.), and 4 on mixed soils, 1 of them (Pyzdrskie Ol.) on podzolic soils, muck soils, river alluvia and dune sands, and 3 on podzolic-muck soils (Wrąbczynek and the Kazimierskie St. and Nw. Ol.). Of these soils, the black earths, on which only 4 settlements arose, are counted as fertile; the marsh, muck and alluvial soils, on which 24 Olęder villages were founded wholly and 4 in part, as moderately fertile; and podzols and dune sands, on which 28 settlements were founded wholly and 4 in part, as infertile[^128]. So half of the Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest were founded on fertile and moderately fertile soils, half on infertile soils, and 4 on mixed soils, though with infertile soils predominating.

5) The Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest were founded by the founders, the owners or holders of the landed estates, in agreement with the locators, the representatives, and perhaps also the recruiters, of the settlers, acting on behalf of the whole future commune, or with whole communes; they often issued to them first individual or collective but preliminary charters (which were in fact agreements between the two parties), and only later (for instance once the necessary number of settlers had gathered) collective and final ones, which settled the Olęder character of the village. On the royal estates they were founded by their tenants (lessees) of the time, on the church estates by the bishop and by the superior and other members of the governing body of the convent of the Franciscan nuns in Kalisz, on the town estates by the magistrate of Pyzdry, and on the noble estates chiefly by the rich and middling nobility. The co-founders or founders of the largest number on a single noble property, that of Grodziec-Królików, no fewer than 8, were its successive owners, Józef Wężyk and Count Franciszek Stadnicki. On the few other properties at most 3-2 were founded, or only 1. Most of them were founded on so-called raw root, that is on places not settled before, in forests or woods, and in a few cases also on the sites of old villages dating back to the Middle Ages but deserted, that is overgrown again with forest, which is also shown by the long free years, 7, meant for clearing, setting up and building the settlements. These settlements were settled by people often described in the foundation charters as "honest" (*uczciwi*), that is personally free (at least once they had crossed the Polish border). They also had to have – as we shall show below – cash (needed to pay the entry money for the holdings of land they received, which guaranteed them the acquisition of the perpetual rent tenure of the land) and the basic live and dead stock (tools), which allowed them

[^128]: Cf. Z. Chodyła, *Osadnictwo olęderskie w Wielkopolsce (1597-1793)*, map 10.

[p. 77]

to take up, build on and bring into use the holdings of land they received. The settlers undoubtedly took part in founding the settlements, which, as the text of many foundation charters shows, also took place at their request. In the founding of a few settlements one may guess at the presence of a locator or locators (*osadźca*, *zasadźca*), founding entrepreneurs acting on the authority of the whole commune, but for the most part whole groups (communities) of settlers acted in this capacity. The settlers of most settlements were Protestants by confession (chiefly Lutherans), some of them Catholics, and by nation Germans and Poles, who in some settlements also settled together. The settlements were founded on the strength of charters, which had to be paid for. The process of founding the Olęder settlements lasted at least through the free years (that is mostly several years), but often, as the information quoted above shows, even longer, probably mainly because of the difficulty of finding enough settlers and their coming and going. That is why settlers sometimes received individual contracts, sometimes collective but preliminary ones, replaced only in time, after the free years of all the settlers had run out, by collective, final, emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charters guaranteeing the perpetual rent tenure of the farms they held. The way, that is the technique, of founding the settlements, by clearing and/or by draining, which consisted in digging or deepening drainage ditches, depended on the character of the land taken up. Both sides bore the costs of the founding. The manor not only gave land for founding the settlements but probably organised part of the transport of the colonists, granted them building timber free of charge (allowing them to sell what was left over after the buildings were put up, in order to raise cash), and sometimes helped financially with putting up the buildings, which we discuss in detail below.

An important cultural aspect of Olęder settlement in the area of the Pyzdry Forest was the toponymy of the settlements. Of the 58 villages, most had, beside their name proper, the additional element *(H)olędry* or *(H)olendry*, used to mark the legal and economic character and the spatial layout of the place (like the terms *folwark*, *kolonia* and so on). As a component of the name of a settlement, this element served at the same time to identify it and to distinguish it[^129].

Most of the settlements, founded sometimes on raw root, but mostly near old mother settlements, centres and estates and on their lands, received names that were:

1) possessive, that is, such as: *Lisewskie, Pyzdrskie, Wrąbczynkowskie, Gadowskie St. and Nowe; Grodzieckie/Grójeckie, Jaroszewickie, Kowalewskie, Lisieckie, Siąszyckie or Złotkowskie, Smuszewskie, Trąbczyńskie Stare and Nowe; Ciemierowskie/Szymanowickie Stare, Cekowskie Stare and Nowe, Jarantowskie, Oborskie Nowe, Plewińskie Stare and Nowe, Zbierskie – Olędry.*

2) topographical, derived from local names: of woods, thickets, forests and wilds, such as: *Borowiec/Borowieckie Stare and Nowe, Dębniałki, (Dzierzbińskie)*

[^129]: W. Rusiński, *Osady tzw. „olędrów”*, p. 20.

[p. 78]

*– Zdanowiec and Zamęty, Grądy, Luśnie, Orlińskie (Orlina) Duże and Małe, Piskory, Poroże Małe, Wierzchy – Olędry; of marshes and bogs, such as: Białebłota Olędry;* names of industrial sites, such as: *Stara Huta Grójeckie and Stara Huta Zagórowskie – (Olędry)*; 3) characteristic, formed independently of the naming of old settlements, that is, among others, personal names, derived from the first names and surnames of the founders and members of their families, such as: *Kazimierskie (Stare and Nowe) Olędry.*

There were also combinations of two types of name, namely possessive and topographical, such as: *Ciążeńskie* or *Zarzeczne, (Kosmowskie)* or *Szadokierskie (Szadykierz), Kuszyńskie* or *Jaszczurowo, Lisewskie* or *Olsz/Olszowiec, Obory Stare* or *Wronów, Przespolewskie-Przespolewa (Pańskiego) and Poroża Wielkiego, Mycielińskie* or *Kazala Stara and Nowa, Stropieszyńskie* or *Radzany and Gostynie – Olędry.*

Some settlements, on the other hand, bore names inherited from settlements long deserted (overgrown with forest and bushes), or from settlements that had changed their legal situation, that is had been turned from labour-service villages into Olęder ones.

These were names that were:

1\. topographical, such as: *Bukowa/-e, Gliny, Imielno, Konary, Łazy, Lubień, Świdle Nowe, Wielołęka, Wrąbczynek*

2\. possessive, such as: *Myszakówek Olędry (written with or without the element Olędry).*

The names of these settlements were also often written in both forms, interchangeably, as *Konary* or *Konarskie*, *Lubień* or *Lubieńskie – Olędry.*

Because of the changes in the meaning and function of the term *(H)olędry*, the names of Olęder settlements containing this element, but not only these, were transformed in the nineteenth and twentieth centuries. This happened above all by dropping the element *Olędry (Olendry/Holendry)*. It also happened that in the names of Olęder settlements the distinguishing elements *Kolonia* or *Pustkowie* were used in place of *(H)olędry*, *(H)olendry* (in such forms as *Świdle Nowa Kolonia*, *Pyzdrskie Pustkowie* for the *Pyzdrskie Ol.*), applied to the Olęder villages because of their scattered spatial form and their small size. Several other factors also affected the transformation of the names of Olęder settlements, namely: 1) the persistence of the earlier tendency in the evolution of naming, connected with the settlers' own interchangeable use of the names of settlements, and with their changing forms in official records and lists of places (soon after their founding); 2) the parcelling and consolidation of land as a result of the regulation and the enfranchisement reforms, which brought back names used earlier, which in the case of the *Pyzdrskie Ol.* meant that one collective name was replaced by nine names of scattered settlements that had probably been moved to Olęder law together (*Baraniec, Glinianki, Dolne and Górne Grądy, Królewiny, Kruszyny, Tłoczyzna, Trzcianki, Zapowiednia*); 3) decisions of the administrative authorities at various levels, following a tendency to shorten and simplify the written forms of place names, as a result of which the adjectival elements of the names of Olęder settlements were changed into noun forms, for example *Borowieckie St. and Nowe Ol.* into *Borowiec Stary and Nowy*.

As a result of the structural and linguistic transformations of most of the old names of Olęder settlements with the element *(H)olędry*, *(H)olendry*, caused by the factors mentioned

[p. 79]

and carried out at various times in the various ways described above, their historical and cultural origin has been blurred. Only 26 (3.8%) names of Old Polish Olęder settlements with this element have survived to this day in the rural settlement landscape of Greater Poland proper, within its historical borders. In the area of the Pyzdry Forest this element has survived to this day only in the names of five such settlements, namely: Ciążeńskie Holendrów, Gadowskie Holendry, Wrąbczynkowskie Holendry, Smaszewskie Olendry and Holendry Jaroszewickie.

[p. 80]

# 2. The area and layout of the Olęder settlements, their size and types by the number and structure of hearths, and their social and functional-economic organisation

The area of the Olęder settlements that arose in the years 1746-1793 in the Pyzdry Forest, at the time of their founding, is not exactly known for all 58. For the greater part of them (37) it can be fixed approximately from the foundation charters and other records giving the number and/or size of the holdings of land (farms) received or held by the settlers not long after the founding. For the remaining settlements one can try to fix it only probably, from the information on the number and character of the hearths in general and of the farming hearths (farms) in these settlements in the years 1789 and 1791. Multiplying these numbers by hypothetical holdings of one huba or half a huba makes it possible to fix the lower and upper limits of the area of their arable land.

In the sources on the Olęder settlements in Greater Poland the area of the holdings of land assigned to the settlers was mostly given in włóki (łany), mostly called hubas (from German *Hufe*), or more rarely *ślady*. The włóka, as a unit of measure of arable land, was the basis for fixing the amount of rents (in money and in kind) and of labour services. It was identified with the area of a full, economically independent farm. Hubas (włóki, łany), however, varied, and their area also depended on the measure by which they were laid out. Usually this was the Chełmno włóka (earlier also called the Flemish or the Środa łan), used in four variants of different area. This włóka was divided into 30 morgen, the morgen = 300 (30x10) square rods. The rod, also called in the sources *laska* or *rózga*, often varied in size, however; it ranged from 7½ ells (the rod of the new or Warsaw measure of 1764) to 9 ells (the margrave's rod). The sources mention ells of the Chełmno, Polish (old), German and Poznań measure, but do not define these measures more closely. Besides, the ell itself, used within the same measure of area, such as the various kinds of włóka, also often varied in size. The constitution of the Diet of 1764 unifying measures fixed the Warsaw ell, as a measure of length, at 24 inches (59.55 cm). A włóka of 30 morgen, each of 300 square rods measured with this ell, would equal 17.95 ha, that is 179,550 m² After 1764, in spite of this unification, ploughland was still mostly measured with the Chełmno rod, which moreover varied, most often of 8 and 9 ells, most rarely of 7½ ells. The Chełmno ell,

[p. 81]

probably the same as the old Polish or the German ell named in the sources, was somewhat shorter than the Crown ell of the new measure of 1764 and came to 57.6 cm. The area of a włóka measured with a rod of 7½ ells comes to 16.80 (more exactly 16.7962) ha, with one of 8 ells to 19.20, with one of 8½ ells to 21.60, and with one of 9 ells to as much as 24.20 ha. Exceptionally, measures of area other (at least in name) than the Chełmno włóka were used in Olęder settlements. One Warsaw ell as a measure of area, in turn, came to 0.354666 m². Land was sometimes measured in *ślady* identical with the Chełmno włóka (huba), but also sometimes equal to almost or exactly two such włóki (that is, of 50–60 Chełmno morgen). In isolated cases the sources mention land being measured with the Magdeburg rod, but there is no information on how many ells it contained. In the second half of the eighteenth century, in the districts of Kalisz, Konin and Pyzdry, land was often measured in the "rydlandska" measure, that is probably the Rhenish (the name probably comes from the word Reihnland), with a rod of 7½ ells. The Rhenish rod, from 1816 also called the Magdeburg rod (and so probably the same as the one mentioned earlier) or the Prussian rod (equal to about 3.766 m), was the measure generally in force (by a royal order of 1750) and the most widely used in Prussia, and from 1775 it was in force in all the provinces of the country except Silesia and the Polish lands newly annexed in the First Partition. In settlements founded with settlers coming from Silesia, especially up to 1740, holdings were also measured, at the settlers' wish, with the Imperial rod, probably equal to 8½ ells[^130].

Here is a survey of the Olęder settlements founded in the Pyzdry Forest by their area, especially their arable land, in the measures given in the sources, by their district and in alphabetical order.

In the district of Pyzdry, then, the area can be grasped for only 3 of the 5 Olęder settlements.

In the Ciążeńskie or Zarzeczne Ol., in the Ciążeń key of the estates of the bishopric of Poznań, 4 settlers received 3 włóki in all, probably of the Chełmno measure, so that on average each of them would have had a farm of 22½ morgen[^131].

In the royal-town Pyzdrskie Olędry, 19 settlers settled "on the forests of the town of Pyzdry" held holdings of 1 huba each, probably of the Crown measure, that is measured with a rod of 7½ ells (1 rod = 4 m 46 cm) by a surveyor brought in at their cost[^132]. So the area of the whole village was at least 19 włóki.

In the two settlements in the starosty of Pietrzyków, Wrąbczynek and the Wrąbczynowskie Olędry, the settlers received in 1781 more than 21½ włóki of arable land and meadows in all. In the first settlement they obtained 11 hubas [1 huba = 30 morgen] and 16 morgen of land *wyrudowany*, that is cleared for meadows, and one of them one and a half morgen; in the second settlement 9½ hubas. Besides, in Wrąbczynek, where already earlier, in connection with the compulsory communal three-field system of tillage,

A handbook for archival studies]. 2nd ed., revised and enlarged... by K. Tyszkowski and B. Włodarski, Lwów–Warsaw 1926, pp. 218–219; cf. also K. Górska–Gołaska, *Pomiary gruntowe w Wielkopolsce* [Land surveys in Greater Poland], Wrocław–Warsaw-Kraków 1965, p. 77; Z. Chodyła, *Osadnictwo olęderskie w Wielkopolsce (1597–1793)*. DuninWąsowicz, Stam

[^131]: Cf. n. 123 (Revision of the Ciążeń key of 1793).

[^132]: Cf. n. 91 (foundation charter of 24 IV 1778).

[p. 82]

the land was divided into three fields (spring crop, winter crop and fallow), the sołtys also received for his labours for the commune an *ostrów* [allotment] *of five morgen in each field*. The innkeepers in both settlements also obtained half a huba each free of rent, and land of an area not more closely stated was also assigned for the school (the teacher) common to both villages and for the cemetery[^133]. According to the survey of the crown estates of the district of Pyzdry of 1789, there were 14 farmers in Wrąbczynek with 11 hubas, and in the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol. 17 farmers with 9½ hubas of ploughland and 17½ morgen of meadows; there was also a little school there, newly built[^134], on the holding mentioned, assigned to the teacher.

Of the 28 Olęder settlements in the district of Konin, by contrast, the total area can be established from written sources for only 16.

In the Białobłockie or Królikowskie Olędry the holdings, of a number not exactly known, were probably of at least half a włóka. According to the foundation charter of 1787, ½ huba was assigned for a school common to the communities of Orlina and Białobłoty[^135]. In 1791 there were probably about 26 farms here[^136].

In the Borowieckie (Stare) Olędry, according to the inventory of the properties of Grodziec and Królików of 1779, 27 farmers lived on holdings of ½ (15), ¾ (8) and 1 (4) huba, with stated buildings, which allows the area of the whole village (with the school land) to be put at 18 hubas[^137]. The charter of 1785, common to the Konarskie and also to the Borowieckie – not only the Stare but also the Nowe – Olędry, did not state the number and area of the farms then existing and created there. It granted *to the whole community of the Borowieckie Hollendry, settled long ago, which has not yet paid the gruntgielt [entry money], and which it is reserved and left to set and receive at a suitable time" – ½ włóka for a school for both communities, of Borowiec and of Konary... gratis* [free of charge][^138]. In 1789 there were probably as many farms in both settlements as hearths in general, that is 21, of which about 10, it seems, in the Borowieckie Nowe Ol.; in 1791, 24 farming hearths were recorded in both villages, and later, in 1796, as many as 60 houses in all[^139].

In the Gadowskie Nw. and St. Olędry the dissenters received for a school and a cemetery, according to the foundation charter of 1784, 1/4 huba, that is 7½ morgen, free of rent. The settlers' holdings were of 1 or ½ włóka (the włóka, otherwise the huba, equalled 30 morgen, the morgen 30x10 rods measured with the Polish rod of 8 ells). The sołtys received in addition half a quarter of a huba free of dues. The charter also stated that... *large ditches are not to be counted in the measure of the włóki, but small ditches running from the włóki are to come into the reckoning or measure.* The settlers were to pay for their holdings a grungielt (entry money) not exactly stated – *the customary one among the Holendry.* The farmer

[^133]: Cf. n. 82 (emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter of 20 X 1781).

[^134]: AGAD. ASK XLVI, 116, p. 22 (survey of the starosty of Pietrzyków of 1 IX 1789).

[^135]: Cf. n. 65 (foundation charter of 17 X 1787).

[^136]: APP. Pyzdry. Gr. 115, fol. 540: "Otnińscy [otherwise Orlińscy] i Białegłowy" [*recte* Białobłoty], that is the Białobłockie Ol. together with the Orlińskie Ol. – 49 self-tilling hearths in 1791. Since in the latter there were, according to the foundation charter of 1784, 20 farmers, their number in the Białobłockie Ol. can be estimated at about 26.

[^137]: Cf. n. 69.

[^138]: Cf. n. 61.

[^139]: APP, Tab. 21. Kalisz (Table of the hearth tax of the districts of the voivodeship of Kalisz of 1789), fol. 11v: "Olędry zwane Borowiec i Huta" [the Olędry called Borowiec and Huta] – – 21 hearths (fol. 11v); Pyzdry. Gr. 115, fol. 540: "Borowieccy" – 31 "self-tilling" hearths. AGAD. Gen. Direkt. Südpreussen I/1745, p. 396: "Borowiec H." – 60 hearths (p. 396).

[p. 83]

who kept the tap (that is, sold the lord's drink) received ½ huba of land.[^140]. In 1791 there were probably at least as many farms in the Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol. as farming hearths, that is 22[^141], but it is not known how many of them were of ½ and how many of 1 huba.

In the Grądy St. and Nw. Ol., according to the charter of 1791, the Olęders probably had holdings of 1 huba. This seems to be suggested by the 7 free years meant for clearing the forest. Each huba, that is włóka, was to have 30 morgen measured with the Crown rod. Half a huba was received by *the Grądy community* (that is, both settlements) *for a little school gratys* [free of charge, without payment] *and as much for the innkeeper without payment. They have already paid the entry money for them*[^142]. According to the tariff of the grain levy of 1791 they had 18 self-tilling hearths, that is farms[^143].

In the Grójeckie/Grodzieckie Ol., or the Ol. in Grodziec (later called Janowo?), according to the inspection of the estates of Grodziec and Królików with their appurtenances of 1771, the settlers were settled on 10 hubas[^144].

In the Jaroszewickie/Jaroszewskie Olędry, according to a decree of the landowners' court of 1792, there were 9 farms of an area not more closely stated[^145].

In Konary, or the Konarskie Olędry, the number of włóki was not stated in the second charter of re-founding and enlargement of 1785. It is known only that the settlers received holdings of one huba in the charter, 1 huba to have 30 morgen measured with the Crown rod. The settlement also received 1/2 włóka for a school/teacher together with the Borowieckie Ol.[^146]. In 1791 Konary had 19 "self-tilling" hearths[^147], that is farms.

In the church (monastic) Lubieńskie Ol., founded in 1776 by two collective charters of 10 November 1777, the holdings assigned earlier under individual contracts were probably of one huba and paid for by entry money. The huba was to measure in length *16 rods, that is surveyor's laski of entering feet* [?], *in breadth 45 such rods*. The village also received ½ huba for an inn[^148].

In the Luśnie (Lusina) or Łukomskie Olędry, understood at first as a part of the Trąbczyńskie St. Ol., there were 12 Olęders with a shepherd in 1771; eleven of them held holdings of a size not more closely stated[^149]. There were as many of them according to the decree of the boundary commission court of 1776 on fixing the boundaries between the estates of Trąbczyn and Łukom[^150]. In 1789 the Luśnie Ol. had 10 hearths, and so probably as many farms in all[^151].

[p. 84]

In the Orlińskie Małe and Duże Olędry, according to the foundation charter of 1784, 20 settler farmers were settled, 18 of whom had a whole huba each and 2 only ½ huba each, which allows the area of the village at that time to be put at 19 hubas. They received for clearing in the forest *the hubas beyond Otnie in the place by the name of Orla* and the Królików woods called *Orlina*[^152]. In 1791, together with the Białobłockie Ol., they had 49 *self-tilling* hearths[^153], that is farms.

In the Trąbczyńskie Stare Olędry, according to the inspection of the Trąbczyn estate with the villages of Osiny, Łazy, Nowa Wieś, Olędry and Huta of 1754, made at the request of Katarzyna Prusimska, there were: – *15 Olęders, the sixteenth huba empty; some have built well, others* [!] *have only huts in the ground*. From this information it may be supposed that the village had 16 hubas in all, one of them not settled. In 1789 the Trąbczyńskie – Luźne Stare and Nowe Ol. had 10 hearths in all, and in 1791, after the founding of the latter had been completed, 31 farming hearths[^154].

In Wielołęka, according to the inventory of the properties of Grodziec, Królików and others of 1779 already named, there lived 15 farmers, an innkeeper and a teacher, partly still clearing their holdings: of an area not stated (2), on parts of a huba (5), on ¼ huba (3), on ½ huba (4) and on 1 huba (1), with stated buildings, which allows the area of the whole village to be estimated at about 6 hubas (włóki)[^155] (after the re-founding and enlargement of this village by the charter of 1791[^156]). The area of the village undoubtedly grew too, since in 1789 it had only 13 hearths in all, and in 1791 there were already 24 *self-tilling* hearths[^157], that is farms.

Finally, of the 25 Olęder settlements in the district of Kalisz, the total area is known from the foundation charters, or from inventories and inspections, for only 18.

In the royal Cekowskie Stare Ol., founded in 1768, the farmers received farms of 19 morgen, and 15 morgen were assigned to the school besides[^158]. With 5 farmers, according to the inventory of 1774, the area of the whole village could thus be put at 110 morgen[^159]. In 1791 the Cekowskie St. Ol. had 9 *self-tilling* hearths[^160], that is farms.

In the Cekowskie Nowe Olędry, or Prażuchy, also royal, founded in the years 1774 and 1780, the farmers' holdings differed. The settlers, 12 in number, took into hereditary possession in the years 1774 – 1787 respectively 1 huba, 1 h. and 9 morgen, 1/2 h. and 5 morgen, 1 h., 1½ h., ¾ h., 1¼ h., 1 h., 3/4 h., ¾ h., 1 h. and 2 h., paying 7½ złoty of entry money, grungielt, per huba. The whole village also obtained ½ h.

In 1796, according to AGAD. Gen. Direckt. Südpreussen I/1745, p. 406, they had 34 or 36 hearths ("Trąbczyn H." 34 + "Stara Huta Trąbczyn" 2).

[p. 85]

for an inn and 3 morgen for the teacher (school), free of rent[^161]. The total area of this settlement can thus be put at more than 14 hubas. In 1791 the Cekowskie Nw. Ol. had 22 self-tilling hearths[^162], that is farms.

In the noble Dębniałki and Piskory Olędry the settlers "*... who ... have settled and will settle*" received holdings of 1 and ½ huba *according to the settlers' wish* (h. = 300 rods), and in the second charter *free pasture in the forests and pastures, and the use of the pond at Piskory*"[^163]. In the Table of the tax of the offering of 10 or 20 grosz of 1789 the Piskory Olędry were entered in two ways. First as those which, from *separate* Olęders, *whose years of freedom end from 1791 and who will begin to pay in that year*. Then with the information that (...) *in the year 1792 again 11 szczególni [individual] Olęders will end their freedom and begin to pay...* This source also records that one Olęder (probably belonging to the Piskory Ol.) lived in the village of Lipe[^164]. In 1791 there were 9 *self-tilling* hearths, that is farms, in the Dębniałki Ol., and 16 in the Piskory Ol.[^165].

In the noble Dzierzbińskie, or Zdanowiec and Zamęty, Olędry, also founded in 1783, the settlers first received under separate contracts holdings of one włóka (the włóka equalled 30 morgen, the morgen 30x10 rods measured with the rod of 8 ells), *into which włóka the buildings, meadows and gardens are to be included* [counted], and also the small ditches, but not the large ditches. 7½ morgen were granted for the *bakałarze* (teachers), free of rent and labour, both on the Zdanowiec and on the Zamęty; 15 morgen in each for the innkeepers, which the Olęders were *to win at their own cost* [that is, to clear and build on]; and for a common cemetery for the dissenters [Protestants] a plot of 14 x 14 rods[^166]. With 37 *self-tilling* hearths in 1791[^167], the combined area of the two villages can be estimated at as many włóki, and with the farms of the innkeepers, the teacher and the cemetery at about 39 włóki.

In the Kosmowskie Ol. the holdings were most probably of one huba. The village also received in the foundation charter of 1775 ½ huba each for the school and the innkeeper. In 1784 2 more settlers obtained a foundation charter to build on half-hubas in Kosmów under Olęder law, *like the other Kosmowskie Olędry*[^168]. With 16 hearths in 1789 the area of this settlement could be estimated at a maximum of 15 hubas (włóki), and with the two farms in Kosmów at 16[^169].

In the Mycielińskie Stare and Nowe Olędry, 2 settlers obtained in the first charter of 30 March 1787 holdings of one huba, not counting the part of the land taken for the road and 1 morgen meant for the buildings; the huba
