was measured with the rod of 8 ells. In the second foundation charter of the same day, month and year, issued for 6 settlers, who probably also received holdings, though probably not of one but of half a huba, there is the founder's reservation: *...having on the manor land of the said village* [Mycielin] *a part of the ground, that is thickets, dense with various trees, called krzewina* (...)*, since the... Holendry... undertake to settle the said ground called Rusiny...;* (...) *and should there not be enough ploughland in the Rusiny for the sixth Holender, then I allow the sixth Holender to rudować* [clear]... *ploughland for himself on the other part of my manor land called Kozale* [Kazala]. According to the first charter the Olęders were to settle from the boundaries of Korzeniew, and according to the second from the boundaries of Słuszków. In both charters the amount of the entry money per morgen was set at 8 zł. The second charter also contained the provision that *the szkolny and the innkeeper will each receive 1 quarter of a huba from what "will be left over" from the hubas cleared by the Olęders*[^170]. From the detailed tables of hearths and population of 1789 it is known that then *the Holendry settled last year on the new colony have 6 hearths*, and also that there were in this settlement 25 *farmers, Holendry settled last year*; in 1791 there were 9 "self-tilling" hearths in the Mycielińskie St. and Nw. Ol., and five years later, in 1796, there were already 17 houses there[^171]. So, as a result of further settling, the area of the whole village would have grown again.

In the Oborskie Stare Ol., that is Wronów, and the Nowe Oborskie Olędry, there were farms of half a huba[^172]. According to the list of hearths of 1789 there were 27 of them, among them: 1 "*inn hearth*", *16 "hearths of rent-paying and entry-fine Holędrzy" and 10 "hearths of contract Holędrzy and lodgers*"[^173].

In the Plewińskie (Stare) and Nowe, or Kuźnickie, Olędry, founded before 1783 and in 1793, there were 19 farms (14 of them "*newly settled on the Kuźnica by Plewnie*" and 5 "*By the Old Hollendry, the New Hollendry*"). The holdings differed, of 1, ½ and ¼ huba; the village also received ¼ huba for the *szkolny* and ½ huba for the inn[^174].

In the Przespolewskie Olędry, that is a part of Przespolew Pański and of Poroże Wielkie, according to the foundation charter of 1772, at least 3 individual holdings were assigned at first, with ½ h. for the innkeeper and 1/2 h. for a school, free of the manor's dues and services, and a plot for a cemetery, which the Olęders were to enclose with a fence; the *grundgeld* for a holding (probably of a huba) was to be 8 Polish złoty[^175]. The inventory of a part of the Przespolewskie Ol. of 1785 named 22 Olęders, 19 of them with holdings of half a huba and three (the innkeeper among them) of 1 huba[^176]. In 1789, as was said above, 37 "self-tilling" hearths, that is farms, in all were recorded in the Przespolewskie Ol.

[^170]: Cf. nn. 111 and 112.

[^171]: APP. Tab. 23. Kalisz (Detailed tables of hearths and population), fols. 501 and 500; Pyzdry Gr. 115, fol. 531v; AGAD. General Directorium Südpreussen I/745, p. 350: "Mycieliner H." (p. 350).

[^172]: Cf. n. 105.

[^173]: APP, Tab. 24. Kalisz (Table of the offering of 10 (20) gr with the table of hearths and population of 1789), fol. 362.

[^174]: Cf. n. 101 (foundation charter of 1 IX 1793).

[^175]: Cf. n. 40.

[^176]: Cf. n. 42.

[p. 87]

In the Stropieszyńskie, or Radzany and Gostynie, Olędry, the foundation charter of 6 XII 1787 set the entry money per morgen (equal to 300 square rods of 7½ ells) of ready land or meadow at 18 złp., and of overgrown land at 3 złp., payable in three instalments, by 1789; it assigned 4 morgen to the *szkolny* free of land payment and rent[^177]. According to the foundation charter of 1791, which at the same time confirmed and supplemented the first charter, the Olęders received 3 morgen for a school and a cemetery, and timber from the manor[^178]. In 1791 they had 6 *self-tilling* hearths[^179], that is farms.

In Wierzchy, or the Tomickie Nowe Olędry, founded in 1781, of the 12 settlers all but the first were in principle to hold ½ huba each, for which in 1786 they paid the founder together 24 ducats of grungeld, that is entry money[^180]. In time the settlement was extended and its area enlarged, since in 1789 it had 18 hearths[^181].

In the Zbierskie Olędry 14 settlers obtained, in 1761 and later, holdings of one huba in perpetuity; the huba equalled 30 morgen "*of the rydlandska* [Rhenish] *measure*", and the morgen had 30 rods in length and 10 in breadth, measured with the rod of 8½ ells; the whole huba had 150 x 60 rods. Besides, ½ huba was assigned for the teacher and ½ huba for the inn, free of rent, and for the "kirchow" (burial ground) a plot of 15x15 rods[^182]. In 1791 the Zbierskie Ol. had 17 *self-tilling* hearths[^183], that is farms.

So of the total of 58 Olęder settlements founded in the Pyzdry Forest, the area can be established exactly or approximately for 37. For the remaining 21 Olęder settlements the area of their land is unknown. If, however, one assumed that the individual holdings there too were of 1 and of ½ włóka (Chełmno-Crown?), then, with the known numbers of *self-tilling*, that is farming, hearths in 1791, their areas could be estimated approximately within two ranges, a minimum and a maximum. The matter is complicated, however, by the variety of the measures of land then in use, and by our not knowing them in the case of these settlements.

The data quoted above for the greater part of the Olęder settlements that arose in the Pyzdry Forest show that their land was given in włóki laid out mostly in the Crown measure, that is measured with the rod of 8½ ells, and in the *rydlandska* (Rhenish) measure, in which the morgen were laid out with the rod of 7½ ells. In some villages the number of farms changed considerably over the several, dozen or several dozen years after the founding, compared with the initial state, mainly because of further settling, but probably also because of the division of farms by inheritance. This is confirmed by the information given above on the number and size of the holdings from the foundation charters, inspections and inventories, together with the information of the tables of hearths of 1789 and of *self-tilling* hearths of 1791.

[^177]: Cf. n. 113.

[^178]: Cf. n. 114.

[^179]: APP, Pyzdry. Gr. 115, fol. 531v: "Holendry Bugaj" = Gostynie Ol. – 4 "self-tilling" hearths; "Radzany" – 2 "self-tilling" hearths.

[^180]: Cf. n. 95.

[^181]: APP. Tab. 21 Kalisz (Tp. of 1789), fol. 13.

[^182]: Cf. n. 50 (emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter of 16 XII 1761).

[^183]: APP, Pyzdry. Gr. 115, fol. 536: "Holendry do Zbierska" [the Holendry by Zbiersk] – 18 "self-tilling" hearths.

[p. 88]

The number and sizes of the holdings (farms) in 37 of the 58 Olęder settlements founded in the Pyzdry Forest, as they stood at the founding and sometimes also later, do not give a full statistical picture of their size at one time common to them all. This can be created only from the data of the tables of hearths and of hearth tax of 1789, drawn up in the course of the first census of population in Poland and the second in Europe and checked in 1790, and from the tables of farming hearths of 1791, drawn up in connection with the grain levy that the Diet voted on them. Since these are sometimes given jointly for one or even more settlements belonging to the same landed estate, and are sometimes even missing, their state in some settlements has to be established indirectly and by estimate (resorting to other sources, in the form of information from the foundation charters, the tables of hearths and hearth tax of 1775 (with suitable corrections), the survey of the royal estates of 1789, and the inventories and revisions of church estates of the years 1791-1793). Moreover, detailed tables of hearths, which make it possible to grasp their functional-economic structure, survive only for some of the Olęder settlements that concern us here, those in the districts of Pyzdry and Kalisz. For the district of Konin they have to be established indirectly from the table of the hearth tax of 1789 or the table of the grain levy of 1791. Before we proceed, however, to set out the numbers of hearths in general and of farming hearths of all the Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest, taken from the sources named or estimated on the basis of them, and to interpret their numbers and in part the structure mentioned, some explanations are needed.

First, it should be said that in the census tables (protocols, sheets) of hearths of 1789, by a hearth in general (*dym*), treated as the unit of account of the tax called the hearth tax (*podymne*), was understood, in the countryside but also in the towns, above all, but not only, a farm with its dwelling and farm buildings, or, more exactly, a farmstead. For this term covered *cottages in the village and in the futory* [outlying farmsteads] and (or) *distant from the village – with one farmer*, but sometimes also *with two farmers*, and also the *garden* (*ogrodowe*) – smallholders' – ones (*with land and garden or without land and garden*), *craftsmen's, manufacturing, and manor* and *church hearths, mills* (water and wind), *breweries, inns* and *Jewish hearths*. The manor and parsonage buildings, on the other hand, of which there were none in the purely Olęder villages, and which in the mixed ones belonged legally to their labour-service parts and not to their Olęder rent-paying parts, were *counted as one hearth*[^184].

Second, it should be said that by the Diet's resolution, in accordance with the text of the constitution of the Diet of 1791, every hearth whose holder or inhabitant sowed land with grain, regardless of the nature of the estate and of whether he paid the offering of 10 or 20 gr., was to be considered a farming hearth subject to the grain levy for the army (in the amount of 2 korzec of rye and 4 korzec of oats). Because of the differences in the size of their land, farming hearths were allowed to be burdened with the tax in different amounts,

[^184]: W. Rusiński, *Struktura osadnictwa...*, p. 59. The notion of the hearth-farm did not always mean the same, however, as is shown by the "village cottages with two or more farmers"; such farms, or rather in this case farmsteads with 2 farmers, were, however, relatively few..

[p. 89]

leaving the setting of it on the noble estates to their "superiors", that is the owners. Only those hearths were to be counted as freed from the grain levy, and so as non-farming, whose holders had no land at all or had "*vegetable... gardens*", that is, were occupied solely in growing vegetables[^185]. So by non-farming hearths were meant hearths without land, only with a garden, that is craftsmen's and strictly smallholders' hearths, and perhaps lodgers' ones too, if they were separate cottages inhabited by lodgers (*komornicy* and *komornice*), single or with families (as, in the last case, the information in the tables of hearths and population of 1789 seems to show).

Third, the table of the hearth tax of 1789, since it contains the amounts of this tax from only one instalment, the Midsummer (June) one, makes it possible to establish the numbers of hearths in the settlements indirectly, by multiplying these amounts by two (to obtain the sum of the tax payable in two instalments for the whole year) and dividing the product by the annual rates of this tax, which for rural settlements were 2 Polish złoty per hearth under the law of the Diet of 1789[^186].

A summary of the total numbers of hearths (farms) in general in the years 1789-1790 in the 58 settlements founded in the Pyzdry Forest, showing their numbers and partly also their functional-economic structure by the criteria of the time, together with the numbers of farming hearths in 1791 for all the settlements, makes it possible to establish approximately the size of these settlements in these respects. It has been set out in the form of table no. 2.

<tr><td><b>58.</b></td><td>Zbierskie Olędry</td><td></td><td>1</td><td>13</td><td>1</td><td>–</td><td>2</td><td>2</td><td>18</td><td>18</td></tr>
<tr><td colspan="2"><b>Total</b></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td><b>271</b></td><td><b>270</b></td></tr>
<tr><td colspan="2"><b>Total in the three districts</b></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td><b>651</b></td><td><b>662</b></td></tr>
</tbody></table>

The analysis of the whole of this table shows that in the 58 Olęder settlements founded in the Pyzdry Forest there were in the years 1789-1790, approximately, 651 hearths in all. So on average there would have been 11.224 hearths to a settlement. For the greater part of these settlements the number of farms showed a certain constancy compared with the initial state of the period of their founding, shown above; for the smaller part, however, it varied, most often upwards, because further settlers were brought in, or because of the increasingly varied social and economic structure of the villages, through the division of farms by inheritance.

There was a wide range in the number of farms among the Olęder villages. It can be grasped directly for the 38 settlements given separately. Among them there were: very small settlements, of 2-5 hearths – 9; small ones, of 6-10 hearths – 11; middling ones, of 11-15 hearths – 5; larger than middling, of 16-20 hearths – 8; large, of 21-25 hearths, only 2; and very large, of 26 hearths, only 1. It is impossible to give the proportions by number of hearths for the settlements for which these were given in the tables, directly or indirectly, but jointly. The number of their hearths ranged from 6 to 33, so that on average one of them would have had from 3 to 16.5 hearths.

For 16 of the 58 Olęder settlements Tab. 2 gives detailed data on the functional and social-economic structure of these hearths. Among them, if

Kalisz (Tof. z Todl), fol. 63; Tab. 44. Pyzdry (Td), fols. 318v-319, 371v-372, 410v-411; Tab. 45 Pyzdry (Tod), fols. 9, 12, 15v; Kalisz. Gr. 481 (tabddel.p), fol. 222v; Kalisz. Gr. 483 (Tab. del. p.pp), fol. 107; Pyzdry Gr. 115, fols. 528v, 529, 531v, 532-532v, 533v, 534, 535-536, 538-538v, 540-540v, 542-542v, 543-543v, 540, 545v, 549, 549v-550v, 551. Notes: a) "Hollendry Wrąbczyńskie z młynem Białobrzeg" [the Wrąbczyn Hollendry with the mill Białobrzeg]; b) together with the Orlińskie D. and M. Ol., c) "Holędry zwane Borowiec i Huta" [the Holędry called Borowiec and Huta], d) together with the Białobłockie Ol.

[p. 92]

we leave aside 2 breweries, 6 inns and 2 craftsmen's hearths, hearths with 1 farmer decidedly predominated, found in all 15 Olęder villages, 636 in all. Hearths with 2 farmers, 37 in all, were found in only 5 settlements, garden hearths with land and garden, 16 in number, in 3 settlements, and those without land and garden, 2 in number, in 1 settlement. Unfortunately there are no data on the structure of the other 43 Olęder villages. It may be supposed, however, among other things from the social structure of the inhabitants, that not only in these 16 settlements but also in most of the Olęder settlements that concern us, hearths-farms with one farmer predominated, that is of one or half a włóka (full and half peasant farms, *kmiece*, *rolnicze* and *półkmiece*, *półrolnicze*), and only a very small part of them were smaller ones, with two farmers, and smallholders' ones with land and garden, and rather rarely lodgers' ones.

According to the tables of the grain levy (in rye and oats) for the army of 1791 for the districts of Pyzdry, Konin and Kalisz, there were 662 "self-tilling", that is farming, hearths in the 58 Olęder villages of the Pyzdry Forest. A comparison of the data on the farming hearths of 1791 with the information on the total numbers of hearths of 1789 shows that in all there were 1.662% more farming hearths two years later. Since, however, the numbers of hearths for 34 settlements are estimates, the totals of hearths of both categories need correcting. We do this by raising the total of 662 farming hearths by 6.34%, that is by 42 hearths, since that was the difference between farming hearths and hearths in general shown by the complete statistical data surviving for a representative sample of rural settlements of the districts of Kalisz, Pyzdry and Kościan. As a result of this addition we obtain 704 hearths. By adding this sum of 42 hearths to the 649 hearths in general (since most of the estimated numbers in the first column of the table repeat the number of farming hearths as a minimum), we obtain the number of 691 hearths in general, which should be taken as more realistic for the year 1789. By 1791 their number, in keeping with a slightly higher annual rate of natural increase[^187], could have grown by at least 1%, that is by 6 persons, that is to about 699 persons in all. The literature shows, however, that more than 98% of the inhabitants of the Olęder settlements were occupied in farming. This will be discussed further below. So probably almost the same, or a similar, percentage of the hearths in the 58 Olęder settlements founded in the Pyzdry Forest were farming hearths (farms) in 1789. For the farms of innkeepers and teachers, which were the main source of their holders' living, were not counted as farming hearths.

The settlement charters of the Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest contain, in principle, no information on their layout and the character of their buildings. Only in the foundation charters cited above, for the Orlińskie D. and M. Ol. of 1784 and the Białobłockie Ol. of 1787, is it said that their inhabitants are "*...to build properly and on a line*"[^188], that is to create a village in the form of a street village. From what is known of the layout of the Olęder settlements

[^187]: *Dzieje Wielkopolski*, Vol. 1, p. 812, n. 22 (text of J. Topolski, in which the author puts this increase somewhat lower, at 0.45%).

[^188]: Cf. nn. 106 and 65.

[p. 93]

founded in other areas of Greater Poland, however, their layout was of three kinds: 1) mostly spontaneous (sometimes growing gradually), 2) planned, made at one stroke on the initiative of the feudal lord, or 3) inherited (as in the case of Wrąbczynek). Within each of these ways of founding Olęder villages, mostly according to their form on the ground, shaped chiefly by factors of the geographical environment, there were three types of spatial form: 1) the lowland settlement adapted to the relief and the water conditions of the land, the row village (*rzędówka*), a village with compact, more concentrated (a regular street village) or looser (an irregular street village) buildings; 2) the loose, scattered settlement (in the form of larger or smaller irregular clusters of farmsteads, sometimes with a green or a pond); and 3) the mixed settlement, combining some elements of the first two types. The more or less compact settlement, that is the row village (also called the marsh row village), and the loose, scattered settlement were, in principle, two new types of village layout brought by Olęder settlement into Greater Poland, and the most widespread in it. Row villages – street villages with strips of land holding various kinds of farmland (fields, meadows, pastures and others) – formed in the conditions of Greater Poland one, two, but sometimes three straight, bent or broken rows, lines of farmsteads (farms), placed in various ways in relation to each other (parallel and perpendicular), more or less concentrated and of various lengths (mostly 1–1.5 km), much less, however, than in Royal Prussia[^189].

The layout of most Olęder settlements in the period closest to their founding can be established only from the later Map of the Quartermaster General's staff of the Army of the Kingdom of Poland of 1839, and from the modern Map of Poland of the Surveyor General of the Country at a scale of 1:100,000 and the tourist map of the Pyzdry Forest at 1:55,000. From an analysis of these maps, taking into account the fact that as a result of the enfranchisement reform of 1864 the layout of these rent-paying villages without a demesne farm did not change fundamentally, it can be said that of the 58 Old Polish Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest, 9 settlements were marsh row villages, founded where marshy and dry land met, on mixed ground, with rows of buildings in various arrangements, sometimes partly scattered (Białobłockie Ol., Cekowskie St. Ol., Gadowskie Ol., Kazimierskie St. and Nw. Ol., Lisewskie Ol., Luśnie Ol., Myszakowskie Ol., Piskory Ol.); 2 settlements were regular and irregular street villages founded on mixed ground (Dębniałki Ol., Grodziec Ol.); as many as 40 settlements were loose, scattered settlements; and 7 were mixed (Białobłockie Ol., Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol., Orlińskie St. and Nw. Ol., Wierzchy Ol. and Wrąbczynek).

The layout of the Olęder settlements (made at the founding on raw root, or taken over from older settlements), the network of roads, the arrangement and system of the land, and sometimes the placing of the buildings, shaped their geometric forms. Accordingly, from the point of view of their physiognomy, the Olęder villages represented various types and subtypes of form.

[^189]: W. Rusiński, *Osady tzw. „olędrów”*, pp. 56–57; J. Burszta, *Od osady słowiańskiej do wsi współczesnej* [From the Slavic settlement to the modern village], pp. 108–109, 114–118; the same, *Osadnictwo i kształty wsi* [Settlement and the forms of villages], [in:] *Kultura ludowa Wielkopolski* [The folk culture of Greater Poland], ed. the same, vol. I, Poznań 1960, pp. 31- 105; Z. Chodyła, *Osadnictwo olęderskie w Wielkopolsce (1597-1793)*.

[p. 94]

The form of these villages was also affected by the layout of the fields (arable land, meadows, pastures) and the system of tilling them. As to the latter, the three-field system in the Olęder villages was, to a far smaller degree than in the labour-service villages, a communal three-field system, that is one tied to the so-called compulsion of the field (*przymus polny* or *niwowy*) for the whole village, under which each peasant had in a given year to sow, on one of the fields of his farm, lying in one line with and next to the like fields of other farms, particular crops (winter or spring crops) or to leave that field fallow, according to the decision of the whole village community. In the compact Olęder settlements, in the regular and irregular row villages, but also in those of various other forms, often, and in the scattered, loose ones generally, the individual three-field system was used. This followed from the predominance in them of the *łan* (strip) layout. With such a layout of the land each settler had his field in one piece (block), usually on a long strip of land (a rectangle), and tilled it as he saw fit, independently of his neighbours. These strips lay parallel to each other and ran from the middle of the village to its boundary. A characteristic feature of the *łan* layout, which distinguished the villages in which it was found from those with the *niwa* (open-field) layout, was also that each farm had its own field road running the length of its whole strip of land. The *łan* layout of many Olęder villages came about because they were founded on forest clearings, where the land lay in just such a way and the soils varied, or on so-called *pustkowia* (isolated holdings), where the land was brought into use gradually. In the latter case settlement most often began with one, two or a few scattered farms, distant from each other, which of course had no conditions for the communal three-field system[^190].

The layout of the Olęder villages, especially their internal layout, was affected by the types of their homesteads, their farmsteads. Of the four types of farmstead – sets of buildings (distinguished by the placing of the masses of the dwelling and farm buildings, and by their position in relation to the roads, the watercourses and each other) – proper to the traditions of the country and place the settlers came from, the most widespread in Greater Poland, including the settlements founded in the eighteenth century in the Pyzdry Forest, was the fourth type: the scattered farmstead, most often with an arcaded cottage (in time this type of building disappeared and is not found today), set with its gable or its wall to the road, and with free-standing farm buildings, put up of wooden logs, in post-and-plank or, more rarely, log construction, and also, in large numbers, of clay and bog iron ore. In general people built with the material that was most readily available and cheapest. All the buildings (house, cowshed-pigsty, stable, barn, granary) were put up separately, scattered, most often on a quadrangular plan; barns were sometimes placed apart, beyond the road. The commonest pattern was one of two or three buildings: the cottage, the barn, and a livestock building (made up of pigsty, stable, cowshed and cart shed). At the same time, though more rarely, there were free-standing buildings such as sheepfolds, sheds and cart sheds[^191].

[^190]: J. Burszta, *Osadnictwo i kształty wsi*, [In:] *Kultura ludowa Wielkopolski*, ed. the same, Poznań 1960, pp. 44–46; the same, *Od osady słowiańskiej do wsi współczesnej. O tworzeniu się krajobrazu osadniczego ziem polskich i rozplanowań wsi* [From the Slavic settlement to the modern village. On the making of the settlement landscape of the Polish lands and of village layouts], Wrocław 1958.

[^191]: W. Rusiński, *Osady tzw. „olędrów”*, pp. 57–58; A. Pelczyk, *Poolęderskie budownictwo mieszkalne na obszarze Równiny Nowotomyskiej* [Post-Olęder dwelling houses in the area of the Nowy Tomyśl Plain], "Studia Lednickie", IV, Poznań – Lednica 1996, pp. 357–383.

[p. 95]

At the beginning of the clearing of wooded and overgrown land, the inhabitants of the Olęder settlements put up temporary huts (*budy*) to live in, and then, still within the free years, they were bound to put up cottages. Huts are mentioned in the foundation charters and other sources for the Cekowskie St. Ol., the Przespolewskie Ol., Świdle Nowa Kolonia and the Trąbczyńskie St. Ol.[^192]. The charters for some settlements stated that the settlers were to put up their buildings before their free years ran out (Gadowskie St. Ol.), within a set time of two or four years (Białobłockie Ol., Orlińskie D. and M., Lubieńskie Ol.), properly (Cekowskie Nw. Ol.), as quickly as possible, properly and "on a line" (that is keeping to the layout of a street village), in the first case under the supervision of the sołtys and assessor (Grądy St. and Nw. Ol.), within a set time of several years under threat of inspection and a money fine (Grądy St. and Nw. Ol., Borowieckie St. and Nw. and Konarskie Ol.[^193]). Such orders arose from the dawdling or laziness, or the limited means for investment, of some Olęders, as the case of the Trąbczyńskie St. and Nw. Ol. confirms[^194]. The area of the land assigned for the homestead, that is for the buildings of a farm, was stated only once, in the charter for the Mycielińskie Ol., at 1 morgen (Magdeburg?)[^195].

In the buildings and layout of the Olęder villages in the Pyzdry Forest, most of which were wholly or partly inhabited by people of the Protestant confession, important elements were the buildings for worship that were also educational (schools – houses of prayer, later churches), and the burial grounds, which lay on the whole on the edges of the villages, on hilly, wooded or poor land, and which will be discussed more fully in the last chapter of this study.

[^192]: Cf. nn. 58, 40, 125, 34.

[^193]: Cf. nn. 82, 65, 106, 78, 74, 72, 70, 60–61.

[^194]: Cf. n. 34.

[^195]: Cf. nn. 111–112.

[p. 96]

# 3. The population of the Olęder villages in the Pyzdry Forest and its demographic, social and occupational, confessional and ethnic structure, and the origin of the settlers

In keeping with the differences in the size of the 58 Olęder villages in the Pyzdry Forest by the number of hearths in the years 1789-1791, their population too differed at this time. Only for some of them do we know, from foundation charters or other sources, the numbers of the settler farmers (the "heads" of families) at the time of their founding or in the following years. For most of them (50) these numbers, with their structure, can be established at one time on the basis of the data of the detailed tables of population of 1789 and the record of the visitation of the deanery of Konin of 1790-1791, and for the remaining 8 settlements (1 in the district of Kalisz and 7 in the district of Konin) approximately, by multiplying the previously estimated numbers of their hearths by the population index we have established, 6.05 persons per hearth[^196].

The population of the Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest in the years 1789-1791, within the three former districts of Pyzdry, Konin and Kalisz and in alphabetical order, is shown in Table 3, prepared in three parts, a, b and c. Parts 3a and 3b show respectively the numbers and, for the most part, the demographic structure (by sex and to some extent by age) and the social and occupational structure of the inhabitants of 5 Olęder settlements in the district of Pyzdry and 25 settlements in the district of Kalisz, mostly on the basis of the data of the detailed population tables drawn up at the census of 1789, and for a few settlements for which there are no source data, only their total populations, estimated. In the last case we determine the numbers of inhabitants of such settlements by multiplying the numbers of their hearths (given in Tab. 2) by the estimated average population per hearth mentioned above. Part 3c of the Table, on the other hand, shows the population of 28 such settlements in the district of Konin, and for 20 of them with their demographic and confessional structure, according to the lists of 1790-1791 contained in the record

[^196]: This index is lower than the average of 6.37 persons per rural hearth that we established in the article: *Nowy szacunek zaludnienia Wielkopolski i wskaźników jego struktury u schyłku XVIII wieku* [A new estimate of the population of Greater Poland and of the indices of its structure at the end of the eighteenth century]. [In:] *Przemiany demograficzne Europy Środkowej w czasach nowożytnych. Zielonogórskie spotkania z demografią 1.* Ed. H. Kurowska, University of Zielona Góra, Zielona Góra 2010, pp. 87– 129. It has been lowered because the natural increase in the Olęder settlements was smaller than in other rural settlements. See W. Rusiński, *Osady tzw. olędrów w dawnym woj. poznańskim*, and Z. Chodyła, *Osadnictwo olęderskie w Wielkopolsce (1597-1793).*

[p. 97]

of the visitation of the deanery of Konin of 1792, and for the remaining 8 only the total, estimated.

**Key: M – male, Ż – female, K – women, married (widows), Ch. – boys, Dz. – girls, kat. – Catholics, [ ] – estimated number.**

This table shows that in the 5 Olęder villages founded in the district of Pyzdry, within the Pyzdry Forest, 499 persons in all lived in the years 1789-1791. So the average population per settlement would have been 99.8 persons. For 3 of these settlements the demographic and social and occupational structure is known. Of the total of 449 inhabitants of the three settlements attested directly in the sources, 234 persons were male and 215 female. By age and marital status, 177 persons were adults, counted among husbands and wives and widowers and widows. On the other hand, 272 persons can be counted as children and young people, 146 of them male and 126 female. These figures are open to doubt, however, because of the number of sons over 15 (38), undoubtedly understated in order to protect them from conscription into the army, and also because the age limit is not stated for the daughters. By social and occupational structure, of the 449 inhabitants of these villages 90 were counted as farmers, 218 as cottagers paying rent, 9 as lodgers, 4 as craftsmen, 21 as farm servants, 53 as *luźni*, that is hired workers, and 54 in the category of other persons, among whom were counted teachers, but also persons described as "beggars", cripples and others. The total populations of two settlements, Wrąbczynek and the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol., from two sources of 1789 and 1791, differ by 99 persons. According to the first source 309 persons lived in the two villages, and according to the second (a year later) only 210. The second of these sources also gives the confessional composition of the population of the two Olęder villages. Of the total of 210 persons, 111 (in Wrąbczynek) were exclusively Catholics, and 99 (in the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol.) exclusively dissenters (Protestants).

The number and structure of the inhabitants of the Olęder settlements within the Pyzdry Forest in the district of Kalisz are shown in Tab. 3b.

Sources: APP, Zbiór tabel podatkowych i lustracji. Tab. 46. Pyzdry (Tl.), fols. 376, 436v-437, 436v-437, 478v-479; Diocesan Archive in Włocławek. Archive of the Archbishops of Gniezno. Wiz. 85: Records of the visitation of the deanery of Konin 1790-1791, call no. 85, fols. 292 (Wrąbczynek, Wrąbczyn[kowskie] Ol. in the parish of Zagórów).

The table shows that in the 25 Olęder settlements founded in the district of Kalisz, within the Pyzdry Forest, 1,457 persons lived in the years 1789-1790. So the average population per settlement was 62.18 persons.

Of the total of 1,368 inhabitants in the 22 settlements for which their demographic and social and occupational structure can be established, 691 persons were male and 677 female. By age and marital status, 591 persons were adults, counted among husbands and wives and widowers and widows. On the other hand, 772 persons can be regarded as children and young people, 382 of them male and 395 female. These figures are open to doubt, however, because of the number of sons over 15 (78), undoubtedly understated in order to protect them from recruitment into the army, and also because the age limit is not stated for the daughters. By social and occupational structure, of the 1,368 inhabitants of these 23 villages 1,210 were counted as farmers, 41 as lodgers, 3 as craftsmen, 13 as innkeepers and tapsters, 81 as farm servants and servants, 15 as *luźni* (landless labourers), and 5 in the category of other persons.

The number and demographic structure of the inhabitants of the Olęder settlements within the Pyzdry Forest in the district of Konin are shown in Tab. 3c.

[p. 104]

Tab. 3c shows that the population of 20 of the 28 Olęder settlements in the district of Konin, 1,337 persons in all, was made up demographically of 654 males and 683 females. Adults numbered 782 persons, and children and young people (described as boys and girls without division by age) 482 persons. By confession, the 1,337 inhabitants of 20 of the 28 settlements were as follows: there were 6 settlements with an exclusively dissenting population, 385 in all (Lutheran, and perhaps to a small degree Reformed), 4 with a Catholic population, 210 in all, and 10 settlements with a mixed population, Catholic and Protestant (almost exclusively Lutheran), with 365 Catholics and 377 dissenters in all. So in all these settlements there would have lived 575 Catholics and 762 dissenters in all, that is Protestants – Lutherans, and perhaps in two or three of these settlements also Reformed. The former would thus have made up 43.01% and the latter 56.99% of their whole population. In the remaining 8 settlements of the district of Konin, for which the demographic and confessional composition is not known from the sources, there lived, according to estimates made in the same way as for such settlements in the districts of Pyzdry and Kalisz, 522 persons. In all, 1,859 persons would have lived in all 28 Olęder villages of this district.

Summing up the results of the calculations given in Tab. 3a, b, c, it must be said that about 3,815 persons in all lived in the 58 Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest in the years 1789–1790, of whom 469 in 5 settlements in the district of Pyzdry, 1,457 in 25 settlements in the district of Kalisz, and 1,859 in 28 settlements in the district of Konin. The statistical average number of inhabitants per settlement would have been 65.77 persons. In the 45 settlements for which the demographic structure of their inhabitants is known there lived 3,154 persons, 1,707 (50.32%) of them male and 1,685 (49.68%) female. Of these 3,154 persons, 449 (234 male and 215 female) lived in 3 settlements of the district of Pyzdry, 1,368 persons (691 male and 677 female) lived in 22 settlements of the district of Kalisz, and 1,337 persons (654 male and 683 female) lived in 20 settlements of the district of Konin. For 13 settlements (2 in the district of Pyzdry, 8 in the district of Konin and 3 in the district of Kalisz) the structure by sex is not shown in the sources that survive and are available to us. The demographic structure of 25 Olęder settlements (3 in the district of Pyzdry and 22 in the district of Kalisz) looked different again by the criteria adopted in the census of 1789, with the population divided into men, married men and widowers; sons (that is boys of pre-conscription age, under 15, and those subject to the conscription register, aged 15 and over); *białogłowy* (married women and widows); and daughters, that is girls, in the sense, it seems, not only of persons young in age but of unmarried women in general, from infancy to old age. Thus in 3 of the 5 Olęder villages in the district of Pyzdry, of a total of 449 inhabitants, 88 were men, 108 sons under 15, 38 sons aged 15 and over,

Sources: Diocesan Archive in Włocławek. Archive of the Archbishops of Gniezno. Wiz. 85: Records of the visitation of the deanery of Konin 1790-1791, call no. 85, fols. 292 (Białobłockie Ol., Borowieckie St. and Nw. Ol., Grądy St. and Nw. Ol., Imielno, Huta St. [-Grojeckie Ol.], Huta St. [-Zagórowskie Ol.], Imielno, Kowalewskie Ol., Łazy, Myszakówko, Orlińskie M. and D. Ol., Trąbczyńskie Luźne St. and Nw. Ol.). Notes: a) together with the Białobłockie Ol.

[p. 105]

89 "białogłowy", that is women, and 126 daughters. In 22 of the 25 Olęder villages of the district of Kalisz, with a total population of 1,368 persons, 309 were men, then 304 sons under 15, then 78 sons aged 15 and over, then 282 women, and 395 daughters. So in the 25 Olęder settlements of the districts of Pyzdry and Kalisz, of a total of 1,817 persons of their population, 397 were men, 412 sons under 15, 116 sons aged 15 and over, 371 women and 521 daughters. Among these five categories of the demographic composition of the population the category of sons aged 15 and over was undoubtedly understated, probably because of the threat of conscription into the army. Perhaps some of them were counted among the sons under 15, whose number seems somewhat too high, especially given the high mortality of children under 5 in former times. In a somewhat simplified form (without dividing the boys into age groups) the demographic composition of the population of 20 of the 28 Olęder settlements in the district of Konin can be grasped; according to the lists of 1790-1791 it is given in the records of the visitation of the parishes of the deanery of Konin already mentioned above. In these lists the population of the settlements was divided into four categories: men, women, boys and girls. Adding up the data in this source for the 20 Olęder settlements that concern us, it can be said that of the total of 1,337 inhabitants (in this case probably covering only the Olęder farmers with the members of their families, leaving out the farm servants and servants and other categories of landless people) men numbered 427 persons, women 428, boys 227 and girls 255. Summing up the information above on the numerical relation of the four demographic categories of the population of 45 Olęder settlements in the three districts of Pyzdry, Konin and Kalisz, leaving aside the differences of age among the boys, it can be said that of their 3,124 inhabitants for whom such a division is known, 824 persons were men, 598 women, 956 boys and 756 daughters (girls and unmarried women of various ages).

While the size of the population of each, and of all, of the 58 Olęder villages in the Pyzdry Forest, and the demographic structure of most of them, 45, can be established exactly or by estimate, it is harder to give exact statistics of their social, property and occupational structure. It can be grasped in the detailed census tables for only 25 of them (3 in the district of Pyzdry and 22 in the district of Kalisz). There were in all in them 1,518 farmers, that is Olęders proper with the members of their families, farmers and rent-paying cottagers, who can in principle be regarded as one and the same social and occupational category, 50 lodgers, 7 craftsmen, 13 innkeepers and tapsters, 102 farm servants and servants, 68 *luźni* (landless people) and 59 "other" persons. The village community of the settlements, as far as the Olęder farmers were concerned, was internally differentiated in property already at the founding, according to the size of the holdings received, and still more so a dozen or several dozen years after it, when farms were sometimes divided into smaller ones through inheritance. The differences of property were also tied to the financial means of the settlers themselves, in the form of the size of the entry money they had to, and could, pay for the holdings of land of a given size that they received for perpetual use.

As to the confessional and ethnic composition of the inhabitants of the 58 Olęder settlements founded in the years 1746-1791 in the Pyzdry Forest, it must be said that it was

[p. 106]

varied. This follows from the statistical data for 22 Olęder villages (2 in the district of Pyzdry and 20 in the district of Konin), already given above in Tab. 3a and 3c, contained in the records of the church visitation of the deanery of Konin of 1790-1791, and also from the direct and indirect information on this question in the foundation charters of these settlements and other sources concerning them, especially such as inspections, inventories and revisions, and sometimes also the census tables of 1789, which have partly been given above and will partly be presented below.

Thus it is known that in the three oldest settlements, neighbours of one another, in the district of Konin – the Trąbczyńskie St. and Nw. Ol. and the Luśnie Ol. (founded in 1746 and 1754) – Catholics and Lutherans settled, the latter predominating. This follows from the names given in the commission decree, Kinos and Meller, and from the list of population of 1790-1791 in the record of the visitation of the deanery of Konin[^197]. In the Łazy Ol., probably also founded about 1754, and certainly before 1789, only Catholics lived in 1790[^198].

In the Przespolewskie Ol. in the district of Kalisz at least several German settlers, Lutherans, settled before 1750 and in 1772, 3 of them known by name: Marcin Schmarschel, Fryderyk Jakobeck, Marcin Lehmann[^199] The decree of the commission court of 1779 on the tradition (judicial handing over) of a part of the Przespolew estate in payment of a debt of its owner, Tadeusz Zaremba, to the creditor Tomasz Moraczewski names 3 Olęders living in a part of this settlement, Poroże Wielkie: Marcin Smaża, Andrzej Szuldz and Frydrych Jakopczyk[^200]; two of them probably the same as those named above in the charter of 1772. The inventory of (a part of) the Przespolewskie Ol. of 1785 names 22 Olęders with names that are (except perhaps for one Polish one) undoubtedly German. Here they are, with their first names, in the forms written in this source: Krystian Lekner, Krystian Werner, Mateusz Bruchajzer, Wawrzyniec Fagen, Balcer Gotfryd, Krystian Szulc, Józef Kubs, Kr[z]ysztof Mager, Henryk Draier, Adam Walkier, Krystjan Macanka, Jan Raych, Wojciech Walle, Jan Walkier, Bernard Caydler, Gotfryd Daubyc, Jan Perszel, Jan Caydler, Wojciech Frank, Kasper Waber, Marcin Leyman, Bogusław Geerszling[^201]. Marcin Schmarschel and Fryderyk Jakobeck/Jakubek/Jakobczyk, named above, are not among them.

In the Lisewskie Ol. (Olsz), in the district of Pyzdry, founded after 1751 and before 1765, the existing Polish and Catholic population probably lived, though in one or two cases also, it seems, German people[^202]. The names and first names of the inhabitants of the Lisewskie Ol. are given only in the registers of the parish of Szymanowice. As living in this settlement there were first entered there, under the year 1775, in turn: Weronika Pankowska, Wojciech Panek, and Stanisław and Maria Krzywiński. In 1776 the names of Elżbieta Pankow, Gertruda Panek and Jan Łamek were entered in them, in 1779 Agnieszka and Łukasz Nowacki, in 1780 Grzegorz

[^197]: Cf. n. 34 and Tab. 3c

[^198]: Cf. Tab. 3c.

[^199]: Cf. nn. 40 and 41.

[^200]: Rus. Inw. 2, Pt. I, p. 222 (decree of the commission court of 10 III 1779).

[^201]: Cf. n. 42.

[^202]: ADWł. Parish registers of Szymanowice, 1756-1758, pp. 163, 166, 182, 183, 185, 186, 298.

[p. 107]

and Maria Dąbrowski, later, in 1793, Feliks and Mara Koźlak were recorded in them, and in 1794 Józef and Maria Broniarczyk[^203]. The form of the names seems to show that the population of this settlement was mixed, Polish-German, the German part, being in the minority, becoming assimilated fairly quickly, to judge from the change of a name (Pankow to Pankowski).

In the Ciemierowa/Tomickie/Szymanowickie Stare Ol., in the district of Kalisz, which arose before 1755-1756, it can be inferred from the form of the names (Cyft, Rotenberg(er), Dresler, Klim, Sypniewski, Vaber and others) and from the baptismal registers of the settlers' children that they were Catholics, but perhaps partly also Protestant Lutherans, mostly Germans, but also Poles[^204].

In the Zbierskie Ol., in the district of Kalisz (founded in 1761), 14 settlers with German and Polish names, Lutherans, were settled. Under the text of the foundation charter of that year the following settlers are named: Andrzej Hert, Bogusław Cherman (Herman), Marcin Krol, Krzysztof Szulc, Maciej Góra, Marcin Szulc, Krystian Kurtz, Paweł Hauman (or Hawman), Krzysztof Bonig, Marcin Weys, Marcin Reszka, Godfri(e)d(t) Wuntrach, Jędrzej Kamyszek, Krystian Ryska, the widow of Maciej Tak, settled in the years 1761-1769, three of them only temporarily. The inventory of the Zbiersk estate belonging to Gąsiorowski, royal chamberlain, drawn up in 1771 by the married couple Przespolewski, names 6 Olęders (Marcin Szulc, Marcin Krol, Maciej Góra, Maciejowa Takowa, Krzysztof Szulc and Jan Krzysztof Wajstak) and the amount of their dues, and 3 Olęders not yet paying them. The inventory of the same estate of 1775 found, among other things, 14 farmers in the Zbierskie Ol. By confession they were Protestants, perhaps even Reformed, since, as was mentioned above, they used the Reformed church at Żychlin[^205], though it is not out of the question that they were Lutherans going to that church because it was close to them.

In the Wielołęka Ol. in the district of Konin, founded before or in 1767, there settled, according to the charters of 1770, Jan Czarneski, Sebastian Rest, Walenty Ginter, Bogusław Sztruma, Wojciech August Ralcer, Arnold Jan Kimel, Krzysztof Fryze, Bogumił Wondrak, Krystian Kiejzer. According to the inventory of the properties of Grodziec and Królików of 1779, 15 farmers lived in this settlement (14 of them named with first names and surnames of German sound, 1 innkeeper and 1 *szkolny*, that is teacher, not named). In 1790, 168 dissenters, that is Lutherans, lived in this village[^206].

In the royal Cekowskie St. Ol. in the district of Kalisz, 9 settlers were settled in 1768 (2 with Polish names – Kazimierz Kuczka, *wójt*, and Andrzej Warmiński – and 7 with German names), Lutherans and Catholics, described as people "of the Olęder estate"; some of them most probably came from abroad, since the foundation charter guaranteed them the right to sell the farms granted them *...should any of them not like it in this country*[^207].

[^203]: ADWł. Parish registers of Szymanowice, 1762-1793 (in order of the dates given).

[^204]: ADWł. Parish registers of Szymanowice, 1756-1758, pp. 163, 166, 182, 183, 185, 186, 298.

[^205]: Cf. nn. 50, 51 and 52.

[^206]: Cf. nn. 53 and 54, 55 and 56-57; Tab. 3.

[^207]: Cf. n. 58; *Królewszczyzny kaliskie*, p. 171; ; Kal. Gr. 481 (1791), fol. 808 (obl. 1 VI 1791).

[p. 108]

In the Cekowskie Nowe Ol., or Prażuchy, their neighbours, which arose on the same royal lease of Ceków in 1774 and 1780, at least 8 Lutheran colonists, with German and Polish names, settled according to the settlement contract, namely: Wojciech Pruchno, Krzysztof Stefan (sołtys), Gotfryd Reszka, Krystian Kurc, Krystian Heinc, Frydrych Heinc, Gotfryd Szwander, Jan Paszkowski. Later, in the years 1782, 1785 and 1787, there were settled: Kups, Hertel, Puntzler, Krzysztof Kowal, Gajzler and Grynberg[^208].

In the 6 Olęder settlements founded before 1770 and in 1771-1772 (and re-founded in 1787 and 1789) in the district of Konin, on the noble estates of Grodziec and Królików, the population was mixed in nation and confession. In the Białobłockie Ol. at least several colonists settled, Lutherans with German names. The number, names, nationality and confession of those brought in to settle in 1787 are unknown. In 1790, 157 Catholics and 48 dissenters lived here. In 1792 the Olęders Józef and Jakub Hersa, brothers, and Andrzej Langa/Lange lived in this village[^209].

In the Borowieckie St. and Nw. Ol., the settlers, settled in an unknown number, bore German and Polish names and were Lutherans and Catholics by confession. According to the inventory of the properties of Grodziec and Królików of 1779, 27 farmers lived in the Borowieckie Ol., named with their first names and surnames (4 of them of Polish sound). In 1790 there were 52 Catholics and 42 dissenters in this settlement[^210].

In the Grójeckie or Grodzieckie Olędry there settled, in an unknown number, probably 10 farmers, Germans – Lutherans[^211].

In the Grądy Stare and Nowe lived settlers with German and Polish names, in 1790 exclusively Lutherans, 100 in number[^212].

In Konary Ol., in turn, whose locator, that is establisher, was Jan Kimel, mentioned above, sołtys of the Wielołęka Ol., settlers with German and Polish names were settled, in 1790 exclusively Lutherans, 81 in number[^213]).

In two settlements founded in the district of Konin about 1771 and in 1776, but on the church estates of the convent of the Order of St Clare (the Franciscan nuns) of Kalisz, namely in the Gliny Olędry and the Lubieńskie Ol., lived Poles and Catholics[^214]. In the foundation charter for the latter settlement the settlers are described as honest Olęders *...of the honest faith and rites of the Roman Catholic church...*, being Poles now and *hereafter*.[^215].

In Poroże Małe, in the district of Kalisz, which arose in or before 1775, settled settlers whose names and confession are unknown.

In the Kosmowskie or Szadokierskie Olędry, which arose in the years 1775-1784 in the same district, there lived, according to the settlement agreement (foundation charter), Olęders with Polish names: Marcin Kapliński, Jan ? Droga,

[^208]: Cf. n. 64.

[^209]: Cf. n. 65 and Tab. 3c; Py. Zm. 1, fols. 482, 532 and 536.

[^210]: Cf. nn. 68 and 693 and Tab. 3c.

[^211]: Cf. n. 62.

[^212]: Cf. n. 72 and Tab. 3c.

[^213]: Cf. n. 60 and Tab. 3c.

[^214]: Cf. n. 73.

[^215]: Cf. n. 78.

[p. 109]

*...who make the contract in their own name and that of others...* (joined later by the honest Antoni Morawski and Bartłomiej Masłowski, who had earlier lived on the Turek estates), who were, however, Protestant Lutherans[^216].

In the Kuszyńskie or Jaszczurowo Olędry, in turn, also in the district of Kalisz, 7 colonists settled, 4 with German and 3 with Polish names, Protestant Lutherans. They took up "the forest or deserta" (empty land) Jaszczurowo, belonging to Kosmów. The settlement agreement was first made with the founder in 1776 by two representatives of the colonists, Michał Gesse and Krzysztof Malus (Mikosz?), and then in the years 1776-1781 3 more settlers were added to it: Marcin Makosz, Paweł Dysza and Piotr Jering[^217].

In the years 1776 and 1781 Wrąbczynek and the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol. were founded. In Wrąbczynek at least 4 settlers settled (3 with German names and 1 with a Polish one), Protestant Lutherans. The settlers of the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol., Jakub Krysa/Kriese (sołtys), Wojciech Kurtz and Krzysztof Szultz (assessors), were settled on overgrown, probably wooded, land, since the second *konotacja* contained the information: *Since the Rąbczynek Holendry, their free years for the rumunek being out, have today received the law under which they are to remain in their dues, so that these too, settled together with them, are not raised to higher dues than the Holędrzy, they are ascribed an equalisation with those, so that they* [belong] *to the dues set out here*.

In the *asekuracja* of 20 XI 1781 of S. Dobrski, plenipotentiary of Turno, starost of Pietrzyków, besides confirming that the labour services had been done, the Olęders are promised that they will obtain the royal law (that is the king's confirmation of the foundation charter issued by the starost-tenant) once they have paid a contribution for the purpose, and it is stated *that the Germans, who have been measured out on the lord's demesne farm, will be bound to pay the remaining half of the grungielt*[^218]. In the Wrąbczyńskie Ol. 3 settlers settled, with German and Polish names, Protestant Lutherans. *Settled on the Wrąpczyn wood, three new inhabitants give fl.* [silver złoty] *32*; according to the charter of 1776, *and also the Rąbczyn Holendry, who hold half of the tenth* [9½] *huba cleared from the deserta in the forest, with Bogusław Szeyder, sołtys, Jakub Wróbel and Michał Szpryngier, assessors*[^219].

In the charter for the Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol., set up in 1777 and then before and in 1784 in the district of Konin, 3 settlers with German names are named: Walenty Ginter (probably the same as the Olęder who had settled earlier, in the years 1767-1770, in Wielołęka), Bogusław Gryger and Jonat Zamer, the sołtys and assessors; but others with German and Polish names, Lutherans and Catholics, also lived there[^220].

The settlers settled in 1778 in the Pyzdrskie Olędry were Poles and Catholics, partly burghers of Pyzdry and partly peasants of the surrounding area. In the commune book of this village in the years 1778-1793 they appear with the following names:

[^216]: Cf. nn. 75-76.

[^217]: Cf. n. 77.

[^218]: Cf. nn. 82, 83 and 84 and Tab. 3a, which records only Catholics, 223, in Wrąbczynek, and only dissenters, 99, in the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol.

[^219]: Cf. n. 82.

[^220]: Cf. n. 86.

[p. 110]

Baszcz, Bończyk, Baczkiewicz, Baranowski, Broniarczyk, Depczyński, Drzewiecki, Frankowski, Galeski, Konieczny, Kotecki, Kruszyński, Łyszkiewicz, Modrzejewski, Nowiński, Rodowski, Sobierajski, Stencel[^221].

In the Jarantowskie Olędry, founded in 1778 in the district of Kalisz, in the parish of Lipe, on a noble estate, probably Lutherans and Catholics, Germans and Poles, lived[^222].

In Wierzchy, or the Tomickie Nw. Ol., which arose in 1781 in the district of Kalisz, 12 settlers settled with Polish (8) and German (4) names (Jan Bukowiecki, Jędrzej Markwat, Kazimierz Ignaczak, Stanisław Opielak, Wojciech Szulc, Wojciech Korbal, Krystian and Wojciech Janke, Józef Szumański, Maciej Jedwab, Karol Białeszyński and Karol Szewc), most probably Catholics[^223].

In the Kazimierskie or Niniewskie St. and Nw. Ol., founded in the same district in the years 1782 and 1790, lived Germans, Lutherans, who left behind a church and a cemetery. The locator of the latter was Georg [Jerzy] Müller[^224].

Settlers of the same nation and confession settled in the Dzierzbińskie – Zamęty and Zdanowiec Olędry, also founded in the district of Kalisz, in 1783, by one foundation charter, but forming separate communes. Two settlers, with names of German sound, are known from the charter: Marcin Szynwald and Jan Fitz, Lutherans. Their confession is also shown by the grant in the charter of land for the *bakałarze* (teachers/schools) and for a common cemetery "*for the dissenters*"[^225].

In the Plewińskie (Stare) and Nowe, or Kuźnickie, Olędry (founded before 1783 and in 1793), in the district of Kalisz, 19 settlers settled (14 with German and 5 with Polish names), Lutherans. They included Bogusłw Werner and Paweł Pus, who made the foundation agreement with the founder, Antoni Kawiecki, on behalf of the whole commune, and Krystian Hek, Rejmand Gaysler, Marcin Nyps, Jędrzej Fire, Bogumił Konczak, Krystian Vizur (?), Marcin Tulik (or Talik), Jan Reska, Bogusław Lang, Bogusław Litman, Samuel Bigiel, Jan Gierat, Bogusław Stary Werner, Maciej Piwecki, Jan Ściernisko, Bogusław Glapa and Krzysztof Figa. Their Protestant confession is confirmed by the village's receiving land for a *szkolny* (teacher)[^226].

In the settlements set up in 1783, Dębniałki or the Bolimowskie/Brudzewskie Ol. and the Piskory Olędry, in the district of Kalisz, the first joint settlement charter of 1784 names only these settlers: 2 with German names (Mikołaj Hayschler, sołtys, and Michał Helman, assessor) and 1 with a Polish name (Franciszek Borowy, assessor), Catholics and Lutherans. There were, however, more settlers (6 Olęders received free years to 1791 and 2 to 1792); the dissenters were also to receive plots for a burial ground and for a teacher (school), common to both settlements[^227]. It is also known that 11 settlers with German and Polish names, Catholics and Protestant Lutherans, were settled in the Piskory or Lipeckie Olędry[^228].

[^221]: Cf. n. 92 and APP. A. m. Pyzdry I/63.

[^222]: Cf. n. 94.

[^223]: Cf. n. 95.

[^224]: Cf. n. 96.

[^225]: Cf. nn. 98-99; APP. Kalisz. Gr. 448 (1783), fols. 317, 319-320; Kal. Gr. 451 (1783), fol. 217.

[^226]: Cf. n. 101.

[^227]: Cf. n. 102.

[^228]: Cf. n. 104.

[p. 111]

In the Oborskie Stare Ol., or Wronów, and the Nowe Ol., which arose in 1783 and before 1788 in the district of Kalisz, Protestants, Germans, also or exclusively lived[^229].

In the settlements of the Orlińskie (Orlina) Małe and Duże Olędry, in the district of Konin, 19 settlers settled in 1784 (most with Polish names), Germans and Poles, Catholics and Lutherans. They included among others: Bogusław Krol, Michał Arent, Franciszek Maciejowski, Maciej Walczak, Maciej Jarmuszkiewicz, Wawrzyniec Kośmicki, Micha Wynt, Krystian Tenus, Johannn Samuel Weychnert(?), Franciszek Organiściak, Grzegorz Gojak (?) with their families. In 1790 there were 29 Catholics and 36 Protestants[^230].

In the Lisieckie Ol., in the starosty of Konin, in the Lisiec key (founded in 1783-1784 and before 1789), lived Germans, dissenters (20 in number in 1791)[^231], perhaps of the Reformed confession.

The inhabitants of Stara Huta – the Grójeckie Olędry (founded perhaps as early as about 1777, and certainly in the years 1785 and 1789), in the same district, were Germans and probably also Poles, Catholics, 51 persons in 1790[^232].

In the Siąszyckie or Złotowskie Olędry, which arose probably about 1786, and certainly before 1790, one of the two settlers known from the sources, Łukasz Łyszkowski, was an emigrant who had come back from abroad[^233]. Both settlers were probably Catholics.

In the Mycielińskie Stare and Nowe Olędry (today Kazala St. and Nw.), founded in the years 1786-1788 in the district of Kalisz, in the parish of Kościelec, on the noble estate of Mycielin, whose locators were Jakub Tonka and Godfryd Legner, 6 settlers in all settled (1 with a German name, 3-4 with Polish names and 1 whose name is unknown), most probably Protestants, since they were to receive land for a school[^234].

In the Stropieszyńskie, or Radzany and Gostynie, Olędry, in the district of Kalisz (founded in the years 1787 and 1791), the settlers were Germans and Protestant Lutherans. They were expressly described as Germans in the foundation charter. Their nationality is also confirmed, indirectly, by a separate charter for the honest Marcin Werner and his successors for the Bugaj inn, which lay on the Kalisz road, binding him, should the property be sold, to present as purchaser a man who knew the Polish language. The Protestant confession of the inhabitants of these settlements is shown by the assignment in the foundation charter of 6 XII 1787 of land for the *szkolny* (the teacher). The foundation charter of 1791, which at the same time confirmed and supplemented the first, also added to the commune the married couple Józef and Elżbieta Gierecki, manor servants, settled under a separate charter on ½ huba of ploughland [most probably Catholics and Poles]. In granting the Olęders at the same time land for a school and a cemetery, the charter confirmed their German nationality and Protestant confession[^235].

[^229]: Cf. n. 105.

[^230]: Cf. n. 106 and Tab. 3c.

[^231]: Cf. Tab. 3c.

[^232]: Cf. n. 88 and Tab. 3.

[^233]: Cf. n. 109.

[^234]: Cf. nn. 111-112.

[^235]: Cf. nn. 113-114.

[p. 112]

In the Ciążeńskie Olędry, on the other hand, created before 1788-1789 in the district of Pyzdry, in the Ciążeń key of the mensal estates of the bishopric of Poznań, there lived, according to the revision of the Ciążeń key of 1793, 4 Olęders (3 with Polish names: Jan Macieje[w]ski, Mateusz Czajka, Marcin Ławicki, and 1 with a German name: Augustyn Maytler), Poles, Catholics[^236].

The inhabitants of 8 smaller Olęder settlements that arose before 1789, and at the latest before 1792, in the district of Konin, varied in confession and nation. In 1791 in Bukowa/Bukowe (Bukowe Budy), or the Łomowskie Olędry, lived only Catholics, 74 in number; in Imielno lived 21 Catholics and 42 dissenters; in the Myszakówko Olędry 16 dissenters; in Stara Huta – the Zagórowskie Ol. 37 Catholics[^237]. In 1790, in turn, 21 Catholics and 20 dissenters lived in the Kowalewskie Olędry[^238], and the Jaroszewskie Olędry were probably inhabited by Catholics and Lutherans, Poles and Germans[^239].

In the three hearths of the Olęder settlement of Świdle Nowa Kolonia, founded in 1790 in the district of Kalisz by the old village of Świdle, there lived, it seems, people of the Protestant and the Catholic confession, of German and Polish nationality.

Summing up the information above on the confession, nationality and origin of the inhabitants of the 58 Olęder settlements that arose in the years 1746-1789 and 1791 in the Pyzdry Forest, it must be said that information stating directly the ethnic belonging of the Olęders occurs in the sources only three times. There are, however, indirect indications of the ethnic composition. One of them, besides confession, is their first names and surnames, often distorted by being Polonised (first names) and written as they sounded (surnames). In some cases, from the form of the names, Polish Protestants or German Catholics, or Polonised Germans, may be guessed at among the settlers. However, if not all of the colonists, then certainly a fair number of them also understood Polish, as is shown, indirectly, by the fact that most of the foundation charters and other documents or records were written in Polish. Other indications pointing indirectly to the nationality of the Olęders are the dialect of German they used (known chiefly from the pre-war research on part of the German minority in Poland) and the kind and size of the measures of land (rods), which also point to their place of origin, though not in the sense of a locality but of a region or country.

For the localities from which the Olęder settlers came are, for the period that concerns us, with very few exceptions, unknown. In 16 of the 28 Olęder settlements in the district of Konin, inhabited exclusively or partly by dissenters, in the light of the information on their confession, and also of the dialect of Low German (*plattdeutsch*) they used and the kind and size of their measures of land (rods), emigrants may be guessed at from the borderland of Greater Poland and Silesia and from the older Olęder settlements that had arisen in western Greater Poland, that is in the voivodeship of Poznań (from the area of Zbąszyń

[^236]: Cf. n. 123.

[^237]: Cf. Tab. 3c.

[^238]: Cf. Tab. 3c.

[^239]: Cf. n. 121.

[p. 113]

and Nowy Tomyśl)[^240]. From these lands, chiefly from the districts of the voivodeship of Kalisz (from towns and villages), came the Catholic and Polish settlers who lived, exclusively or partly, in a dozen or so settlements, especially those that were church property, but in a few cases also on noble ones[^241]. It seems that already in the first decades after the settlements were founded the confessional and ethnic composition of the villages changed, though to a very limited extent, as their inhabitants moved, because, among other things, of their personal freedom and the sales of land, and through marriages with women and men of neighbouring villages, especially rent-paying or Olęder ones. They were inhabited, at least half of them, wholly or partly, by German Protestant settlers, and afterwards, until 1945, by their descendants, who sometimes (especially in the settlements mixed in confession and nation) adopted Catholicism and in time became Polonised.

In three Olęder settlements, besides peasants, petty nobles also lived, though mostly on special terms, not being part of the peasant communes of the Olęder settlements and not subject to their self-government, or not willing to submit to it. Thus in 1791 one Różański lived under Olęder law on the land of the village of Ceków, a royal property[^242]. In 1792 there lived in the Białobłockie or Królikowskie Ol. Mikołaj Mąkowski, who then summoned before the landowners' court Count F. Stadnicki, the owner, the Olęders Józef and Jakub Hersa, brothers, Andrzej Langa, former sołtys, and Józef Koźmiński, commissary of the Królików estate, and other persons, for damage to his sown crops and pastures, for verbal and physical insult and for being treated like a peasant by the sołtys, and for the violent taking of his oxen. In reply Count F. Stadnicki summoned the same M. Mąkowski before the landowners' court, holding that by his marriage to an Olęder widow he was subject (like her previous husband) to Olęder law in matters with the sołtys and assessor and the persons of the commune, with the threat of a money fine for summoning them wrongly before the landowners' court[^243]. In Świdle, in turn, but not in the Świdle Ol., in the years 1789-1792, the noble Hieronim Nieszkowski held under a contract, that is under Olęder law, 5½ hubas and 3 morgen of land measured with the rod of 7½ ells. He remained, however, outside the Olęder commune created in 1790[^244].

[^240]: Cf. Z. Chodyła, *Pochodzenie, nazwiska, wyznanie i narodowość mieszkańców osad olęderskich założonych w latach 1700–ok. 1783 na obszarze dzisiejszego powiatu nowotomyskiego w XVIII w.* [The origin, names, confession and nationality of the inhabitants of the Olęder settlements founded in the years 1700–about 1783 in the area of the present district of Nowy Tomyśl, in the eighteenth century], "Przegląd Nowotomyski", No. 2 (18), April – June 2011, pp. 3-22.

Z. Chodyła, "Kronika Miasta Poznania" 2002/2, pp. 69–80.
