[^243]: APP, Księgi sądów ziemiańskich [Books of the landowners' courts]. Pyzdry. Zm. 1, fols. 482, 532 and 536 (summonses of 1792 and 30 VI 1792).

[^244]: Cf. n. 104; APP, Kalisz. Gr. 482 (1791), fols. 177-178 and 172-172v; (act of *komplanacja* (settlement) at the manor of Przespolew, 4 IV 1791); Tab. 22, Kalisz (Table of hearths and population of 1789), fol. 132v; Tab. 2 (Table of the offering of 10 (20) gr. of 1789), fol. 21; APP, Kalisz. Gr. 486 (1792), fols. 54-58v (decree of the court of the assembly of 1792, obl. 6 VII 1792).

[p. 114]

# 4. The legal status and the administrative and judicial organisation of the Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest

In the Olęder settlements founded in the Pyzdry Forest, the legal relation of the first settlers and of the later inhabitants of the Olęder settlements to the feudal lord (the founder of the settlement and his successors), the rules of the Olęders' life together in the settlements, the organisation and functions of their self-government, and finally their obligations to institutions and third persons (among them the state and the Church) were set by the foundation charters of the settlements, also called laws (*prawa*), consents (*konsensy*) and letters (*listy*). Formally they were unilateral acts, issued and signed by the lords, the founders of the settlements; in fact they were agreements made between them and the locators acting on behalf of whole communes as their representatives, or, mostly, with the settlers as a body. Sometimes they could also be oral, or written but preliminary, or individual, and only in time were they replaced by final written documents common to the whole commune[^245]. So it was in Wielołęka, Konary, the Lubieńskie Ol., the Przespolewskie Ol., Wrąbczynek and the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol., the Gadowskie St. and Nw., and the Dzierzbińskie-Zdanowiec and Zamęty Ol., where the settlers, settling gradually, first obtained individual contracts fixing their free years, or, as in the Mycielińskie St. and Nw. Ol., preliminary charters[^246], which were replaced only later by collective charters (agreements) of a final character. The Olęder commune had to pay for the issue of a collective charter; the foundation charter for the Cekowskie St. Ol. of 1768 states that the settlers "*declare they will give for the contract a minted thaler each, and have already given one red złoty towards it*"[^247].

The Olęders, unlike the peasants owing labour service, were always personally free. This was expressly confirmed in the collective foundation charter for the Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol. (*I have resolved... to give emphyteutic law to the Gadowskie Holendry, to free people subject to no subjection, brought in while my husband was still alive... and coming in during my possession... according to its contracts*)[^248]. That those who settled in the Olęder settlements that concern us were people

[^245]: W. Rusiński, *Osady tzw. „olędrów”*, p. 40; Z. Chodyła, *Osadnictwo olęderskie w Wielkopolsce (1597-1793)*.

[^246]: Cf. nn. 41, 54, 60, 78, 82, 86, 98.

[^247]: Cf. nn. 111-112.

[^248]: Cf. n. 86.

[p. 115]

personally free is also shown, indirectly, by their being described as *honest* (*uczciwi*) in the foundation charters and other records for: the Pyzdrskie Ol., Białobłockie Ol., Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol. and Lubieńskie Ol., Cekowskie Nw. Ol., Kosmowskie Ol., Plewińskie Nw. Ol., and the Stropieszyńskie, or Radzany and Gostynie, Ol.[^249]

The Olęders' subjection as to the land showed itself in their rights in rem to the land, through the definition of the forms of holding it and the conditions of the freedom to alienate and sell farms, in their rights of use on the lord's land (the so-called servitudes), and in their dependence on the manor's monopolies (chiefly that of propination). The most important of them, the right to the land, took in Greater Poland in the eighteenth century (apart from a few exceptions of emphyteusis in the sense of a short-term lease of land, for several decades) in principle the form of emphyteusis in the sense of a lease unlimited in time, perpetual, tied to the requirement that the holding of land first be bought by entry money, which once the entry money was paid turned in practice into a perpetual rent tenure (German *Erbzinsrecht*). This was guaranteed by the emphyteutic-perpetual charters, tied to the prior payment to the manor of entry money (*grundgeld*) for the land received. The sources show that the following settlements enjoyed perpetual rent tenure: the Pyzdrskie Ol. (in which, according to the census of 1789, lived *entry-fine holders, rent-payers* [settled] *on the forests of the town of His* [Royal] *Majesty, Pyzdry*), Wrąbczynek and the Wrąbczyńskie/Wrąbczynkowskie Ol., the Białobłockie Ol. (and in them at least the brothers Józef and Jakub Hersa), the Borowieckie St. and Nw. Ol. (which, however, had not paid the *grundgeld* by 1789), the Lubieńskie or Biała Ol. (where the Olęders settled *by the right of free purchase*), the Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol. (who had still to pay *the grungielt customary among the Holendry*), the Grądy St. and Nw. Ol., Konary or the Konarskie Ol., Wielołęka (where in 1791 the settlers received a second emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter), the (Ciemierowskie) Stare Ol. (described as *a rent-paying, entry-fine village*), the Cekowskie St. and Nw. Ol., the Dębniałki and Piskory Ol., the Dzierzbińskie – Zamęty and Zdanowiec Ol. (whose inhabitants had earlier obtained individual charters), and at least partly the Jarantowskie Ol., the Kazimierskie St. and Nw. Ol., the Kosmowskie Ol., the Kuszyńskie or Jaszczurowo Ol., the Mycielińskie St. and Nw. Ol., the Oborskie St. and Nw. Ol., the Plewińskie (St.) and Nw. Ol., the Przespolewskie Ol., the Stropieszyńskie or Radzany and Gostynie Ol., Wierzchy or the Tomickie Nw. Ol. and the Zbierskie Ol. (where too, in these settlements, the census of 1789 names *Holendry and entry-fine holders, Olendry and entry-fine holders* or *loose entry-fine holders*)[^250]. The foundation charters for some settlements give the amount of the entry money. In the Cekowskie St. Olędry and the Mycielińskie St. and Nw. Ol. the settlers paid 8 Polish złoty of gruntgielt, that is entry money, per huba; in the Stropieszyńskie or Radzany and Gostynie Ol. (where, according to the charter of 1787, they gave on this account 18 złp. per morgen of ready land or meadow and 3 złp. per morgen of overgrown land, in three instalments, by 1789); in the Wierzchy Ol. (*where they were given a receipt for paying 24 ducats of grundgeld in 1786*)[^251].

[^249]: Cf. nn. 91, 65, 86, 78, 58, 75-76, 101, 113-114.

[^250]: APP, Tab. 46 Pyzdry, fols. 478v-479; cf. nn. 82, 65, 70, 78, 86, 72, 60-61, 54-56, 58 and 74, 102, 98, 94, 97. 75-76, 77, 111-112, 105, 101, 40, 113-114, 95 and 50; Tab. 22. Kalisz, fols. 26v, 260, 264 and 348; Tab. 23. Kalisz, fol. 82v.

[^251]: Cf. nn. 58, 111-112, 113-114, 95.

[p. 116]

Under a law of 1775, emphyteutic charters were to be issued on stamped paper, with a fiscal fee of 1 grosz. If this condition was not met, the official validity of the charter could be legally questioned, even by its issuer himself. This was what happened in the Pyzdrskie Ol., and it became the cause of a long lawsuit between their inhabitants and the magistrate of the town of Pyzdry, which had perhaps deliberately issued the settlers a charter on ordinary paper[^252].

Be that as it may, the ownership of the land assigned to the Olęders was not full but usufructuary and divided, since its legal owner was still the lord, who, however, had no right to remove an Olęder from it as long as he used it in accordance with the provisions of the charter. The Olęder was bound to take such care of the land as if it were his property, and could dispose of it freely as long as he paid the lord the dues owed and paid the entry money mentioned. In Greater Poland this was paid only once, on obtaining the right of perpetual possession of the land, in a lump sum, exceptionally in instalments. It was a payment for the land allotted[^253]. For this reason the peasants – Olęders who had paid the entry money also obtained freedom of alienation: of transferring the ownership of the land by gift to their natural heirs, and by transactions of sale and purchase to other persons, on condition of obtaining the manor's consent and paying the lord a fee (*wadium*) of one grosz in ten of the sale price of the farm. This was stated expressly in the charters for: the Pyzdrskie Ol. (freedom to sell farms with the magistrate's consent, on payment of a *vadium* of one grosz in ten), Wrąbczynek and the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol. (the Olęders may sell, give or exchange the lands they have paid entry money for, cleared and built on, after informing the manor), the Lubieńskie Ol., the Orlińskie D. and M. Ol., the Cekowskie St. Ol. (the buildings and live stock belong to the farmers, who may sell them *...should any of them not like it in this country*), the Cekowskie Nw. Ol. (permission to sell farms with the manor's consent, on giving it one grosz in ten of the sale price,

[^252]: In the complaint of the Olęders Mateusz Konieczny, sołtys, and Józef Baranowski and Jakub Bończyk, assessors and plenipotentiaries of the commune, against the magistrate of the town of Pyzdry, there is the information that the magistrate, taking advantage of the fact that they, "..not being enlightened, as unlearned little peasants", had received from the town council an improperly written foundation charter, since it was not on stamped paper, and for that reason the chancery of the municipal court of Pyzdry would not accept it for entry (*oblata*), gave them, in place of the old charter they had handed in, one on stamped paper, but with the signatures of its own members and without the signatures of the commonalty [the guild elders], and demanded a second payment for it. Now, they said, it wanted to abolish this law and take it away from them by deceit, spreading it about that this was because of the objection of the commonalty, which "knew everything", although they had already paid the town the entry money for the holdings of land they had received, acquiring a perpetual rent tenure of them. To this end it informed the Olęders that it had once obtained a decree of the starost's court abolishing this foundation charter, which had, after all, earlier been confirmed by the king. Besides, attempts were made in various other ways to drive the Olęders out of their farms, among others by cutting the deeds of sale of farms out of the sołtys's record and taking from them the original law kept in that record, and by a raid of citizens of Pyzdry with 15 horses, acts of violence committed under the pretext of taking recruits, and other abuses. Accordingly, the Olęders declared they would pursue the matter before the Referendary Court. That proceeding, which ended in a just judgment, is the subject of other records (a royal letter of safe-conduct for the Olęders, complaints of the Olęders, powers of attorney for the court from the Olęder commune, summonses, decrees, correspondence and others, of 1790), with their call numbers, titles and the pages of the edition of the Ks. RKor., in: Z. Chodyła, *Osadnictwo olęderskie w Wielkopolsce (1597-1793)*, Annex I/5.

[^253]: W. Rusiński, *Osady tzw „ olędrów”*, p. 44.

[p. 117]

the sołtys and the assessors being responsible for the land not being left without a successor farmer by a farmer moving away), the Dzierzbińskie – Zamęty and Zdanowiec Ol., the Kosmowskie Ol. (whoever wishes to move away is to settle another Olęder on his farm with the knowledge of the sołtys and the assessors, *and of the whole commune*), the Plewińskie Nw. Ol. (whoever moves away is to settle another Olęder on his farm with the knowledge of the sołtys and the assessors and *of the whole commune*, otherwise the others will answer for him), the Wierzchy Ol. (freedom to sell farms after informing the manor) and the Zbierskie Ol.[^254].

The settlement charters for the Olęders limited the circle of buyers of farms to people of equal condition (that is above all personally free peasants): in the Lubieńskie Ol. (a farm may be sold after informing the manor, *without reducing the sowing on the huba and without ruining or carrying away the buildings...*, on payment of one grosz in ten to the manor, *...to one equal in his estate...*, to burghers, but not to nobles)[^255], and in the Zbierskie Ol. (they may sell the lands to people of equal condition, give, donate or exchange them *without any claim of the lord*)[^256]. Transactions in farms, with the sellers and buyers and the conditions and prices of the sales, are confirmed by their entries in the village (sołtys) books of the Pyzdrskie and the Dzierzbińskie – Zdanowiec and Zamęty Ol.[^257]

The right of inheritance in the emphyteutic settlements in the Olęder villages of the Pyzdry Forest was probably held – as in the Olęder settlements in the districts of Poznań, Kościan and Gniezno, for which the relevant data survive in the foundation charters – by all the natural heirs of a deceased farmer, women included (in an order set by the degree of kinship). If an Olęder had no offspring or nearest relatives (brother and sister), the farm he tilled, with all its equipment, was to return to the disposal of the manor.[^258]

In the foundation charters surviving for 37 of the 58 Olęder settlements founded in the Pyzdry Forest, with the exception of those for 2 settlements, the principle of the Olęders' solidarity and their collective responsibility for the dues to the manor is not stressed. In the charter for the Stropieszyńskie, or Radzany and Gostynie, Ol., this principle is expressed in general terms by the stress on the punctual payment of the rents and *other dues*[^259]. For a dozen or so of the other settlements, however, it appears implicitly in statements that speak of the joint payment of the manor's dues through the sołtys, or the sołtys and the assessors, who were made responsible for their collective payment, which will be discussed in detail below.

The law in rem also included, as confirmed in the charter: 1) rights of use on the lord's land – that is servitudes of pasture (the basis

[^254]: Cf. nn. 91, 82, 78, 106, 56, 98, 75-76, 105, 101, 95 and 50.

[^255]: Cf. n. 78.

[^256]: Cf. n. 50.

[^257]: APP, Akta miast wielkopolskich [Records of the towns of Greater Poland]. Akta m. Pyzdry I/63; SAB 803.

[^258]: W. Rusiński, *Osady, tzw. „olędrów”*, pp. 49-50; Z. Chodyła, *Osadnictwo olęderskie w Wielkopolsce (1597-1793).*

[^259]: Cf. n. 113.

[p. 118]

of stock-raising), from which the Olęders drew profits that were an important addition to the income from their farms, 2) the lord's occasional economic help, 3) the manor's monopolies.

Under the servitudes of pasture the inhabitants – farmers in the Olęder settlements could graze cattle or pigs on the lord's land, on condition of making a certain payment or giving a due in grain. In the Dębniałki Ol. and the Piskory Ol. the settlers received in a separate charter pasture in the forests and the use of the pond at Piskory, in return for each of the 16 farmers ploughing within the year 10, and in all 150, *laski* (rods) of the manor's ploughland on the Bilawy "*for fallow, spring crop and strip*"[^260].

The lords' occasional economic help for the Olęders took the form of material and non-material benefits, temporary or long-term, which they received from them. During their free years they could use free of charge, freely or to a set degree and extent, every kind of building and firewood on the land taken up for their farms. In the Cekowskie Nw. Ol. they obtained in the foundation charter help in firewood and building timber on their own huba, and after fires they were to receive timber for rebuilding from the Ceków forest; they could also dig clay on land set aside for it[^261]. In the Stropieszyńskie, or Radzany and Gostynie, Ol. they were granted timber for building a school[^262]. In the Orlińskie D. and M. Ol., and also in the Wierzchy Ol., they obtained help in the building timber cleared from their holding[^263]. In the Grądy St. and Nw. "*Every Olęder, before selling off the timber, is to build himself properly and in line with the others within four years...*"[^264].

The regular and joint payment of the dues to the lord, the state and the Church, the carrying out of other administrative and judicial tasks, and the keeping of the peace in the Olęder settlements of the Pyzdry Forest were ensured, as in other Olęder villages, by an elected self-government. It consisted of the sołtys and the assessors, elected by the commune assembly of all the farmers (sometimes called the *gmin* or *gromada*). In administrative matters the sołtys and the assessors were, as was said above, to see that the settlers built as quickly as possible, properly and on a line (Orlińskie D. and M. Ol.[^265]), and to watch that *...every discipline and obedience of the said Dutch communes* was kept (Gadowskie St. and Nw. and probably the Smuszewskie Ol.)[^266].

The existence of a sołtys, and often also of assessors, is clearly confirmed in the foundation charters or other records in 21 of the 58 Olęder settlements that concern us. Namely: in the district of Pyzdry in the Ciążeńskie Ol. (sołtys), Wrąbczynek (sołtys and assessors) and the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol. (sołtys and assessors); in the district of Konin in the Białobłockie Ol. (sołtys and assessors), the Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol. (sołtys and assessors), the Grądy St. and Nw. Ol., Konary Ol. (sołtys), the Orlińskie D. and M. Ol.

[^260]: Cf. n. 103.

[^261]: Cf. n. 74.

[^262]: Cf. nn. 113-114.

[^263]: Cf. 106 and 95.

[^264]: Cf. n. 72.

[^265]: Cf. n. 106.

[^266]: Cf. n. 86.

[p. 119]

(sołtys and assessor) and Wielołęka (sołtys and assessors); in the district of Kalisz in the Cekowskie St. Ol. (where the sołtys was described as *wójt*) and Nw. Ol. (sołtys and assessors), the Dzierzbińskie – Zamęty and Zdanowiec Olędry (where each settlement had a separate sołtys and assessors), the Kosmowskie or Szadokierskie Ol. (sołtys and assessors), the Stropieszyńskie or Radzany and Gostynie Ol. (sołtys and assessors), the Wierzchy Ol. (sołtys) and the Zbierskie Ol. (sołtys)[^267].

The sołtys and the assessors were on the whole elected every year by the commune assembly and confirmed by the manor, though there were sometimes departures from this rule, on the whole short-lived. In Wrąbczynek and the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol. the sołtys and the assessors were elected, with the manor's consent, by the commune assemblies (meetings of the Olęder farmers) of both villages; in the Pyzdrskie Ol., according to the foundation charter of 1778, the farmers were to elect from among themselves every 3 years, on the first Monday after St Adalbert (23 IV), a sołtys and 2 assessors, confirmed by the magistrate; in the Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol. and the Grądy St. and Nw. Ol. the sołtys and two assessors were elected by the *gromada*, that is the *gmin*, in the Stropieszyńskie or Gostynie and Radzany Ol. by the commune assembly, and the manor had to confirm them for a year[^268]. In the Orlińskie M. and D. Ol. the sołtys Bogusław Krol, who had permission for "*...free clearing and rudowanie with his co-samsiads* [!]..." [neighbours], was given Michał Arent to help him[^269], probably as an assessor, which suggests seeing the two of them as the locators of one of these settlements.

In Konary Ol., in turn, the sołtys was elected from 3 candidates put forward by the community[^270]; the two of them not elected to the office probably then served as assessors. In the Dzierzbińskie – Zamęty and Zdanowiec Ol. there were separate sołtyses and assessors, elected every year on 11 XI; in the Kosmowskie Ol., the Plewińskie St. and Nw. Ol., the Stropieszyńskie or Radzany and Gostynie Ol. and the Wierzchy Ol. the sołtys was also elected every year and approved by the manor; in the last of these, in the first year, the sołtys Daniel was chosen by the manor (probably because he was the locator), and the assessors by the commune, that is by the commune assembly made up of the farmers[^271].

For his work the sołtys often received an additional endowment in land or other remuneration. Thus in Wrąbczynek, for his labours for the commune, the sołtys received an *ostrów* [allotment, island, part] *of five morgen in each field* [that is the spring, the winter and the fallow field]; in the Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol. and the Grądy St. and Nw. Ol. half a quarter of a huba in addition, free of dues[^272].

As the commune court, the sołtys and the assessors were to keep internal peace and order in the settlements and to settle quarrels and disputes. They judged by Olęder law, whose terms were set by the foundation charters of the individual settlements and, for some of them, also by the *wilkierze* (village ordinances). The *wilkierze* were collections of legal rules issued formally by the feudal owners – the founders of the settlements or their successors – but in fact formulated by the commune assembly

[^267]: Cf. nn. 123, 82; 65, 86, 72, 60-61, 106, 54-56; 58 98, 75-76, 113-114, 95 and 50.

[^268]: Cf. nn. 82, 91, 86, 72., 113-114, 95.

[^269]: Cf. n. 106.

[^270]: Cf. nn. 60-61.

[^271]: Cf. nn. 98, 75-76, 101, 113-114.

[^272]: Cf. nn. 82, 86 and 72.

[p. 120]

(meeting), alone or in agreement with the lord, and they regulated the internal constitution of the Olęder villages. Such a *wilkierz*, a very extensive one (of 41 points), written in German, undated, but most probably from before 1793, survives for the Wielołęka Ol. Because of the limited size of this study its content cannot be discussed in detail. It is worth saying, however, that it regulates such matters as the role of the commune self-government in ensuring harmonious life among neighbours and order and peace in the village, among other things by controlling the movement of persons coming into it from outside; the organisation and working of the commune court; the keeping and watching over of honesty (also by imposing penalties); it set the rules of trade in cattle, horses and other goods, of neighbourly help in rebuilding after fires, the conditions for returning neighbours' livestock seized for damage, help for widows and persons over 70, and the inhabitants' share in founding and maintaining a church and a school after the free years had run out[^273]. A *wilkierz* is also mentioned in the foundation charters for the Zbierskie Ol. and the Dzierzbińskie – Zamęty and Zdanowiec Ol. The first states of it: *They are to be judged by the sołtys according to their law for good conduct, which they will give, alias the wilkier, and from him the wronged party is to have appeal to the manor*[^274]. The second contains this information about it: *They are given a wilkir, which will belong to the charter, and the charter to the wilkir; this, however, is the most principal* [the first] *point in the wilkir: that when there is need they should be ready with their whole heart, with whatever proper weapons they can have, for a sudden need, that is should an enemy break in, for defence, and also for defence [against] fire, should the forest catch fire by some chance*[^275].

From the judgments of the commune court the Olęders had the right of appeal to the manor, that is the lord's court (in the Pyzdrskie Ol. to the court of the magistrate, in the Cekowskie Nw. Ol. and the Zbierskie Ol. to the *dominium* court, that is the lord's), for which the right to judge in criminal cases of robbery, arson, homicide and serious bodily harm, and of course all offences against the lord, was also reserved[^276]. Court fines went half to the manor and half to the court (Cekowskie Nowe Ol.), or to the manor alone[^277].

The lords also guaranteed the Olęders their protection, and promised them and their descendants, in their own name and that of their heirs, to keep the terms of the foundation charters. They sometimes also bound the Olęders not to seek anyone else's protection against them, stating that *should anyone make use of it, he cannot claim payment for his plot*[^278].

[^273]: APP, SAB 789.

[^274]: Cf. n. 50

[^275]: Cf. n. 98.

[^276]: Cf. n. 72 and APP. A. m. Pyzdry I/62: *Protokół rezolwowanych dekretów przez apelację lub zaskarżenie od sołtysa i ławników Hollendrów miasta Pyzdr dziedzicznych przed szlachetny magistrat tegoż miasta jako panów swych i dziedziców poddanych*, 88 fols.; *KSRK (2)*, vol. II, p. 144, no. 548; A. m. Pyzdr I/63: [Court book of the sołtys of the village of Holendry belonging to the town of Pyzdry], 24 fols.; cf. nn. 59, 40; W. Rusiński, *Osady, tzw. „olędrów”*, pp. 42-43; Z. Chodyła, *Osadnictwo olęderskie w Wielkopolsce (1597-1793).*

[^277]: Cf. n. 74.

[^278]: W. Rusiński, *Osady, tzw. „olędrów”*, pp. 42-43; Z. Chodyła, *Osadnictwo olęderskie w Wielkopolsce (1597-1793).*

[p. 121]

Among the members of the self-government the main burden of administrative duties lay on the sołtys. He was above all bound to see to order and good husbandry in the commune, and that the whole commune met its obligations to the manor – rents, labour services and payments to the manor and the Church, and taxes to the state treasury – punctually and in full. It was for him and the assessors to collect these dues (recorded for the Stropieszyńskie or Gostynie and Radzany Ol.). Since the commune was usually jointly responsible for the manor's dues, the commune self-government was bound to remove an Olęder who farmed badly and to put another on the farm (Cekowskie Nw. Ol., Plewińskie St. and Nw. Ol.). The sołtys probably also, with the lord's support, carried out the judgments of the commune court[^279].

Most of the 58 eighteenth-century Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest, of various sizes in their area of land (especially arable), their number of hearths and their population, were independent spatial-economic and administrative-judicial units (communes). They formed separate one-village communes with their own self-government, a sołtys and assessors. Some of them, however, 14 (the Borowieckie St. and Nw., the Grądy St. and Nw. Ol., the Orlińskie D. and M. Ol., the Trąbczyńskie St. and Nw. Ol., the Mycielińskie St. and Nw. Ol., the Plewińskie St. and Nw. Ol., the Stropieszyńskie – Radzany and Gostynie Ol.), formed two-village communes, and in one case the Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol. and probably the Smuszewskie Ol. formed a three-village commune, with a common sołtys and assessors.

Perhaps some of the Olęder villages or communes founded in the Pyzdry Forest had their own seal as a mark of their self-government, with which its organs – the office and the court (or rather the office-court) – sealed their letters and documents, which, as originals or copies, were entered in their own commune books – sołtys books, sometimes town books – and were also sent to other communes[^280]. Commune and sołtys books (containing, among other things, copies of foundation charters, registers of rents and labour services, transactions of sale and purchase of farms, inventories of movables and the Olęders' wills), kept by the commune clerk, who was probably most often the teacher, survive for only three Olęder communes, the Pyzdrskie Ol. and the Dzierzbińskie – Zamęty and Zdanowiec Ol., one common book for the last two[^281]. (Ill. 25)

[p. 122]

[p. 123]

# 5. The economic and social life of the Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest in the years 1746-1793

The technique of tilling the land in the lowland settlements consisted in draining the land by digging, and keeping in good order, a system of large and smaller drainage ditches, and in keeping the arable land, once drained, fit for tillage. In the settlements that lay higher, on dry forest land, tillage consisted in clearing the forests and thickets and then raising the fertility of the soil by manuring (chiefly with dung, provided by well-developed cattle-raising). Sometimes the Olęders took up ploughland already in use, even with buildings and sown crops. Settlements founded on the ready or partly overgrown land of former villages kept the communal three-field layout, tied to the system of tillage then general. With the scattered layout of the farms in the great majority of the settlements, however, the three-field system was individualised, and there was, on the whole, no compulsion to till in common in the individual settlements. The varied and often low fertility of the soils, partly wet, partly (after the forest was cleared) sandy, together with the extensive grain-and-fallow three-field system and moderate manuring (in spite of fairly well-developed stock-raising, especially of cattle), made for rather low yields.

The basic branches of farming in the Olęder settlements of the Pyzdry Forest were crop-growing and stock-raising. In the former, grain predominated. Among the grains rye predominated; oats and barley were also grown, more rarely buckwheat, and among other crops peas and flax. Because of the low fertility of the soils, most farms obtained yields of grain of 3-3½ grains for 1 sown. Meadows and pastures were of great importance, as places for grazing cattle but also for getting fodder for them for the winter in the form of hay. Garden crops were of secondary, supplementary importance: hops, tobacco, potatoes and carrots, chiefly for the household's own use. The sources we have used allow conclusions about the character of crop-growing only to a very limited extent, only from the dues in kind set out below and the information on meadows and pastures. The data given above have been formulated by analogy with other Olęder settlements in Greater Poland for which the relevant concrete data survive[^282].

[^282]: They are given by W. Rusiński, *Osady tzw. olędrów*, pp. 71-72.

[p. 124]

The second important branch of the economy of the inhabitants of the Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest was the raising of animals: cattle, sheep and pigs, and also horses and poultry. This is shown by direct information about permission to keep sheep and cattle, and by indirect information about meadows and pastures, given in the charters for settlements already named and others, namely for: Wrąbczynek and the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol. (where the settlers received 16 morgen of land cleared for meadows, and Jakub Wróbel one and a half morgen for keeping sheep and cattle), the Pyzdrskie Ol. (free pasture for livestock in the forests), the Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol. (permission to raise cattle and other livestock, but without damage to the manor's pastures), Wielołęka (permission to fatten hogs, free pasture for cattle in places set aside), the Cekowskie St. Ol., the Dzierzbińskie – Zamęty and Zdanowiec Ol. (they may keep as many horned cattle as they wish), the Przespolewskie Ol. and the Zbierskie Ol.[^283] The charters for the Cekowskie St. and Cekowskie Nw. Ol. set limits on the number of stock kept. The first allowed *the raising of 25 sheep, and the second the keeping on a huba of 7 cows, 4 horses, 4 oxen, 10 pigs and 30 sheep*[^284]. In the Ciążeńskie Ol. there were fees for pasture: *for a cow 1 zł, for a head of young stock, not counting calves, 18 gr*[^285]. In the Dębniałki and Piskory Ol. the settlers received free pasture in the forests and pastures in return for each of the 16 farmers ploughing within the year 10, and in all 150, *laski* (rods) of the manor's ploughland on the Bilawy, *for fallow, spring crop and strip*. The raising of poultry also played a significant part. This is confirmed indirectly by the information quoted below about dues in kind: in geese, capons and eggs.

The people of the Olęder settlements founded in the Pyzdry Forest drew additional income from non-farming occupations: from bee-keeping, which was allowed by the settlement charters for the Pyzdrskie Ol., Wrąbczynek and the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol., and Wielołęka[^286].

The people of the Olęder settlements founded in the Pyzdry Forest in the years 1746-1792 also most probably took an active part in trade, mostly local and retail, selling chiefly the surplus of their crops (grain) and their stock (cattle, horses, pigs, poultry and dairy produce) and of what they made or obtained in their non-farming occupations (for instance crafts and bee-keeping), and buying in return grain and other foodstuffs, timber, or tools at the markets and fairs in the towns and little towns. The foundation charter for the Cekowskie St. Ol. (allowed trade in salt and bread), and that for the Cekowskie Nw. Ol. of 1774, guaranteed the settlers freedom to trade in hogs, horses, cattle and sheep, and forbade trade in salt and iron[^287]. None of the foundation charters for the other Olęder villages states this expressly, but there is no doubt that the Olęders were guaranteed freedom of trade, since it was chiefly in this way that they could obtain the money to meet their financial obligations to the manor, the state and the Church. Jews were often the intermediaries in the trade in farm produce.

[^283]: Cf. nn. 82, 91, 86, 55-56, 58, 98, 40 and 50.

[^284]: Cf. nn. 58 and 74.

[^285]: Cf. n. 123.

[^286]: Cf. nn. 91, 82 and 55-56.

[^287]: Cf. nn. 58 and 74.

[p. 125]

Gross income was reduced by the unknown costs of the household's own consumption and of investment, and above all by the obligations to the manor, the Church and the state, which decisively determined the value of the net income.

On account of the perpetual, hereditary use of the land, the Olęders settled in the Pyzdry Forest on private estates were bound to render to the lords – the founders of the settlements and their heirs – money rents for the land, dues in kind and labour (the repair of roads, and *tłoki*, seasonal labour service at harvest and haymaking, and other services, set on a yearly basis after the free years had run out), and to buy the manor's drink under the compulsion of propination. They also bore some administrative fees, for instance on the sale of land, mentioned above.

The charters and other records show that the most important burden was the money rent to the manor, paid on the whole once a year, on a set date (mostly 11 XI), and in an amount according to the size of the holding (farm). It was collected from the individual farmers and handed over jointly by the sołtys of the village, against a receipt, to the feudal lord or his representative. Such receipts for rents received, of the years 1769-1783, are known for the Ciemierowskie/Szymanowickie or Stare Ol.[^288] and in the Pyzdrskie Ol. (in their commune or sołtys book[^289]).

For the Cekowskie Nw. Ol. there is an exact list of the payments and the amounts of rent for the individual farmers, named by their surnames: *Krzysztof Stefan, in whose place now Grymberk, from 1 huba 80 zł; Gotfryd Reszka from 1 huba 9 morgen 105 złp 10 gr; Wojciech Pruchno from ½ h. and 5 morgen 46 złp. 20 gr, from 6 I 1782; Krystian Kurcz, in whose place Kups, from 6 I 1785; the rest of the Olęders settled on 1 and ½ h., from 6 I 1787; Hertel from 1 h. 80 złp; Puntzler from 1½ h. 120 złp; Heinc from ¾ h. 60 złp; Szwander from 1¼ h. 100 złp.; Kups from 1 h. 80 złp.; Krzysztof Kowal from ¾ h. 60 złp.; Gajzler from ¾ h. 60 złp.; Jan Paszkowski from 1 h. 80 zł; Grynberg from 2 h. taken up in 1782, 160 zł.*[^290]. A register of rents paid and labour done, and of fines imposed for various offences, together with transactions of purchase and sale of farms, of the years 1792-1813, is known from the village (sołtys) book of the Dzierzbińskie [-Zamęty] Ol.[^291].

The annual rates of rent per huba varied, but were mostly close to one another. Thus it is known that they came to: in the Dębniałki and Piskory Ol. 72 złp; in the Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol. 74 złp.; in the Ciążeńskie Ol., the Pyzdrskie Ol., the Białobłockie Ol., the Borowieckie St. and Nw. O., the Konarskie Ol., Wielołęka, the Cekowskie St. Ol. and the Oborskie Ol. 76 złp.; in the Lubieńskie Ol. 78 złp.; in the Kazimierskie Nw. Ol. 45 złp., but per half-huba; in Wrąbczynek and the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol. and the Cekowskie Nw. Ol. 80 złp.; in the Grądy St. and Nw. Ol. 90 złp.[^292] In the Dzierzbińskie – Zamęty and Zdanowiec Ol., the Kosmowskie Ol., the Kuszyńskie Ol. and the Plewińskie Ol., on the other hand, they were set at *60 tynfs per huba*, and in the Wierzchy Ol. at *30 tynfs per half-huba*[^293]. On the other hand,

[^289]: APP, A.m. Pyzdry I/63.

[^290]: APP, Kalisz Gr. 450, fols. 27-27v and 29-29v.

[^291]: APP, SAB 803.

[^292]: Cf. nn. 102, 86, 123, 91, 65, 70, 60-61, 55-56, 58, 105, 78, 97, 82, 74, 72.

[^293]: Cf. nn. 98, 75-76, 77 and 101.

[p. 126]

in the Przespolewskie Ol. they were set at 12 *reichsstalers*, and in the Zbierskie Ol. at *10 thalers per huba*[^294]. The inhabitants of the Orlińskie D. and M. Ol., the Mycielińskie – Kazala St. and Nw. Ol., and the Stropieszyńskie – Gostynie and Radzany Ol. paid them in an unknown amount[^295]. In the Przespolewskie Ol. in 1779 the first three Olęders settled on Poroże paid *2 red złoty* *(ducats) of rent each and worked a day and a half a year*. Of others, settled later, it is said that they give no rent *since they still enjoy the freedom under their contracts for a time, except the innkeeper (...)*[^296]. The inventory of (a part of) the Przespolewskie Ol. of 1785, which names 22 Olęders, states that seven of them (one of them for part of a holding) had 3-6 free years left in favour of the manor, and so bore no burdens yet; and the inventory of the same of 1786 states that for the *tłoka* (harvest labour) *a barrel of beer must be given* to the Olęders. The table of hearths of this settlement of 1789, in turn, names 25 hearths in it, with the additional explanation: "*...the Hollendry, the New Colony by the name of Poroże Wielkie"; among them "cottages having their ground cleared and paying rent from it" 13, "Huts not yet out of their free years and paying no rent, besides one still without buildings 12 (of these, 7 huts settled in 1789, which began clearing in 1789 and are to pay rent of 36 złp. each in 1796, 2 huts settled, which began clearing in 1787, and rent of 36 złp. each in 1793) and in 1 hearth*"[^297].

Dues in kind were rendered in many, but not all, of the settlements, in oats, poultry and eggs, with the possibility of commuting them into cash at a set rate. Their amounts varied. Thus *2 capons* (fattened, castrated cockerels) were given only in Wrąbczynek and the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol., and *4 wiertele of oats of the Warsaw measure, 4 capons or 2 geese instead, 2 mendle (fifteens) of eggs in the Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol.*[^298]. On the other hand, *4 wiertele of oats or 8 zł, 2 geese or 2 zł, 2 capons or 1 zł 6 gr, a kopa (sixty) of eggs or 24 gr, in all 12 złp.* (1 zł = 30 gr at the time) were given in the Białobłockie Ol., the Borowieckie St. and Nw. Ol., the Grądy St. and Nw. Ol. and the Konarskie Ol.[^299]. In Wielołęka, in turn: first 2 wiertele of oats of the Rychwał measure, 2 capons or 1 goose, and then 4 wiertele of oats or 12 zł, 2 geese or 3 zł, 2 capons or 2 zł, a kopa of eggs or 2 zł, in all 18 złp.[^300]. Further: the farmers were bound to give 4 wiertele of oats, of the new royal measure, and 4 capons or 2 geese once a year in the Lubieńskie Ol., 4 capons or 2 ganders in the Dębniałki and Piskory Ol., and 2 wiertele of oats, 2 hens and 1 goose, and 2 mendle of eggs in the Dzierzbińskie-Zamęty and Zdanowiec Ol.[^301]

The charter for the Stropieszyńskie Ol. of 1791 stressed the punctual payment of the rents and "*other dues*"[^302]. In Wierzchy, or the Nowe Tomickie Ol., the rents and other burdens to the manor were borne in 1789 by the entry-fine Olęders

[^295]: Cf. nn. 106, 111-112, 113-114

[^296]: Cf. n. 40.

[^297]: APP. Tab. 22. Kalisz, fol. 131v.

[^298]: Cf. nn. 82 and 86.

[p. 127]

(*okupnicy luźni*, loose entry-fine holders) and the contract Olęders[^303]. In the Zbierskie Ol. the farmers paid the manor in all 540 złp. of rent a year and gave dues in kind, *18 wiertele of oats and 18 hens*[^304].

Labour services were of secondary importance in the Old Polish Olęder settlements of the Pyzdry Forest. They are indeed named in the foundation charters of many of them, on a yearly basis per huba (or per half-huba), in the form of manual work, varying in kind and in the number of days, chiefly seasonal (the so-called *tłoki*), and of journeys (carting, *podwody*), but often with the possibility of commuting them into money at a set rate.

Labour services are known for: the Ciążeńskie Ol. (6 manual *tłoki* at harvest), Wrąbczynek and the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol. (*to plough 3 morgen, to cart dung 2 days and at harvest to work 3 manual days*)[^305]. Then in: the Białobłockie Ol. (*for digging ditches 4 days or 2 zł during the free years, for the harvest 4 days during the free years or 2 zł, with the scythe 1 day or 1 zł, for mud or dung 1 day or 2 zł 15 gr, for raking 2 days or 1 zł, 1 day for cutting a square sążeń of wood, or ploughing 3 morgen in place of the journey*), in the Borowieckie St. and Nw. Ol. and the Konarskie Ol. (*for digging ditches 6 days or 3 zł, 8 days at harvest, for the scythe 6 days or 6 zł, for raking 2 days or 1 zł, to cut 1 square sążeń for the manor*), the Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol. (*at harvest 4 days, "szarwark* [road labour] *for the pasture, for which a barrel of beer from the manor for the 2 days"*), the Lubieńskie Ol. (*2 tłoki at the manor's order*), Wielołęka (24 days of manual service; for each day not worked, 1 zł with the rent)[^306]. Further in: the Cekowskie St. Ol. (3 days of labour service) and the Cekowskie Nw. Ol. (formerly 3 days, later commuted to *2 capons or 3 larger hens*), the Dębniałki and Piskory Ol. (*3 days of harvest service*), the Dzierzbińskie Ol. – Zamęty and Zdanowiec Ol., the Kosmowskie Ol. and the Kuszyńskie Ol. (*2 days at harvest*), the Oborskie St. and Nw. Ol. (6 days), the Plewińskie St. and Nw. Ol. (*8 days at harvest*), the Przespolewskie Ol. (*3 days of the manor's work, for which they will receive a barrel of beer*), the Stropieszyńskie – Gostynie and Radzany Ol. (*once a year to cut bushes or chop wood for the brickworks, or to renew the ditches, for which each will receive a garniec of beer*), and the Wierzchy Ol. (*8 days of tłoka at harvest from ½ huba and ploughing the fields once in winter and once in summer, carting 2 loads of wood at Easter and Christmas*)[^307].

A significant burden on the inhabitants of the Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest owed to the manor were the journeys (carting), found, however, only in some villages. Thus in: the Pyzdrskie Ol. (journeys to Leszno and Toruń), Wrąbczynek and the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol. (the journey to Poznań or Wschowa, *and when it is not needed, 10 zł*)[^308]. Then in the Białobłockie Ol. and the Grądy St. and Nw. Ol. (*1 journey of 6 miles, with whatever and for whatever the manor orders, with a pair of horses, or 6 zł*), in the Borowieckie St. and Nw. Ol. and the Konarskie Ol. (*1 journey of 12 miles or 2 journeys of 6 miles, with whatever and for whatever the manor directs, a pair of horses, or 12 zł*), the Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol. (*a two-horse journey, at the farthest to Krotoszyn*), Wielołęka

[^304]: Description of the state of the Zbiersk estate on the occasion of its acquisition by Leon Gąsiorowski, of 30 VI 1775. Der III, p. 54 (obl. Konin 31 VII 1775).

[^305]: Cf. nn. 123 and 82.

[^307]: Cf. nn. 58, 74. 102, 98, 75-76, 77, 105, 101, 40, 113-114, 95.

[p. 128]

(*1 journey of 12 miles or 24 zł*)[^309]. Further in: the Oborskie St. and Nw. Ol. (*1 journey or 12 złp. for it*)[^310].

Another serious burden to the manor was the complete compulsion of propination, in force in all the Olęder settlements of the Pyzdry Forest. The Olęders were forbidden, under penalty, to make any drink *or to take it from anyone but the manor*. This was stated expressly, either in general terms or with the penalties for breaking this ban, by the foundation charters for the Pyzdrskie Ol., the Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol., the Lubieńskie Ol., the Orlińskie D. and M. Ol., Wielołęka, the Cekowskie St. Ol., the Cekowskie Nw. Ol. (with the threat of a fine of 40 zł for a garniec of beer and 80 zł for a garniec of vodka), the Oborskie St. and Nw. Ol., the Dębniałki and Piskory Ol. (with the threat of a fine of 2 thalers), the Kosmowskie Ol. (with a fine of the price of 1 "*dry barrel*" and 3 garniec of vodka), the Kuszyńskie Ol., the Mycielińskie Ol. (under threat of paying a "*dry barrel*"), the Plewińskie Ol., the Przespolewskie Ol. and the Wierzchy Ol.[^311] On such occasions too as christenings, weddings and parties, the Olęders were always bound to drink the lord's drink. Different from the compulsion of propination, under which buying drink depended on the settlers' will, was the compulsion to buy a certain amount of drink, and so, indirectly, a compulsion to consume. It was rarely met with in the Olęder villages, but it did occur. So it was in the Oborskie St. and Nw. Ol. and the Wierzchy Ol., whose farmers were bound to buy *½ and 1 barrel of beer at the election of the sołtys*[^312].

In some Olęder settlements there was also a compulsion to buy other goods at the inn in a set amount, for instance in the Lubieńskie Ol. ("*the lord's herrings*" during Lent) and in the Stropieszyńskie – Radzany and Gostynie Ol. (20 herrings for each hearth)[^313].

The inhabitants of all the Olęder settlements founded in the eighteenth century in the Pyzdry Forest were also bound, like the inhabitants of non-Olęder villages, to render to the state taxes in money, paid every year in two instalments (March and September) – the poll tax (*pogłówne*), set in the tariffs for 1775 and 1789-1790, the *hiberna* in the settlements on royal estates in the tariff of 1775, and the tax of the offering (from 1789) of 10 gr. (on noble estates) and 20 gr. (on church estates) – and a tax in kind, the so-called levy (*zsypka*) from the "self-tilling" hearths (in the form of korzec of rye and oats per hearth, varying in the individual charters). Their payment is confirmed in the sources for the royal Cekowskie St. Ol. in 1775 (hearth tax of 126 złp from 18 hearths at a rate of 7 zł per hearth, and 56 for the *hiberna* together with Ceków[^314]), the noble Zbierskie Ol. (which paid 36 złp. of poll tax in 1775[^315]), the Ciemierowskie St. Ol., which in 1790 were in arrears with the payment of *112 złp. of hearth tax in the September instalment*[^316],

[p. 129]

and the Kosmowskie Ol. (receipt for the hearth tax for 2 settlers of the years 1787 and 1788)[^317]. Taxes, or the hearth tax, in general terms or with its kind and amount per hearth, are named in the charters for: the Pyzdrskie Ol. (8 złp a year), Wrąbczynek and the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol. (in general terms); the Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol. (4 zł for each instalment), Wielołęka (in general terms), the Dębniałki and Piskory Ol. (in general terms), the Dzierzbińskie Ol. – Zamęty and Zdanowiec Ol. (7 zł a year), the Oborskie St. and Nw. Ol. (2 zł 16 gr per half-huba), the Stropieszyńskie – Gostynie and Radzany Ol. (in general terms), and the Wierzchy Ol. (3 zł 15 gr a year)[^318]. The amounts paid in one instalment of the hearth tax, also called the chimney tax, according to the number of hearths in the settlements, are given in the table of this tax for three districts of the voivodeship of Kalisz of 1789[^319].

The annual rate of the tax of the offering was set for most of the 58 settlements on the basis of the average income of their owners over the last three (exceptionally two) years. According to the data surviving in the table of the tax of the offering of 10 or 20 groszy of 1789, this tax was paid on the annual rent, on the propination of beer and vodka, and on the dues in rye (counting the korzec at 6 zł)[^320].

The grain levy from the self-tilling hearths was fixed in 1791 and served the needs of the army[^321].

Besides, all the inhabitants of many Olęder settlements, Catholics but also, to a large extent, Protestants, were bound to render to the Roman Catholic Church dues in the form of fees *jura stolae* (that is for baptisms, weddings and funerals), *meszne* (mass dues) and *kolęda* (Christmas offering). The Protestants paid them until 1768, as they often did after the toleration law of that year too, especially when they settled on land formerly settled and later deserted, in Olęder villages that arose on the site of medieval settlements assigned in this respect to Catholic parishes, which was sometimes contained in their foundation deeds (*akty erekcyjne*)[^322]. The foundation charters set out these obligations in general terms, saying that they would be fixed in separate agreements made between the settlers and the parish priests through the manor (that is the feudal founder of the settlement), or else they gave conditions already agreed earlier. Thus it is known that in the Białobłockie Ol. the Olęders paid the parish priest at Królików 4 zł each for a funeral and a wedding and 1 zł 6 gr for a baptism, while in the Borowieckie St. and Nw. Ol. the Catholics of this commune were joined "*...for nearness of place*" to the parish church of Grodziec, "*...to which, until the superiors' taxa* [that is, the fixing of the amounts by the higher church authority] is made", they were to pay 4 zł each for a funeral and a wedding and 1 zł 6 gr for a baptism; the Protestants, on the other hand, having freedom of religion guaranteed by law, until the number

[p. 130]

of dissenters grew and the founder established a church for them, could attend *whatever church they wished*[^323]. In the Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol., in turn, the Olęders of different religions gave the parish church of Dzierzbin *2 wiertele of oats of the Warsaw measure and paid for the funeral of an old person 4 zł, of a young person or child 2 zł, for a baptism 1 zł, for the three banns 18 gr, for a wedding 4 zł, for the churching 6 gr). "...and should the dissenters wish to keep their rites in the church of their religion, they shall be free to do everything; nevertheless they promise to give the contribution now settled and agreed to the Catholic Church voluntarily, without any quarrel or dispute*"[^324]. In the Grądy St. and Nw., as in the Borowieckie St. and Nw. Ol. belonging to the same owner, the Catholics were joined to the nearby parish church at Grodziec and were to pay it the same fees for baptism and funeral until their amount was fixed by the higher church authority, and the *dyssidenci* [Protestants] of the same commune were to hold their weddings and baptisms in this church *"pod sztrofem"* [under penalty of a fine][^325]. In the Lubieńskie Ol., which were church property, the inhabitants – Catholics – were to pay the parish church at Dzierbin every year for the administration of the sacraments[^326], that is probably to pay the so-called *kompozyta*, a one-off lump-sum payment for baptisms and weddings, and probably for other kinds of dues as well (*meszne*, tithe). In the Cekowskie St. Ol. an agreement on the tithe was to be made with the parish priest through the manor; in the Dębniałki and Piskory Ol. the dissenters were exempt from dues, and the Catholics were to pay *jura stolae*; in the Dzierzbińskie Ol. – Zamęty and Zdanowiec Ol. the Olęders were by agreement to give *a wiertel of rye per włóka and pay for a wedding 4 zł, for the funeral of an old person 1 zł, for a baptism 1 zł, for the three banns 18 gr*; in the Kosmowskie Ol., the Kuszyńskie Ol. and the Plewińskie St. Ol. they were to render dues according to a future agreement with the parish priest of Kosmów, while in the Plewińskie Nw. Ol. the Olęders of the same Evangelical-Augsburg confession were to give the same payment as the inhabitants of the Plewińskie St. Ol.[^327], not stated more closely, however. In the Oborskie St. and Nw. Ol. the inhabitants were bound to give the parish priest at Żegocin *1 ducat every year*, and in the Przespolewskie Ol. they were to pay the *jura stolae* fees to the Catholic parish priest at Przespolew Kościelny[^328]. The first foundation charter for the Stropieszyńskie or Radzany and Gostynie Ol. of 1787 laid down: "*Since by a recent law freedom of worship has been reserved for all people of the Protestant* [sic!] *religion, therefore, as regards confession and burial, they shall be free to go to their own church, and the manor will give them protection in this, on the foundation of the said law, against all impeachments of the clergy (should there be any)*". The second such charter for these settlements, of 1791, on the other hand, stated: "*It will be the obligation of these Hollędrzy to hold their weddings and baptisms in the parish church, to which parish church they will be bound to pay every year on the feast of the Three Kings 12 złoty for wax, that is*

In the Zbierskie Olędry the Lutheran inhabitants were to pay *the parish priest at Zbiersk the kompozyta, that is the one-off lump-sum payment for baptisms, but probably also for meszne and tithe, in an amount to be agreed, in the fixing of which, and in the making of an agreement on the matter with the parish priest, the founder of the settlement declared he would help them*[^330].

[p. 132]

# 6. The material and spiritual culture and the daily life of the inhabitants of the Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest in the years 1746-1793

The manifestations of the material and spiritual culture and the daily life of the inhabitants of the Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest in the years 1746-1793 can for the most part be grasped only to a very modest extent in the written Old Polish sources, or there is no information about them at all. No objects of the second half of the eighteenth century illustrating them have survived to our day either, so that most of the questions connected with them, especially in the case of material culture, can be described only on the basis of objects (for instance buildings, pieces of the furnishing of houses, farm tools) of the nineteenth and first half of the twentieth century, their museum photographs, and the ethnographic and other literature, mostly by the method of retrogression.

The written sources hand down only a dozen or so pieces of information about dwelling and farm buildings. According to them, the farmsteads of the Olęders in the Pyzdry Forest had, besides the cottage, that is the dwelling house, farm buildings: barns, cowsheds, stables and pigsties for the livestock. As was said above (in Chap. 2), according to the detailed tables of hearths surviving for the Olęder settlements that concern us in the districts of Pyzdry and Kalisz, most of them had "*cottages with one farmer*". The foundation charters surviving for 31 settlements show that the dwelling and farm buildings were put up of timber given by the founder (chiefly pine), mostly standing on the Olęders' holdings and cleared by them. The charters for a few settlements contained recommendations to put them up "*as carefully as possible*", or orders to build them within a set time of several years under threat of a money fine or even removal from the settlement. The dwelling and farm buildings were probably put up by carpenters and masons from the Olęder settlements or neighbouring ones. Among the former, the arcaded cottages proper to the villages of the time, and not only the Olęder ones, most probably predominated, set on the whole with their gable and not their ridge to the road, while the farm buildings were put up round the yard, so that together with the cottage they formed a quadrangle. Unfortunately, in the Old Polish Olęder settlements of the Pyzdry Forest, with a few exceptions, no buildings of the eighteenth century have survived. Far more of the farm buildings and cottages surviving to our day date from the nineteenth century. An inspection of them, together with an analysis of the archival photographs

[p. 133]

of the folk buildings in these villages kept in the District Museum in Konin and reproduced in the book of I. F. Tłoczek[^331], and the information in the ethnographic literature, have made it possible to establish that these houses had elements also proper to eighteenth-century building. Buildings were then put up of wood, in post-and-plank, log and half-timbered (frame) construction. Clay and bog iron ore were also commonly used building materials. Buildings of wood and of bog iron ore were insulated inside with a daub of clay. The architecture of the dwelling houses of the Pyzdry Forest area, as architectural research of the years 2009 – 2011 has shown[^332], has recurring features common to the whole area of the forest. They are: symmetrical pitched roofs at an angle of 45 degrees; gables boarded vertically; rafters resting on decorated ceiling beams projecting beyond the face of the wall; box windows, "standing rectangles", of one or two casements; divided windows, with panes of the same size or larger below and smaller above; outer doors of ledged or frame-and-panel construction, mostly decorated, most often double. The features of the farm buildings are close to those of the dwellings. The visible differences concern the following features: rectangular or square windows; additional small window openings without glass, in the shape of an equilateral triangle; doors of boards set vertically.

The houses usually had wide chimneys, joined at the bottom to bread ovens. The latter, which also served for drying flax, were built by the Olęders of the Pyzdry Forest, as a precaution against fire, in the yard or the garden. The house ovens were inspected every year by the sołtys and the assessors, who collected fines for the manor for the forbidden drying of flax in the cottages[^333].

Because the surviving houses have been rebuilt many times, the original layout of their interiors cannot be established exactly. According to the ethnographic literature, however, it may be supposed that the most popular type was the two-room cottage, with the two rooms on either side of a hall in the middle.

The furnishing of the interiors of the Olęder cottages consisted of beds, standing most often against the walls, chests for keeping clothes, a table under the window, and benches for sitting and for mangling linen. Not every room had a stove. Shelves hung on the walls, and there was also (probably above all in the houses of Catholics) the so-called "*holy corner*", where a crucifix and other devotional objects were placed[^334].

In the interiors of the Olęders' houses there were also a fair number of the utensils needed in the household, most probably like those used in other, non-Olęder settlements and known from the ethnographic literature. They included wooden stave vessels: for water, used for drawing it (*węborki*) and carrying it

[^332]: P. Szwiec, a collective work, *Katalog architektury regionalnej dla obszaru Puszczy Pyzdrskiej*, Pyzdry, 2009; R. Barek, a collective work, *Puszcza Pyzdrska – wzornik zabudowy*, Pyzdry, 2011.

[^333]: M. Jasińska, op. cit., p. 111; Z. Chodyła, *Najstarsze dzieje osad olęderskich w okolicach Nekli w latach 1749-1793* [The earliest history of the Olęder settlements around Nekla in the years 1749-1793], Nekla 2005, p. 43; the same, *Zarys najstarszych dziejów osad olęderskich na Ziemi Jarocińskiej w latach 1747-1790 (1793)* [Outline of the earliest history of the Olęder settlements in the Jarocin land in the years 1747-1790 (1793)], "Zapiski Jarocińskie", No. 1-2 (22/23). 2001, p. 69.

[^334]: *Kultura ludowa Wielkopolski*, ed. J. Burszta, vol. I, Poznań 1961, pp. 174-183.

[p. 134]

(*konewki*, pails), for washing linen (*waski*, *balie*, tubs), for processing milk (cheese presses, *kierzynki* (churns) for making butter, *dzieże* (kneading troughs) for keeping flour, so-called *koponki* for making bread leaven, bread peels, washboards, so-called *prasowalnice* (mangling boards)[^335]. Of other equipment, the querns for grinding grain and the looms for making linen deserve notice[^336].

In the farm buildings were kept all kinds of tools: carts, scythes, *radła* (ards), ploughs, harrows, rakes, pitchforks and others. Among them the wooden *radło rylcowe* (sole ard) used in western Greater Poland, Bohemia and Silesia is noteworthy: it consisted of a straight beam, forked and running into handles, and of a share-beam set slantwise in the beam, with an iron share with an eye fitted on it. Such a share was found in 1958 in the Jarantowkie [sic] Ol. (Jarantów Kolonia), and its occurrence here (and not only here) is explained in the literature by the inhabitants of this village descending from Olęder villages inhabited by Czech people from the area of Zbąszyń[^337].

People dressed according to the customs brought from their places of origin, differently – as is obvious – on weekdays and on feast days, in clothes probably mostly of their own making, of linen and sheep's wool, but partly also sewn from cloth bought in the towns and little towns.

The food of the people of the Olęder settlements that arose in the Pyzdry Forest probably did not differ fundamentally from that of others, in proportion to their prosperity. They ate rye bread and other grain or flour dishes, groats, vegetables, butter, cheese, from time to time meat, in Lent probably herrings and other fish, and honey from their own apiaries, and fruit, and they drank beer, then general, and also milk[^338].

At family celebrations (christenings, weddings) and on other occasions they were obliged (under the manor's monopoly and the compulsion of propination) to drink vodka and beer in the lord's inn or from the lord's distilleries and malthouse. This is stated expressly in the foundation charters for the Cekowskie Nw. Ol., the Przespolewskie Ol., the Zbierskie Ol., the Grądy St. and Nw. Ol. and the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol.[^339]. A ban on bringing in "*beer and spirits and any drink*" from elsewhere was in force in the Cekowskie Nw. Ol., the Dzierzbińskie-Zamęty and Zdanowiec Ol., the Plewińskie St. and Nw. Ol., and also in the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol.[^340].

A comparative description of the Olęders and the local people of the villages round Stawiszyn made in 1889 by Stanisław Ciszewski[^341] confirms that the Protestant Olęders, most often Germans by origin, were, compared with the local people, of a different physical type (outward appearance) and a different mentality, differed in language, did not readily assimilate, and kept no contacts with the Polish peasants, differing from them in outward appearance,

[^335]: M. Jasińska, op. cit., pp. 111-112.

[^336]: St. Błaszczyk, *Kultura ludowa ziemi kaliskiej*. [In:] *Osiemnaście wieków Kalisza*, Vol. 2, Poznań 1961, p. 424, fig. 42.

[^337]: Ibid., p. 428

[^338]: *Historia kultury materialnej Polski* [History of the material culture of Poland], a collective work ed. W. Hensel and J. Pazdur, vol. IV: Od połowy XVII do końca XVIII w. [From the middle of the seventeenth to the end of the eighteenth century], ed. Z. Kamieńska and B. Baranowski, Ossolineum, Wrocław-Warsaw – Kraków – Gdańsk 1978.

[^339]: Cf. nn. 74, 40, 50, 72 and 82.

[^340]: Cf. nn. 74, 98, 101 and 82.

[^341]: Stanisław Ciszewski*: in "Wisła" – Miesięcznik Geograficzno-Etnograficzny. 1889, Vol. 3, no. 2*

[p. 135]

dress and customs even more than a hundred years after they had settled. According to his observations: "the Olęder men were in general shorter than the Polish peasants, but more *"thickset, sturdy and strong".* Their faces were typical, German, shaven; they very rarely wore beards. The Polish peasants wore long *sukmany* (coats), reaching almost to the ground, of grey, coarse cloth, more rarely of coarse linen dyed dark blue, made at home, and under the *sukmany* long cloth waistcoats with shining glass buttons. Their trousers and shirts were of linen; the former were sometimes sewn of the same cloth as the *sukmana*. Their boots were ordinary ones, bought at the fair. On their heads they put in summer a black felt hat with a small brim; in winter the taller ones wore caps of grey lambskin, extraordinarily high, with a blue cloth top and narrow blue ribbons drawing it in, tied in a bow, and the shorter ones a so-called *kaptur*, that is a low four-cornered cap, also trimmed with grey lambskin. The Olęders, like the local peasants, often wore grey or blue *sukmany*, but these were shorter and of a different cut. As headgear they almost never wore hats, but cloth caps, and in winter black lambskin ones. The Polish women dressed in skirts and jackets or bodices in which red always predominated, and only among the older ones was one to be seen the navy-blue cloth *kabat* formerly in general use, fairly short, reaching perhaps half an ell, with a small, roundly cut cape, and gathered at the back. Married women, with their hair cut short, wore on their heads (though not all of them) muslin or tulle caps, bound round the head with a twisted patterned kerchief. Girls plaited their hair into braids, pinning part up at the back and winding part round the ears. Both married women and girls liked to attach bright coloured ribbons to the beads (corals) they wore round their necks. On their feet all the women wore shoes, sometimes adorned with ribbon bows. The Olęder women, on the other hand (married women and girls alike), went about in striped woollen skirts of their own making and aprons of the same, and put on a fustian or other jacket. In summer, instead of the woollen skirts, they wore calico dresses. On their heads they put woollen kerchiefs, but dark ones, of the town type, which they tied in the same way as women of all estates. On their feet they wore shoes.

The customs and manners of the people of these settlements in the second half of the eighteenth century are not known.

As for customs, the Olęders blessed at Epiphany (the Three Kings): amber, myrrh, bryony, marjoram and garlic, which was to protect against an evil person. They also blessed then, for the same purpose, the so-called "devil's dung" (asafoetida), with which they fumigated themselves", giving it also to the cows to eat against bewitchment, especially at the new moon and before Whitsun. They also introduced a way of treating cattle, later known to the Polish peasants, by giving them animal oil. Both they and the Polish peasants, in treating people as well as cattle, set great store by various "ninefold" herbs, grasses, roots, drops and ointments. They also believed that someone could fall ill, for instance, from bewitchment by "*cioty*" [witches]. Besides herbs, a favourite medicine were various fats, such as those of the dog, the bear, the lion [sic!] and the badger, which were to help used internally as well as externally. A remedy against worms in children, to be effective, had to be prepared not in the house but behind it. They also used other, magical ways of healing. A remedy that radically helped a child who "screams from morning to evening", [supposedly]

[p. 136]

because of pain in the small of the back, was to cut crosses with an axe in the three beams supporting the *powała* [ceiling] at sunrise[^342]. According to S. Ciszewski, because the Polish peasants lacked an innate capacity to assimilate, there were clear differences between the two groups of the rural population, caused also by Protestantism, which was the main confession of the Olęders, and by the foreign language. Marriages of an Olęder with a Polish peasant woman, or the other way round, did not occur in the eighteenth century, or at least he had not heard of them, and on the Polish side the lack of relations and the separateness of the two groups were stressed. The Polish peasants were pious and fond of the outward rites of religion, but on the other hand enormously superstitious, mistrustful of the manor and of people from the town, of officials, but also humble towards them, not valuing education, but loving their land and judging a man by his work, but only manual work. Among their faults were a lack of respect for other people's property, especially forest property, and perhaps a somewhat loose life of the women. The Protestant Olęders were not inferior to the Poles in piety, and in superstition they far surpassed them, using, especially in raising cattle, a host of sorcerer's practices, which their Polish neighbours borrowed from them. At the same time, however, they showed a greater knowledge of farming. In their dealings with more educated people the Olęders were less humble than the peasants, showing more worldly manners. They did not attach much importance to learning either; many of them could not write, though probably most could read. Although they lived in Poland, not all of them could speak Polish. There were among them individuals who, having been in the army, had learnt to speak Russian fairly well. The Polish peasants, on the other hand, tried to say at least a few words in their dialect, which was rather hard to understand, even for persons who knew German reasonably well. The Olęders were also distinguished by their industry, but did not show such attachment to the land as the Polish peasants. Among the Olęders one did not meet very poor people, frequent among the Polish villagers, which also followed from their having farms of larger area and from their ingenuity and good husbandry. A fair income came to them, for instance, from home weaving, practised by the Olęder women and neglected by the Polish peasant women. From the sheep the Olęders raised, almost all the Olęder women made very fine and durable woollen skirts, thicker and thinner, and aprons, giving the yarn to the town only for dyeing. The Polish peasants, on the other hand, made only coarse linen, which they sometimes had dyed blue in the little town and from which they sewed trousers and *sukmany* for the men, or else they bought factory goods. The prosperity and higher culture of daily life of the Olęders compared with the Polish peasant showed itself also in their love of a tidy dwelling. The [Polish] peasants' huts looked worse beside the neat Olęder ones, but as a worker, a so-called "manual" one, the Polish peasant was more sought after.

As for the spiritual culture of the people of the Olęder settlements that arose in the eighteenth century in the Pyzdry Forest, the sources allow us to grasp, directly or indirectly, their confessional relations, schooling and religious worship, and the culture of daily life (chiefly material).

[^342]: St. Ciszewski, *Notatka etnograficzna z okolic Stawiszyna, w pow. kaliskim* [An ethnographic note from the area of Stawiszyn, in the district of Kalisz]. "Wisła Miesięcznik Geograficzno-Etnograficzny". 1889, Vol. III, no. 2.

[p. 137]

The composition of their inhabitants by confession was not – as was shown above – uniform. That the greater part of the inhabitants of the settlements belonged to Protestantism is suggested by the information on the character and manner of the payment of dues to the Catholic Church, set out above, and by the confirmation of the existence of schools – houses of prayer and/or their teachers – and of cemeteries, in the form of their endowment with plots of land, in the charters and a few other sources (inventories, inspections).

The sources also speak of schools/teachers for 29 settlements, namely: Wrąbczynek and the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol. (a common one)[^343], the Białobłockie and the Orlińskie D. and M. Ol. (a common one)[^344], the Borowieckie and the Konarskie Ol. (a common one)[^345], the Gadowskie St. and Nw. Ol.[^346], the Grądy St. and Nw. Ol.[^347], the Trąbczyńskie St. and Nw.[^348], the Wielołęka Ol.[^349], the Cekowskie St. Ol. and the Cekowskie Nw. Ol.[^350], the Dębniałki and Piskory Ol. (a common one)[^351], the Dzierzbińskie – Zamęty and Zdanowiec Ol. (separate schools and teachers)[^352], the Kosmowskie[^353], the Mycielińskie St. and Nw. Ol.[^354], the Plewińskie St. and Nw. Ol.[^355], the Przespolewskie Ol.[^356], the Stropieszyńskie or Radzany and Gostynie Olędry[^357] and the Zbierskie Ol.[^358].

The teacher in the school was a person employed specially for the purpose or (as often happened) one of the inhabitants of the village who could read and write. His duties were to teach the children the catechism, to prepare them for confirmation at the age of 14, and to teach them the basics of reading, writing and arithmetic. The children of the Protestant settlers went to the schools, and until the First World War teaching was in German. The children of the Polish Catholic settlers were brought into schooling only at the beginning of the twentieth century.

Schooling lasted at first only two years and, as was general at the time, only in winter and early spring, because the children were used for herding cattle and for field work. Besides, because he could write and read, the teacher was at the service of the settlement's self-government. At the same time the teacher performed religious services to a basic extent (he led the Sunday services and funerals). Sunday services were held not only in the Protestant churches but also in the schools or in chapels built beside the schools, as for example in the chapels still standing today

[p. 138]

in Orlina Duża, Zamęty, Wrąbczynkowskie Holendry and Łazińsk Pierwszy.

For baptisms and weddings, on the other hand, the Olęders living in the Pyzdry Forest went to the pastors of the Protestant churches at Grodziec, Nowa Kaźmierka, Konin, Stare Prażuchy, Stawiszyn, Zagórów and Żychlin[^359].

The schools, confirmed in the foundation charters for 16 settlements, which stood in wooden cottages on half-peasant or smallholder holdings with land and garden, mostly also housed the houses of prayer; in the nineteenth century these were replaced in some settlements by newer wooden buildings, exceptionally still standing today, in Piskory and Stary Borowiec, and above all by brick ones, which survive today in, among other places, Białobłoty, Łazińsk Pierwszy, Nowe Grądy, Michalinów Trąbczyński, Stare Prażuchy, Stawiszyn, Wrąbczynkowskie Hol., Zamęty and Zagórów[^360].

An important element of the spatial organisation and the cultural landscape of the Olęder villages were the village burial grounds. For some of the settlements they are mentioned in the foundation charters cited above. Their existence is also shown by their positions on old maps (especially on the Map of the Quartermaster General's staff of the Polish Army of 1839), and above all by their remains (chiefly with gravestones of the second half of the nineteenth and the first half of the twentieth century) surviving to this day in more than 50 settlements, among them: the Ciemierowskie Stare Ol. (today Ciemierów Kolonia), the Wrąbczynkowskie Ol.[^361], the Cekowskie Nw. Ol. (Prażuchy Nw.), Plewnia Kolonia, Kuźnica, Poroże (Stare), Szadykierz[^362], the Piskory Ol., and the Orlińskie Duże Ol.[^363]. They varied in area according to the size of the village's population. In their centre a tall wooden cross was put up. They were surrounded by a low fence, exceptionally by a wall, also of bog iron ore, as can be seen in Orlina Duża. The existence of burial grounds is confirmed, for instance, by the foundation charters for 10 of the 26 settlements in the district of Kalisz: the Dębniałki and Piskory Ol. (they were to receive land for a common "burial ground"), the Dzierzbińskie – Zdanowiec and Zamęty Ol. (they were to receive for a common cemetery for the dissenters a plot of 14 by 14 rods), the Kuszyńskie or Jaszczurowo Ol., together with the Kosmowskie Ol. ("*...for the smallness of the place on the said Holendry called Jaszczurowo..., so these Jaszczurowo Holendry are to be buried on the Kosmowskie Holendry...*"), the Przespolewskie Ol. (they received a plot for a cemetery, which they were to enclose with a fence), the Stropieszyńskie St. and Nw. Ol. (they received 3 morgen for a school and a cemetery, and timber from the manor), and the Zbierskie Ol. (*for the "kirchow", that is the cemetery, they received a plot of 15x15 rods*)[^364].

The burials of Protestants from Olęder settlements of mixed confession founded before 1768, and also later, but only on the site of earlier non-Olęder settlements, could take place on the strength of agreements made between the Olęder communes

[^359]: W. Rusiński, *Osady tzw. „olędrów”*, pp. 114–115 and 117.

[^360]: Z. Chodyła, W. Kowalska, *Puszcza Pyzdrska. Olęderskie dziedzictwo*, pp. 30-31 (photographs); J. Dolat, *Parafia ewangelicko-augsburska w Prażuchach* [The Evangelical-Augsburg parish at Prażuchy], "Kronika Wielkopolski" No. 2 (102), 2002, pp. 37-48 (here: pp. 45-47).

[^361]: Ibid.
