[p. 139]

and the parish priests, binding the Olęders to pay the Catholic clergyman the so-called *pokładne* (burial fee). The charter for the Stropieszyńskie-Radzany and Gostynie Ol. of 1791 stated: "*Since by a recent law* [that is the constitutions of the Diet of 1768 – added Z.Ch.] *freedom of worship has been reserved for all people of the Protestant* [sic!] *religion, therefore, as regards confession and burial, they shall be free to go to their own church, and the manor will give them protection in this, on the foundation of the said law, against all impeachments* [attacks] *of the clergy (should there be any)*"[^365].

[^365]: Cf. n. 114.

[p. 140]

# Conclusion

This outline of the earliest history of the Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest in the years 1746-1793 has many gaps, which follow from how far the sources survive and how much they contain. It does, however, allow a few final conclusions to be drawn concerning: 1) the chronology, organisation, character of the land, conditions and causes of the founding, and the naming of the settlements, 2) their area, layout and buildings, 3) their population in the years 1789-1790, with the numbers and structure of the inhabitants by sex, age, social and property standing and occupation, 4) the legal, administrative and judicial situation of the Olęder population, 4) the economic and social life of the settlements, and 5) the material and spiritual culture and the daily life of their inhabitants.

1) The Pyzdry Forest, from the early Middle Ages to the present day one of the still fairly large forest complexes in Greater Poland proper, is an area in which, in the eighteenth century, Olęder settlement developed fairly vigorously; it was one of the main forms of rural rent-paying settlement in Poland in the period from the sixteenth to the beginning of the second half of the nineteenth century. In all, 58 Olęder settlements were founded in it in the years 1746-1792 (1793), within three Old Polish districts: the most in the district of Konin (28), then in that of Kalisz (25) and of Pyzdry (5). They belonged to three types of feudal property: royal, or more exactly at that time Crown (7, 1 of them on the estates of the royal town of Pyzdry), church (3, of them 1 on the estates of the bishopric of Poznań and 2 on monastic estates) and as many as 48 on noble estates, mostly on such as Grodziec-Królików, Gadów and Trąbczyn in the district of Konin and Dzierzbin, Chocz, Kosmów and Lipe in the district of Kalisz. Of these, two villages (the Kazimierskie Stare and Nowe Olędry) in the parish and on the estate of Chocz, which formed the endowment of a noble foundation of the mitred provostship of the collegiate church of Chocz, are sometimes regarded in the sources as church villages. Today 67 villages or parts of villages correspond to these 58 Olęder settlements, mostly under changed names, in 15 communes and six districts. The time of origin of 31 of the 58 Olęder settlements that arose in the Pyzdry Forest in the eighteenth century is fixed exactly by their foundation charters; for the rest it can be fixed exactly or approximately from the first mentions in other kinds of sources. Within the periodisation adopted

[p. 141]

for the history of Olęder settlement in Poland and Greater Poland, they arose in the second phase (1660-1793/1795) and the third period (1721-1793/95), most of them (44) in the third and last sub-period (1773-1793) of that period. The Olęder settlements were founded by the founders, owners or holders of landed estates, in agreement with locators acting individually or as a group on behalf of the whole future commune, or, mostly, with whole communes; they often issued to them first individual or collective but preliminary charters, and only later (for instance once the necessary number of settlers had gathered) collective and final ones, which settled the Olęder character of the village. On the royal estates they were founded by their tenants of the time (starosts, lessees), on the church estates by the bishop and by the superior and other members of the governing body of the convent of the Franciscan nuns in Kalisz, on the town estates by the magistrate of Pyzdry, and on the noble estates by the rich and middling nobility. The co-founder or founder of the largest number of Olęder villages was, on the property of Grodziec-Królików, Count Fraciszek Stadnicki, who in the district of Konin alone owned as many as 21 rural settlements. The founders of several dozen others, 12 persons, owned at least several such rural settlements, and sometimes little towns. The oldest settlements, at least 12 of them, were founded in the years 1746-1771; the rest, the great majority, were founded in the years 1772-1792. The founding of the Olęder villages was decided by the founders' wish to raise their income by settling and bringing into use land that had formerly been deserted and depopulated, or wooded, and often not taken up by settlement until then. The settling of Olęders, who answered jointly, in accordance with one of the basic principles of Olęder law, for paying money rents to the lords, assured their founders of a steady and regular income. For the Olęders the motive for settling were favourable legal and economic conditions of life, far better than in the country and the place of their origin or their earlier residence in Poland, and the assurance of religious freedom and freedom of public worship, guaranteed to the settlers in the foundation charters in accordance with the principles of Olęder law. As to the character of the land, of the 58 Olęder villages at least 37 were founded on woodland, and of these at least 27 on raw root, that is in places not settled before, in forests or woods (which is also shown by the long free years, 7-8, meant for clearing, setting up and building the settlements), 5 were founded on the site of settlements or parts of settlements that had existed earlier under the same names, even since the Middle Ages, and had been deserted and overgrown with forest at the end of the sixteenth and the beginning of the seventeenth century, and 5 on land partly tilled and ready. Of the 58 settlements founded in the Pyzdry Forest, 27 were founded on podzolic soils, 4 on black earths, 6 on marsh soils, 13 on muck soils, 4 on river alluvia, and 4 on mixed soils, 1 of them on podzolic soils, muck soils, river alluvia and dune sands, and 3 on podzolic-muck soils. So half of them arose on fertile and moderately fertile soils, half on infertile ones, and 4 on mixed soils, though with infertile soils predominating. Most

[p. 142]

of the Olęder settlements received new names: possessive ones, derived from the names of a former village, or topographical names not tied to such names but referring to the local names of the woods, thickets, forests and wilds in which they were founded. Some villages received characteristic names, formed independently of the naming of old settlements, that is, among others, personal names, derived from the first names and surnames of the founders and members of their families. Some settlements bore names inherited from settlements long deserted, overgrown with forest and bushes, or cleared of their old inhabitants, on whose site or lands they were founded with new colonists settled under Olęder law, or from settlements still inhabited wholly or partly by their existing inhabitants, moved to this law – topographical and possessive names. In both cases the names of these settlements most often appeared with the addition *(H)olendry* or *(H)olędry*. It has survived to this day only in the names of 6 settlements.

2) The area of the Olęder villages that arose in the Pyzdry Forest depended on the reserves of land (forest or other) at the founders' disposal, and also on the number of settlers obtained or coming forward. The holdings of land granted to the settlers ranged from 1 to ½ huba, that is włóka (łan), though often of various kinds and sizes according to the length of the basic measures – rods and ells – by which they were measured. Besides, the Olęder villages received smaller holdings of land for the innkeeper, the school (house of prayer), the teacher and the burial ground. In these 58 Olęder settlements there were in the years 1789–1790 about 651 hearths (farms) in all. In the number of farms there was a wide range between settlements – very small ones of 2-5 hearths were 9, small ones of 6-10 hearths 11, middling ones of 11-15 hearths 5, larger than middling, of 16-20 hearths, 8, large ones of 21-25 hearths only 2, and very large ones, of 26 hearths or more, only 1. For 15 settlements the functional and social-economic structure of their hearths is known. Among them, if we leave aside 2 breweries, 6 inns and 2 craftsmen's hearths, hearths with one farmer decidedly predominated, found in all 15 Olęder villages, 636 in all. Hearths with 2 farmers, 37 in all, were found in only 5 settlements, garden hearths with land and garden, 16 in number, in 3 settlements, and those without land and garden, 2 in number, in 1 settlement. Most of the hearths were "self-tilling hearths", that is farms, of which there were 662 in 1791. The layout of the Olęder settlements in the area of this forest complex varied. 9 settlements were row villages on marshy, uniform land, with rows of buildings in various arrangements, sometimes partly scattered; regular and irregular street villages founded on mixed land were 2; as many as 40 settlements were loose, scattered settlements, and 7 were mixed. The buildings of the farmsteads (farms) – dwellings and farm buildings – were scattered; the buildings were usually put up separately on a quadrangular plan; the cottages were most probably, as in all the villages of the time, mostly arcaded; and the farm buildings consisted of barns, stables, cowsheds and pigsties, put up by various

[p. 143]

techniques, of wooden logs, clay and bog iron ore. In the buildings and layout of the Olęder villages in the Pyzdry Forest, most of which were wholly or partly inhabited by people of the Protestant confession, important elements were the houses of prayer – schools – and the burial grounds, which lay on the whole on the edges of the villages.

3) In the 58 Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest there lived in the years 1789–1790 about 3,815 persons in all, of whom 499 in 5 settlements in the district of Pyzdry, 1,457 in 25 settlements in the district of Kalisz, and 1,859 in 28 settlements in the district of Konin. The statistical average number of inhabitants per settlement was 65.77 persons. In the 45 settlements for which the demographic structure of their inhabitants is known there lived 3,154 persons, 1,707 (50.32%) of them male and 1,685 (49.68%) female. Of these 3,154 persons, 449 (234 male and 215 female) lived in 3 settlements of the district of Pyzdry, 1,368 persons (691 male and 6776 female) lived in 22 settlements of the district of Kalisz, and 1,337 persons (654 male and 683 female) lived in 20 settlements of the district of Konin. The social, property and occupational structure can be grasped for only 25 Olęder settlements (3 in the district of Pyzdry and 22 in the district of Kalisz). There were in all in them 1,518 farmers and rent-paying cottagers (that is Olęders proper with the members of their families), who can in principle be regarded as one and the same social and occupational category, 50 lodgers, 7 craftsmen, 13 innkeepers and tapsters, 102 farm servants and servants, 68 *luźni* (landless persons) and 59 other persons. The confessional structure of the inhabitants of the 58 Olęder settlements founded in the years 1746-1792 in the Pyzdry Forest varied. A population Protestant by confession predominated in them, and, judging not only from religion but also from names and other indications, ethnically German, but it made up no more than about 52% of the whole population. The composition in this respect of the 1,337 inhabitants of 20 of the 28 Olęder settlements in the district of Konin, which is known more exactly, was as follows: there were 6 settlements inhabited exclusively by dissenters, 385 in all (Lutherans, and perhaps to a small degree Reformed Protestants), 4 with a Catholic population, 210 in all, and 10 settlements with a mixed population, Catholic and Protestant (almost exclusively Lutheran), with 365 Catholics and 377 dissenters in all. So in all these settlements there lived 575 Catholics and 762 dissenters in all, that is Protestants – Lutherans, and perhaps in two or three of these settlements also Reformed. The places the settlers came from are not exactly known, but on the strength of certain indications given above (the measures of land, the similarity of names, the dialects used) it seems that they came to the settlements from the borderland of Greater Poland and Silesia and from Silesia, and from older Protestant Olęder, and perhaps sołtys, settlements, chiefly in western Greater Poland, not known by name, but mostly from the same places (as their kinship, legible in identical surnames, seems to show). Ethnically about 52% of them were Germans. Assuming that confession and ethnicity coincided in about 90%, it may be accepted

[p. 144]

that about 38% of the inhabitants of the Olęder villages in the Pyzdry Forest were Poles. Exact findings cannot be given here, since the sense of identity and ethnic identification among the population, especially the peasants, was not then as clear-cut as it is today, and was only beginning to take shape.

4) The inhabitants of the Olęder settlements that arose in the Pyzdry Forest lived according to the principles of Olęder law, that is the solidarity of the whole commune towards the manor and internal equality among themselves. The Olęders, unlike the peasants owing labour service, were always personally free. The Olęders' subjection as to the land showed itself in their rights in rem to the land, through the definition of the forms of holding it and the conditions of the freedom to alienate and sell farms, in their rights of use on the lord's land (the so-called servitudes of pasture, fishing and others), and in their dependence on the manor's monopolies (chiefly that of propination). The most important of them, the right to the land, took in Greater Poland in the eighteenth century, apart from a few exceptions of emphyteusis in the sense of a short-term lease of land, for several decades, in principle the form of emphyteusis in the sense of a lease unlimited in time, perpetual, tied to the requirement that the holding of land first be bought by entry money, which once the entry money was paid turned in practice into a perpetual rent tenure (German *Erbzinsrecht*). The ownership of the land assigned to the Olęders was not full but usufructuary and divided, since its legal owner was still the lord, who, however, had no right to remove an Olęder from it as long as he used it in accordance with the provisions of the charter. The self-government of the Olęder communes consisted of a sołtys and, mostly, two assessors, elected by the commune assemblies, with in principle a one-year term. As the commune court, the sołtys and the assessors were to keep internal peace and order in the settlements and to settle quarrels and disputes. They judged by Olęder law, whose terms were set by the foundation charters of the individual settlements and, for some of them, also by the *wilkierze*. From the judgments of the commune court the Olęders had the right of appeal to the manor, that is to the *dominium* (lord's) court, for which the right to judge in criminal cases of robbery, arson, homicide and serious bodily harm), and of course all offences against the lord, was also reserved. The Olęders' judicial subjection consisted precisely in their being subject to the *dominium* (lord's) court to this extent. The villages they inhabited were for the most part independent spatial-economic, legal-constitutional and administrative-judicial units (communes). Some of them, however, namely 14, formed two-village communes, and in one case even a three-village commune, with common sołtyses. 5) The inhabitants of the 58 Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest in the eighteenth century were nearly 98% directly occupied in farming. This is confirmed, indirectly, by the fact mentioned above that of the total of 681 hearths in 1789, as many as 662 hearths were counted as farming hearths in 1791. For the innkeepers, smallholders, teachers and others with smaller holdings of land, too, work in farming was an important additional occupation. The basic branch of the Olęders' farming was crop-growing, and especially grain. Garden crops were of secondary, supplementary

[p. 145]

importance. The second important branch of the economy of the inhabitants of the Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest was the raising of animals – cattle, sheep and pigs, and also horses and poultry. The Olęders took an active part in trade, selling the surplus of their crops (grain) and their stock (cattle, horses, pigs, poultry and dairy produce), sometimes also trading in goods made or obtained in non-farming occupations (for instance crafts and bee-keeping), and buying in return grain and other foodstuffs, timber, or tools at the markets and fairs in the towns. On account of the perpetual, hereditary use of the land, the inhabitants of the Olęder villages founded in the Pyzdry Forest were bound to render to the lords – the founders of the settlements and their heirs – money rents for the land, dues in kind and labour (*tłoki*, seasonal labour service at harvest and haymaking, and other services, and carting journeys), paid on a yearly basis after the free years had run out, and to buy the manor's drink under the compulsion of propination. They also bore some administrative fees, for instance on the sale of land. The most important burden was the money rent, paid on the whole once a year, on a set date, and in an amount according to the size of the holding (farm). It was collected from the individual farmers and handed over jointly by the sołtys of the village against a receipt from the feudal lord or his representative. The inhabitants of all the Olęder settlements founded in the eighteenth century in the Pyzdry Forest were also bound, like the inhabitants of other villages, to render to the state taxes in money, paid every year in two instalments (March and September) – the poll tax, attested in separate tariffs for the years 1775 and 1789-1790, the *hiberna* in the settlements on royal estates in the tariff of 1775, the offering of 10 gr (on noble estates) and 20 gr on church estates of 1789 – and a tax in kind, the so-called levy (*zsypka*) from the "self-tilling" [farming] hearths, in the form of a set number of korzec of rye and oats from such a hearth. Besides, all the inhabitants of many Olęder settlements, Catholics but also, to a large extent, Protestants, were bound to render to the Roman Catholic Church dues in the form of fees *jura stolae* (that is for baptisms, weddings and funerals), *meszne* and *kolęda*. The Protestants were bound to pay *jura stolae* both until 1768 and often after the toleration law of that year, especially when they settled on land formerly settled and later deserted, in Olęder villages that arose on the site of medieval settlements assigned in this respect to a parish, sometimes also in the foundation deeds of these parish districts.

6) The material and spiritual culture and the daily life of the people of the Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest in the eighteenth century are not easy to describe, because the information in the written sources is very scanty. Supplementing it with data from museum documentation, the ethnographic literature and modern research, it can be said that, in the case of dwelling and farm buildings, the architecture of the buildings has common features characteristic of the area of the Pyzdry Forest.

[p. 146]

As for spiritual culture, it can be said that the inhabitants of the Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest were guaranteed religious freedom and freedom of public worship in the form proper to their religion. The place where it was practised in this period, in every village inhabited by Protestants, was the school-house of prayer, and the services were led, to a basic extent, by the teachers (readers) who taught the children in them, especially the basics of religion, and prepared them for confirmation. The Olęder dissenters (Protestants) also had their own burial grounds, whose remains have survived to this day in practically all the villages in which they lived. The culture of daily life of the people of the Olęder settlements was made up of their housing (the dwelling houses and their furnishings), work, clothing, food, and the customs and manners connected with their production, division and use. People dressed according to the customs brought from their places of origin, differently – as is obvious – on weekdays and on feast days, in clothes chiefly of their own making, of linen and sheep's wool, but partly also sewn from cloth bought in the towns and little towns. The food of the people of the Olęder settlements that arose in the Pyzdry Forest did not differ fundamentally from that of other settlements and was in proportion to their prosperity. They ate rye bread and other grain or flour dishes, groats, vegetables, butter, cheese, from time to time meat, in Lent probably herrings and other fish, which the Olęders had the right to catch in the waters adjoining their land, and honey from their own apiaries, and fruit; they drank beer, then general, and also milk. Various manifestations of the Olęders' material and spiritual culture showed differences, even after a century, compared with those known for the Polish rural population. This followed from the separateness of language, the religious separation and the unwillingness to assimilate on the Olęders' side, and also from differences in the mentality of the two sides. Nevertheless there were some contacts between them, and some practices were taken over or imitated, especially in farming and stock-raising, in which the Olęders led.

Summing up this outline of the earliest history of the Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest, it must be said that, because of the number of these settlements and the detail of some of the source information on some questions, together with its absence on other matters, and the limited size of this study, it was impossible to create an even, full and exhaustive picture of it.

In appreciating the contribution of Olęder settlement to the development of the network of settlements and to the social and economic development of the area, it should be seen in the context of rural settlement as a whole, not only that of its own time but also earlier and later. For in the years 1794/1796-1863 further changes took place in the settlement structure of the area of the Pyzdry Forest. In this period at least 28 settlements were founded. Of these, at least 17 Olęder settlements arose by the 1840s, and by the beginning of the 1860s 11 other rent-paying settlements, among them such as Święcia, Olchowo, Emilienheim, that is Oleśnica, Michalinów, Tarszewo, Grądzeń Las, Mały Las, Chruściki, Grądzeń, Drzewce, Kopojno, Oleśnica Kolonia, Oleśnica Wielka and Zarzew, in the then communes of Dąbroszyn, Oleśnica and Dłusk. Some of them were founded in the period of South Prussia on the site of old villages, deserted or

[p. 147]

cleared of their existing inhabitants[^366]. At the same time and later, the individual parts of the Pyzdrskie Olędry turned, as the area of the larger farms was divided and new settlers were brought in, into villages of many farmsteads spatially and separate administratively. As a result, what was originally one settlement in administrative and judicial terms, with colony-type, scattered (dispersed) buildings in the form of so-called *pustkowia*, turned into 9, or even, counting in turn round the edge of the forest, 12 separate settlements: Kruszyny, Zamość, Grądy Dolne and Górne, Baraniec, Zimochowiec, Tłoczyzna, Glinianki, Trzcianki, Zapowiednia, Żdżary and Królewiny. At this time, too, 2 or even 3 separate settlements in time split off from some other Old Polish Olęder settlements; for instance the Białobłockie Ol. divided into Białobłoty, Garncarski Kąt and Krzyżówka.

After the enfranchisement of the peasants in the Kingdom of Poland (1864), a new wave of settlement appeared in the years 1870-1880 on private landed estates, among them those bordering on the town woods of Pyzdry from the south and east, on the parcelled lands of demesne farms. Then and later, up to 1914, 55 rural settlements, on the whole smaller ones, and at least 9 industrial settlements were founded on them. Among the settlers of the Old Polish and later Olęder settlements and other rent-paying settlements in the Pyzdry Forest, at least 82 in all, the German element predominated. The population of the new colonies, partly forming fairly compact groups, partly scattered, differed from the native population in language, religion, its own social institutions, such as the school and the house of prayer, dress, customs and manners. It came chiefly from Silesia and to a lesser extent from the Neumark (Ciążeńskie Holendry, Wrąbczyn, Wrąbczynkowskie Holendry, Olchowo, Mały Las, Tarszewo, Babia, Józefów, Zarzew, Augustynów, Święcia). In the nineteenth century there were significant changes in the buildings, the population and the confessional, and probably also the ethnic, composition, and in the literacy of the inhabitants of the Olęder settlements that arose in the Pyzdry Forest in the eighteenth century[^367].

In the nineteenth century there were also changes in the church organisation of the Olęder settlements. The settlements with an exclusively or mostly Protestant population, almost exclusively Lutheran[^368], were joined to the Lutheran parishes of the Evangelical-Augsburg diocese of Kalisz. It used the Protestant parish churches at Stawiszyn (1780), Grodziec (from 1796), Prażuchy (from 1808), Konin (from 1826) and Zagórów (from 1858) and the filial churches at Józefów near Kaźmierka Stara (from 1843) and Lisewo (1858) and at Żychlin[^369].

In the inter-war period about 12 new settlements arose, often in the character of hamlets or outlying hamlets with their own names (though etymologically mostly tied to the names of old villages). They were founded on the lands of estates parcelled out in carrying out the provisions of the agrarian

[^366]: Cf. the works of A. Breyer cited above.

[^367]: *Słownik Geograficzny Królestwa Polskiego i innych krajów slowiańskich* [Geographical dictionary of the Kingdom of Poland and other Slavic countries]. Vols. I-XVI, pts. 1-2, Warsaw 1880-1902

[^368]: E. Kneifel, Die evangelisch-augsburgischen Gemeinden der Kalischer Diöcese..., Leipzig 1937.

[^369]: E. Kneifel, Die evangelisch-augsburgischen Gemeinden der Kalischer Diöcese..., Leipzig 1937; K. Gorczyca, *Żychlin pod Koninem: dzieje wsi i zboru* [Żychlin near Konin: the history of the village and the church], Warsaw, "Semper" 1997.

[p. 148]

reform of 1925. At that time too, in carrying out those provisions that concerned the abolition of servitudes, the town of Pyzdry lost 785 ha of woodland to the owners of plots that had been burdened with the duty of protecting the town forests. The newly created plots were laid out as enclaves within forest areas with valuable stands of trees, mostly oak. This operation, carried out chaotically, contributed to the disorganisation of what had once been a rational forest economy, and to the appearance of a considerable number of new farms, also within older villages, without any planning principles. At the same time the process of the fragmentation of farms, begun already in the nineteenth century, went on, lasting here, with some breaks, until the law on the protection of farm and forest land was passed in 1971.

In the years 1945-2015, and also later, as a result of this process up to that time, and also of the creation of recreational plots, 10 new small settlements appeared. Today in the area of the Pyzdry Forest, which lies within the one voivodeship of Greater Poland and the 6 districts and 15 communes mentioned above, there are 328 settlement names, among them 6 little towns, 323 villages and other settlements.

Today the Old Polish, and also the later, Olęder settlements are inhabited only in a smaller part by the descendants of the first settlers – Catholics – and in a minimal part also by the descendants of the Protestants – Germans. This is because almost all the German Protestant inhabitants of these settlements were expelled from the Pyzdry Forest and forced to leave for Germany after 1945. The Olęder settlements they had inhabited until 1945 were settled to a greater or lesser degree by Polish newcomers, and some were afforested.

There have survived to this day in the Olęder settlements of the Pyzdry Forest – from the enfranchisement of their inhabitants on, really post-Olęder ones – such elements of their cultural landscape as the layouts of the villages and their fields, ditches and dikes, the course of some roads, plantings of trees, the former Lutheran churches, traditional farmsteads, schools and cemeteries. Unfortunately, the original names of these villages have survived only to a very small degree, since most of them were changed in the inter-war and post-war periods, among other things with the intention of Polonising them and shortening their linguistic form. Only six settlements contain the characteristic distinguishing element *(H)olędry* or *(H)olendry*: Ciążeńskie Holendry, Wrąbczynkowskie Holendry, Złotkowskie Holendry, Gadowskie Holendry, Holendry Jaroszewickie and Olendry Smaszewskie.

These monuments and this toponymy, being a common historical – human, European – cultural value, raise the tourist attraction of the micro-region of the Pyzdry Forest. They should be protected and cared for permanently by the local communities, local government institutions, religious bodies, schools, and social, cultural, sightseeing and tourist organisations. The Cultural Society "Echo Pyzdr" has done much to protect these monuments, document them and spread knowledge of them in society. Its work helps to deepen the historical awareness of the inhabitants of the individual places and of the whole region, and to spread knowledge of the important elements of the historical space and the cultural heritage not only at the local but also at the wider regional and national level.

[p. 149]

# Glossary of Old Polish terms

**asekuracja** – here: a security, a written guarantee.

**bicie rowów** – digging ditches and lining them with fascines.

**cekowska dzierżawa** (the lease of Ceków) – an organisational unit of the royal/Crown estates in the district of Kalisz, with the village of Ceków as its centre; in the 1770s made up, besides that village, also of the newly founded Cekowskie Stare and Nowe Olędry.

**czeladź** – village servants, hired people, recruited from peasant youth (male and female) (of full peasants, more often of smallholders, cottagers and lodgers), mostly before marriage, employed on peasant farms (chiefly full peasant farms) and on demesne farms, under agreements usually made for a year, and paid with lodging, food, clothing and small sums of money (the so-called *myto*).

**czerwony złoty** (red złoty) – a ducat, a gold coin weighing about 3.5 g of almost pure metal, from 1787 equal to 18 Polish silver złoty.

**czynszujący** (rent-payers) – here peasants, Olęder farmers, who under their agreements paid, as their most important due, a money rent and possibly an additional rent in kind (grain, poultry) in return for the use of the land, most often perpetual.

**dezerta** (from Latin *deserta*), see pustka.

dobra stołowe (mensal estates) – estates forming the endowment, the source of income and the basis of maintenance of a particular office or official connected with the court (household), in this case the mensal estates of the bishop of Poznań.

**efemeryda** (ephemeral settlement) – here: a settlement undoubtedly Olęder in its legal constitution and economy, but existing as such only for a time, turned into a labour-service settlement, not through evolution but for other reasons.

**emfiteuza** (emphyteusis, from Latin *emphiteusis*) – in the feudal period it was most often understood as the long-term (renewable) and perpetual lease of land by peasants, tied to the obligation to pay an annual rent and to inform the owner of an intention to sell, since the owner had the right of pre-emption or 10% of the sale price of the land.

**fundator** (founder, from Latin *fundo*, *fundator*) – here: the feudal founder of a settlement, in this case a rural, Olęder one.

**garniec** – a measure of capacity, from 1764 equal to 3.77 litres, divided into 2 half-garniec, 4 kwarty and 16 kwaterki.

**kasztelan** (castellan) – an Old Polish official, in the Middle Ages the governor of a castle (*gród*, *kasztel*, from Latin *castellum*, castle) and of the castle district (castellany), military commander and judge in the monarch's name in its area, a member of the royal council, in early modern times a senator; castellans were divided into greater (provincial) and lesser (district ones, also called *drążkowi*).

**kmiecie** (full peasants) – the basic stratum of the rural population in the thirteenth to nineteenth centuries, holders of farms regarded as full ones. In the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries, when German law was introduced, they were usually settled on farms of one łan (one włóka), but then and later they often received larger holdings, of two łany (and as such were also called farmers, *gospodarze rolnicy*), or smaller, of half a łan (half-peasants, *półrolnicy*). In the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries the main burdens of the k. were money rents and dues in kind (chiefly in grain) to the lord and tithe to the Church; in the sixteenth to eighteenth centuries they were burdened more and more with labour service, reaching in the seventeenth century 5-6 days, and in the eighteenth century sometimes a dozen or so days a week per łan, which forced the k. to employ servants. To till their own and the lord's land the k. kept a considerable team (in the seventeenth century usually 2 horses and 4 oxen). In the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries the size of full peasant farms shrank considerably, to ½ and even ¼ łan, and the percentage of k. in the structure of the rural population fell. Beside the k. owing labour service there was a far less numerous group of rent-paying k. (for instance the Olęders), as a rule richer and more independent economically.

**komornicy** (lodgers) – a category of the landless rural population found in the fourteenth to nineteenth centuries, owning neither cottages nor land, living most often with farmers (Olęders too) and living by hired work for them or at the manor, often as servants; sometimes owning a few animals and tilling, in return for work, scraps of land; the poorest stratum of the settled rural population.

[p. 150]

**kontraktowy** (contract man) – a peasant (Olęder too), more often a craftsman, settled in a village under an individual contract, that is an agreement made with the feudal lord, the owner or the holder of the landed estate.

**kontrakt** (contract, from Latin *contractus*) – here the agreement of the founder of a settlement with the settlers or their representatives and the whole village commune, most often in the form of a unilateral charter issued by the founder; it could be individual and preliminary, or collective and final, and according to its legal character emphyteutic – short-term (for several decades) or perpetual.

**korzec warszawski** (Warsaw korzec) – a measure of capacity, from 1764 equal to 120.6 litres; it was divided into 2 Gdańsk korczyki and 32 garniec.

**lokacja** (from Latin *locatio*) – here the founding of a settlement, based on an agreement setting its legal and economic conditions, formulated by the founder in agreement with the settlers.

**lokator** (from Latin *locator*) – the *osadźca*, the representative of the settlers who made the agreement with the founder of the settlement on their behalf, a settlement entrepreneur who brought in the settlers and organised the new settlement; most often he settled in the village founded and (at least at the beginning) held the office of its sołtys, and also drew material benefits from his function (for instance in the form of additional land).

**łanowy układ przestrzenny** (*łan* layout) – a layout of arable land in the form of a łan, the area of a full peasant farm, whose size, according to how it was understood as a unit of measurement, ranged from 16.7 to 25.8 ha, and which was laid out with various kinds of rods and ells.

**łokieć** (ell) – a unit of measure; from 1764 the Warsaw ell equalled 59.6 cm (a measure of length) and was divided into 24 inches; the square Warsaw ell equalled 0.354666 m² (a measure of area).

**niwowy układ** (*niwa* layout) – a layout of arable land in the form of a *niwa*, that is a new area of land won for tillage chiefly by clearing forests, belonging to one or several farmers, its shape and size depending on the conditions of the ground and the property relations; it was characterised by the marking out of three basic fields (winter, spring and fallow), in which the peasants, sometimes with the lord's manor, held separate strips (*płosy*) forming a łan (włóka), which divided the *niwa* into long strips.

**oblata** (from Latin *oblatus*, and that from *offerre*, *oblatum*, to bring before; to leave) – here: the entering (copying) or taking of a legal act into the books of a court and office (for instance a municipal court).

**okupne** (entry money; in German most often called *Grungeld*) – the payment for land used in perpetuity, for the perpetual right to hold it and draw its profits.

**plenipotent** – the attorney of another person, acting on his written authority, most often declared before an office.

**podkomorzy** (chamberlain) – an Old Polish land official, presiding over the chamberlain's court, the boundary court, legal representative of poor widows and orphans of the noble estate and (only in Greater Poland and only until the turn of the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries) a member of the land court.

**podwoda** – a transport service, otherwise called a journey (*droga*), rendered by peasants, in this case Olęders, to the manor (mostly once a year) as part of their labour services, one of the forms of manorial obligations, sometimes commuted into a payment; *podwody* were used to carry the demesne farm's grain and other goods to local markets (the so-called small journeys, up to about 35 km) and more distant ones (great journeys, up to about 84 km), made with the peasants' carts and two-horse teams.

**pręt** (rod) – a measure of length, from 1764 equal to 4 m 46 cm 6.54 mm; it was equal to 7½ ells.

przestęp – (white or red bryony) – a perennial, a common plant of the gourd family with black or red berries.

**przymus propinacyjny** (compulsion of propination) – the absolute obligation of the peasants (Olęders too) to buy the drink produced on their feudal lords' estates, under threat of heavy money fines and even removal from their farms.

**pustka** – the land of a village deserted for various reasons, overgrown again with forest and unused, needing clearing.

**rudunek** (from German *Rodung*) – clearing, also called *rumunek*.

**rumunek** – clearing, or cleared places, or a settlement founded on the site of felled woods; the name comes from the German *räumen* (to empty, to clear away, to remove).

**rychwalska miara** (the Rychwał measure) – here: a local variety of the wiertel, referring to the standard of this measure probably kept in the town hall of the little town of Rychwał; not exactly known.

**siąg kwadratowy** (square siąg), otherwise *sążeń* – a non-metric measure; the Old Polish one – 1.786 m (according to the constitution

[p. 151]

of 1764); derived from the *sążeń* is the *sąg*, a cubic measure of wood: 1 *sąg* (forest, for wood) = ½ cubic *sążeń* = 2.58 m³.

**skarbnik latyczewski** (treasurer of Latyczów) – an Old Polish land official, in medieval Poland caring for the royal treasure – valuables, jewels and documents; in early modern times an honorary, titular office tied to a voivodeship or district or land, the last in the hierarchy of land offices; in this case tied to the district of Latyczów/Latyczew in Podolia.

**starosta** (starost) – "with judicial power" (castle starost, general starost) a judicial and administrative official appointed by the king, over a land, a district or a larger area, who also held on lease royal (state) estates forming the endowment of his office; "without judicial power" only a *tenutariusz*, that is the lessee and manager of larger or smaller organisational and economic units of the royal or state landed estates, called starosties, *tenuty* or leases (*dzierżawy*), and sometimes only of single villages, farms (sołtys or wójt holdings) and objects (such as mills).

**stolnik** – an Old Polish land official, in the Middle Ages in charge of the ruler's table, in early modern times an honorary, titular office of a voivodeship or a land.

**sucha beczka** ("dry barrel") – an empty barrel, not filled with beer, the symbol of a set money fine paid to the manor for evading or breaking the compulsion of propination.

**szambelan** (chamberlain), otherwise court chamberlain (*podkomorzy nadworny*) – a court official responsible for the monarch's residence, with authority over the chamber servants and courtiers assigned to serve the king, attending the king at the sessions of the Diet and of the court, on journeys and on campaigns; in the eighteenth century a purely titular, honorary office.

**szarwark** – the compulsory service of the peasant population, Olęders too, in labour or with teams, in repairing and building roads, bridges, dikes and flood embankments.

**talar** (thaler) – a large silver coin weighing about 28 g, of good metal, struck in many countries of Europe from the end of the fifteenth and the first half of the sixteenth century; after 1764 in Poland it equalled 8 złp.; from the sixteenth century in the countries of the Holy Roman Empire (the Reich) called the *reichsthaler*, the Imperial thaler, struck also to its proper standard in Polish mints.

**tatarka** – a grain, a species of buckwheat.

**tenutariusz** – see starosta.

**tynf**, **tymf** – the colloquial name of the silver Polish złoty pieces struck in the years 1663-1667 at Bydgoszcz and Lwów by Andrzej Tymf, lessee of the Crown mints, which had a set rate of 30 gr. but were really worth 12-18 gr.; their enormous production (about 6 million pieces) helped to intensify inflation.

**wiertel** – before the partitions of Poland a measure of farmland equal to 3,591 m² [sic]; later, as the Berlin wiertel (*Viertel*, the Prussian quarter), a measure of capacity of liquids and of the volume of dry goods (grain too), equal to 7.9 l.; here it occurs in the latter sense.

**wizja** (inspection) – a detailed official description of the real and movable property and of the rent drawn from peasant or burgher farms, drawn up most often in connection with a change of the holder of the estate, at the request of the parties concerned, carried out on the spot by a sworn court usher representing the office, the municipal court or the land court, in the presence of witnesses, and differing in this from a private-law description such as other inventories.

**wojski** – an Old Polish land official, in the Middle Ages the castellan's deputy for military affairs, responsible for order in the district during a military expedition, guardian of the wives and children of the nobles at war; in early modern times an honorary, titular office, the last but one in the hierarchy of land offices.

**wolnizna** (free years) – the time of several years, set in the foundation charter, meant for taking up and bringing into use the land received as a holding (the area for a farm), free of obligations to the manor, the state and the Church; its length depended above all on the character of that land.

**wywód** (churching) – here: the blessing of a mother after the birth of a child, when she is led into the Catholic church.

**zagrodnicy** (smallholders) – otherwise *ogrodnicy*, a category of the peasant population with little land, mostly subject, existing in the thirteenth to nineteenth centuries, holding small farms, on the whole of 5-6 morgen; in the Olęder settlements there were also, besides such smallholders, "smallholders without land and garden", who had cottages but were probably in fact people living by hired work.

**złoty polski**, or złoty (*złotówka*) – a Polish silver coin, from 1764 equal to 4 silver groszy, 2 half-złoty, or 30 copper groszy.

[p. 152]

# List of illustrations

1\. The Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest founded in the years 1746-1792, on a part of the *Mapa szczegulna*[!] *Województwa Kaliskiego* of Karol de Perthées [n.d. [1791?] and about 1801, at a scale of 1:225,000.

2\. The Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest founded in the years 1746-1792, on a part of the map *Special Karte von Südpreussen* (...) of David Gilly of 1802 and 1803, at a scale of 1:150,000.

3\. The Olęder settlements in the Pyzdry Forest founded in the years 1746-1792, on a part of the *Topograficzna Karta Królestwa Polskiego* (the map of the Quartermaster General's staff of the Polish Army), [Warsaw], prepared before 1830, published in 1843 dated 1839, column I, section II.

4\. (1-2) Parts of the decree of the boundary commission court of 1776 telling of the founding of the Trąbczyńskie St. Ol. and the Luśnie Ol. by Paweł and Katarzyna née Rozdrażewska Prusimski in 1746. APP, Konin Gr. 145, fols. 681 and 692.

5\. Michał Drogosław Skórzewski, chamberlain of Poznań, starost-tenant of Pyzdry, founder of the Lisewskie Ol. after 1765. J. Łojko, *Civitas Pyzdry. Dzieje miasta do roku 1793*, Pyzdry 2007.

6\. (1-3) Parts of the emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the Zbierskie Ol. of 11 XI 1761, issued by Stanisław Dzik Kożuchowski, land judge of Wieluń, owner of the Zbiersk estate. APP Kal. Gr. 448, fols. 254-255v (obl. 25 VII 1783).

7\. (1-2) Parts of the foundation charter for Wielołęka as an Olęder settlement, issued on 6 XI 1770 by Jan Wężyk, castellan of Konary, of Sieradz, in Polish, an authenticated extract from the records of the Civil Tribunal of the Government of Kalisz of 19 IX/1 X 1841, a typescript copy. APP, SAB. 789.

8\. Part of the foundation charter in German of Józef Wężyk, castellan of Konary, of Sieradz, for Konary/the Konarskie Ol. of 6 IX 1770. APP, SAB 696.

9\. Part of the joint second emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the Konarskie Ol. and the Borowieckie St. and Nw. Ol. in the district of Konin, founded in the years 1770-1772, issued on 25 VIII 1785 by Count Franciszek Stadnicki, starost of Ostrzeszów, owner of the estates of Grójec (Grodziec), Królików and Biskupice. APP. Kalisz Gr. 476 (1789), fols. 421-422v.

10\. (1-2) Parts of the emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the community of the Białobłockie Olędry "now already settled", of 17 X 1787 and 1792, of Count Franciszek Stadnicki, SAB 626 (no. 8, extract of the *oblata* of 29 VI 1788 from the municipal court book of Kalisz). APP, SAB 626.

11\. Part of the joint second emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the Grądy St. and Nw., founded before and in 1772 and in 1791, in the district of Konin, issued on 6 II 1791 by Count Franciszek Stadnicki, starost of Ostrzeszów, owner of the estates of Grójec (Grodziec), Królików and Biskupice. APP, Kalisz. Gr. 482, fols. 77-77v and 89-89v, obl. 4 III 1791.

12\. (1-2) Parts of the emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the Cekowskie Nw. Ol., issued on 28 X 1780 by Krzysztof Celiński, APP, Kal. Gr. 450, fols. 27-27v and 29-29v; obl. 27 III 1783 by the honest Bogusław Szwander, Olęder of the Ceków estate.

13\. (1-3) Parts of the emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the Lubieńskie Ol., issued on 10 XI 1777 by Anna Chlebowska, superior of the convent of the Order of St Clare (the Franciscan nuns) in Kalisz. APP, Kalisz. Gr. 429 (1778), fols. 153v-154v.

14\. (1-4) Emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the Pyzdrskie Ol., issued on 24 IV 1778 by the magistrate of the town of Pyzdry. APP, Pyzdry Gr. 82 (1780), fols. 36-39, the same in: Konin Gr. 118 (1780), fols. 158-159v (obl. 16 X 1780 by the honest Jakub Bończyk, sołtys, and Józef Baranowski, assessor.

15\. The Rev. Kazimierz Lipski, mitred provost of Chocz, founder of the Kazimierskie Stare Ol. (1782) and the Kazimierskie Nowe Ol. (1790) in the district of Kalisz. Chocz. Portrait of about 1790 in the former provost's palace. Katalog Zabytków Sztuki w Polsce. Vol. V, no. 19, p. 5, fig. 4.

16\. Part of the emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the Dzierzbińskie-Zamęty and Zdanowiec Ol., issued on 25 VI 1783 by Kazimierz Poraj Biernacki, *wojski* of the district of Ostrzeszów, owner of the estates of Siąszyce and Dzierzbin and of the property of Sul[i]szew. APP, Kalisz. Gr. 448, fols. 216v-218.

[p. 153]

17\. (1-2) Parts of the emphyteutic foundation charter for the Plewińskie (Stare) and the Nowe, otherwise Kuźnickie, Olędry, issued on 1 IX 1793 by Antoni Kawiecki, owner of the estates of Kuszyn and Plewnie. APP, Kal. Gr. 488, fols. 26v-28.

18\. Wawrzyniec Antoni Łodzia Czarnecki, *stolnik* of Inowłódz, deputy castle starost of Łęczyca (d. 1783), founder, together with his wife Ewa née Nowowiejska, of the villages of Dębniałki Ol. and Piskory Ol. (1783), in the district of Kalisz. Katalog Zabytków Sztuki w Polsce, Vol. V, no. 6, p. 6, fig. 106.

19\. (1-2) Part of the joint emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for two settlements, Dębniałki/the Bolimowskie or Brudzewskie and the Piskory or Lipeckie Olędry, issued on 17 VII 1783 by Wawrzyniec Antoni Łodzia, *stolnik* of the district of Inowłódz, and Ewa, the married couple Czarnecki, owners of estates in the district of Brzeziny in the voivodeship of Łęczyca and in the district and voivodeship of Kalisz. APP, Kalisz. Gr. 482, fols. 1-2v; obl. 7 I 1791 (Ryc. 30 Dębniałki i Piskory Ol.jpg).

20\. Information on the Siąszyckie Ol., founded about 1786, in the *manifestacja* (protest) of Leon Bi(e)lski, plenipotentiary of Kazimierz Biernacki, *stolnik* of Piotrków, owner of the estate of Siąszyce, against Onufry Gałczyński, a *towarzysz* of the National Cavalry, of 1790. APP, Konin. Gr. 122 (1790), fols. 275v-276.

21\. (1-2) Parts of the first emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charter for the Mycielińskie St. and Nw. – Kazala St. and Nw. Ol., issued by Maciej Łuczycki, owner of the estate of Mycielin, on 30 III 1787. APP, Kalisz. Gr. 472 (1788), fols. 50-50v, 53; obl. 22 I 1788.

22\. (1-2) One of the two first emphyteutic-perpetual foundation charters for the Stropieszyńskie-Radzany and Gostynie Ol., issued on 6 1787 by Ignacy Skarbek Kozietulski, treasurer of the land of Czersk, owner of the village and demesne farm of Stropieszyn. APP, Kalisz Gr. 466 (1787), fols. 335-336v; obl. 7 XII 1787 by the honest Michał Gultz, Olęder.

23\. Part of the revision of the Ciążeń key of the mensal estates of the bishops of Poznań of 1793, with information on Bishop Antoni O. Okęcki's foundation charter for the Ciążeńskie or Zarzeczne Ol. of 1789 and with a list of their settlers by name. AAP. CP 152 fols. 23-24v.

24\. Antoni Onufry Okęcki (1729-1793), bishop of Poznań (1780-1793), founder of the Ciążeńskie Ol. in 1789.

25\. Part of the village book of the Dzierzbińskie Olędry in Polish and German of the years 1792-1913, with a register, in Polish, of the Olęders who paid their rent, of 14 XI 1792, APP, SAB 803.

The other illustrations (scans) of archival documents on the history of Olęder settlement in the Pyzdry Forest, which for lack of space could not be included in this publication, can be found on the website www.puszczapyzdrska.com, under the tab: archival documents.

[p. 155]

Zbigniew Chodyła – Doctor of Humanities in history, author of

150 scholarly publications (7 of them in French, German and English, in foreign and Polish journals and publications), among them 2 books, treatises, articles, reviews, and subject and biographical entries (among others in the *Encyklopedia Historii Gospodarczej Polski do 1945 r.* [Encyclopaedia of the economic history of Poland to 1945], the encyclopaedia *Britannica. Edycja Polska*, and the *Polski Słownik Biograficzny*); editor of source publications, editor and co-editor of 3 books chiefly in the field of the social and economic history of Poland in the sixteenth to eighteenth centuries, on, among other things, the history of rural settlement (especially Olęder settlement), towns, prices and price lists, various questions of the history of Greater Poland, and the life and work of a dozen or so historical figures.
